# Supplement to Theory V7.2: full statements, proofs and numerics

*Companion S to THEORY.md. Chapter numbers match the core's sections: S2 holds the proofs for Section 2, and so on; S13 gives the interfaces between models, S14 the counterexamples that bound the claims, S16 the joint correction target and reduced certificate, and S15 the map from results to the blocks of `checks_v72.py`. Each chapter has, where needed, the full statements of results stated briefly in the core, the results moved here from the core (each keeps its key and takes an S-number), proofs, numerics with the block that reproduces them, and bounds. Results that appear only here are numbered with an S prefix. Based on V6.2 drafted by Claude, revised as V7 by Codex at Ren's direction after the V6.2 reviews, as V7.1 after the V7 readiness review, and as V7.2 after the discussion of correction through institutional change. Those reviews and revisions were performed by models; V7.2 has not received human review.*

## S0. Conventions

**Maintained assumptions.** Each is listed with what changes if it is dropped.
- (A1) Exposure to the host is shared, so $G(V)$ is common to all payoffs. Remark 3.8 treats unequal exposure.
- (A2) Field size is fixed. With entry driven by absolute profit, the host settles where rents dissipate, and (reach) must hold at that equilibrium.
- (A3) Conversion is monotone ($b_{ij}\ge0$), so countervailing pressure enters only through correction $d$ (Remark 4.7). A countervailing channel is not a negative term of $\Gamma$, and one that answers with delay can oscillate with growing amplitude while the net gain is below one (Remark S2.3).
- (A4) The cost of conversion capacity is that of Assumption 4.2, with $[H_\gamma]$ and $[H_f]$ stated as hypotheses wherever used.
- (A5) Reports are averaged and non-strategic. Remark 4.9 treats other alarm rules.
- (A6) In the field model, one practice and its alternative per domain, with payoffs linear in the stated terms; parameters uncalibrated. Contest prizes and stock dynamics have the forms stated in their own results. No loop gain is estimated for any channel; Remark S2.5 shows at illustrative values what a gain above one asks of each parameter.
- (A7) Contest models are symmetric unless stated.
- (A8) In the bargaining model of Proposition 5.9, after a shift in power the side that gained the advantage sets the terms each period, and the other accepts or fights.
- (A9) Unless a statement says that rivals respond, the others' conduct is held fixed, and each statement says so. With responsive rivals, the equilibrium win-share elasticity determines local stability; in the deterministic share model the private elasticity enters the local price calculation, together with any additional payoff effects (Proposition 2.2(4)). A lottery's finite-jump continuation value is a separate object, computed in Remark S2.2, and is not obtained by that substitution. The computed price corrections can be severalfold; the reported catch-up reversal concerns win-share elasticities, not the total continuation price [num: MP; held for outside review].

**Symbols.** The table at the end of the core lists the symbols that keep one meaning in all three documents. The following are shared between chapters, each with the meaning given here wherever it appears outside the reuses listed below; any other symbol is local to the model that declares it.

| Symbol | Meaning |
|---|---|
| $G(V)$; $g_V$ | benefit that every payoff draws from the host, common to all (A1); its slope in the commons of Proposition 3.7 |

| $r^{\rm eq}_j$, $r^{\rm pr}_j$; $\xi_{r,j}$ | decisiveness read off the equilibrium win share along a relative shift of stock $j$ (the actor's up, each other carrier's down by $1/(n-1)$ as much), $nX^{j\ast}dp_i/dy^j$, with every intensity responding or with the actor's own held and its rivals' at their Markov policies; elasticity of decisiveness in the level of stock $j$ (0 in a ratio channel, 1 in a linear level channel) |
| $\mathcal J$, $\mathcal D$; $\mathbf u$, $\mathbf v$, $\mathbf z$ | Jacobian of the linearized loop and its diagonal part; conversion and decisiveness vectors, and the Perron vector |
| $\mathcal S$, $\mathcal F$, $\mathcal G$ | the scoring rule, the update of the state under selection (also the selection rule of Definition 3.2), and the update of the rule (Definition 2.4) |

**Reuses.** A letter of either table is sometimes used for another quantity inside one statement or proof, where it is defined. The cases are these.
- *Core.* Proposition 2.3 writes $S^\ast$ for total capacity at equal standing, and its Scope $\Omega=gS$, with $S$ total capacity and $g$ the prize per unit of it; Proposition 3.5(5) writes $g$ for a private gain and $H$ for its cost to the host; Lemma 3.3(3) writes $x$ for the evading share; the Scope of Proposition 4.12 writes $S(x_p)$ for the separatrix's height; Remark 5.5 writes $T$ for a span of periods as well as for the payoff of racing against a restrainer; Proposition 5.12 writes $u$ for the correction applied to a hazard mode $y$ and $w$ for the weight on model error; (15) writes $b$ for the rank prize per unit of racing; Proposition 6.4 writes $p$ for the probability that losing means subordination; Proposition 7.3 writes $E$, $W$ and $X$ for events and $\pi$ for $\Pr(E)$; Lemma 8.2 writes $A$ and $C$ for an arrangement and continuation.
- *S2.* The proof of Proposition 2.2(3) writes $\eta$ for a small radius, $f$ for the map of the deviation, $w(y)$ for the actor's extra winnings and $T$ for a number of periods; Remark S2.6 writes $u$ and $w$ for the deviations of success and of control over the rules, and $a$, $b$ for the cross-gains; the one-stock computations of S2 and S3 write $\kappa$ for $\kappa_c$ and, with a prize that grows with capacity, $G$ for the growth factor of total capacity and $m$ for the multiplier of a share deviation.
- *S3.* The proof of Proposition 3.9 writes $w_i$ for future winnings and $x_j$ for reinvestment; the proof of Lemma 3.11 writes $w$ for fitness, $z$ for the trait and $b$ for its benefit.
- *S4.* The proof of Proposition 4.4 writes $u$ for an investment in correction; Proposition S4.1 writes $Y$ for a carrier's total share across domains.
- *S5.* Remark 5.3 in full writes $F$ for the remaining frontier; Proposition 5.9 in full writes $x$ for a share of the flow; the proof of Proposition 5.8 writes $s$ for the share a winner gives away; Proposition 5.14 in full writes $\tau$ for the date at which hazardous variety is frozen and, in a counterexample, $m$ for an integer; the proof of Proposition 5.15 writes $u$ for the rate at which coverage grows.
- *S6.* The proof of Proposition 6.1 writes $T$ and $H$ for the scaled rank prize and harm; Proposition S6.2 and its proof write $x$ for added hazard.
- *S8.* The reading of Jones (2024) writes $\rho$ for the discount rate, $\gamma$ for the curvature of utility, $u$ and $v$ for utility and the value of a year of life, $g$ for growth and $h$ for the hazard.

**Renamed in V6.2.** To give each shared symbol one meaning, the following were renamed.
- *S2.* The Jacobian $J\to\mathcal J$ and its diagonal $D\to\mathcal D$, with the vectors written $\mathbf u$, $\mathbf v$; an eigenvalue $\lambda\to\Lambda$, the Perron root $\Lambda^\ast$; the Perron vector $w\to\mathbf z$; the deviations of Remark S2.6 $\to u$, $w$.
- *Definitions 2.4 and 3.2.* $\sigma$, $F$, $G\to\mathcal S$, $\mathcal F$, $\mathcal G$.
- *S3.* The costs of a constraint $\kappa_k\to c_k$; the expected sanction $s_k\to\bar F_k$, $\bar F(x)$; the host's payoff coefficient $\varpi\to g_V$, so that $\varpi$ is trust only; the number of non-contestants $\to m_o$.
- *S4.* The cost of capacity $\to\mathrm{Cost}(q;p)$, and the "fixed cost" $f$ is now the per-unit cost floor, a marginal cost that every unit pays; locals $k\to\kappa_q$ (adjustment rate), $\rho\to s_0$, $c\to c_1$, $V\to V_q$ (selection variance), $g\to g_q$, $F\to F_q$ (lump sum), $D\to D_h$ (delegated share), $v_d\to k_V$, and the number of domains $D\to N_D$.
- *S5.* In Proposition 5.4(5), $q\to q_b$ and the value of subordination $\to V^{\rm sub}_i$; locals $w_p$, $\tau_{\rm det}$.
- *S6.* In (15), the harm $d\to h_d$ and its exponent $\beta_d\to\beta_h$; in Proposition 6.9, the one-layer reliability $f\to p_\ell$, the checks per layer $m\to k_c$, $q_{\rm cc}\to p_{\rm cc}$, the layer ratio $m_\ell\to\mathsf m$, and attention $Y\to\mathsf A$, $\mathsf B$; in Proposition S6.4, $q_0$, $q_c\to h_0$, $h_{c,t}$; in Proposition S6.2, $q\to1-\psi_0$; the detection probability $\pi\to p_{\rm det}$.
- *S8.* $U_t\to\mathsf u_t$ in the proof of Lemma 8.2; $X$, $X_{\rm stay}\to V^{\rm exit}$, $V^{\rm stay}$.
- *S10.* The cooperators' number $m\to n_c$; the winner's keep $\to A_w$; in Proposition S10.3, $U^{\rm race}$ and $\mathsf v_i$, clear of the host $V$.
- *S11.* In Proposition 11.1, $k_{\mathsf t}$, $G_{\mathsf t}\to c_{\mathsf t}$, $\Pi_{\mathsf t}$.

Two families are still separated by subscript only: $\kappa$ alone is the unpriced coupling, while $\kappa_s$ is the positional weight, $\kappa_c$ and $\kappa_j$ are convertible shares and $\kappa_W$ a winner's coupling; $\delta$ alone is the discount factor, while $\delta_f$ (diffusion) and $\delta_h$ (erosion of human capacity) are rates.

**Numbering and keys.** The core numbers results by section with one counter per section, shared by all kinds, tables included (Table 7.1 is the first result of Section 7). The Supplement uses the same numbers for full statements and adds S-numbered results, among them results moved from the core; the Discussion has no numbered results. Equations are numbered (1), (2), … in the core and (S1), (S2), … here. Every result has a stable key that never changes (for example `enforce`, `loopgain`); CLAIMS, LEDGER and `checks_v72.py` are indexed by key, and `src/keymap.json` maps keys to displayed numbers.

**Claim tags.** [def] definition; [val] value, written [val: V1–V3] where it names the values it rests on; [thm] proved in its model; [thm∣prem] proved given premises the statement names; [prem] premise; [obs] observation, with its evidence row ([obs: EV5]); [num: X] a number reproduced by block X of `checks_v72.py`; [identity: X] an algebraic identity or threshold formula that block X evaluates against its own statement, which guards against slips and is not independent evidence; [num: legacy …] a number carried from an earlier version and reproduced by its legacy block (`checks_v72.py --legacy`); [conj] conjecture; [rec] recommendation. "Held for outside review" marks a computation not yet checked outside the drafting family. A table of results tagged [def, with support] organizes results proved elsewhere, each row carrying the tag of the result it cites; in Table 7.1 a dagger marks a sign that crosses models through an interface of S13 that is not proved. [corpus: key] marks the source in the author's corpus, the folder that the key names (X1 gives the sources); a quoted line is his own words unless it is introduced as the corpus's, which marks a line whose authorship the corpus does not settle; the English renderings of his Chinese, and the paraphrases a drafting model made of his spoken words, are the drafters' until he confirms them (CLAIMS).

**Premises.** Premises are named in words and stated where they are used; the list below gives each in brief.

| Premise | In brief | Used in |
|---|---|---|
| (reach) | the race load can carry the host below its floor, $h_V\bar e>\rho(1-V_{\min})$ | Proposition 3.4; Proposition 4.13; R1 and R2 of Table 9.1 |
| (decisive), (fast), (unsaturated) | decisiveness is large; leads open within short windows; no level of advantage makes more worthless | Section 5.1; Proposition 7.2 |
| (hazard–damage link) | the hazard of the irreversible event in a round is proportional to the shared damage | Proposition 5.10 |
| (coverage lags) | contestable coverage falls ever further behind hazardous variety, $U_t/Y_t\to0$ over surviving histories | Theorem 5.13 |

| (a), (d), (e); (b), (c), (clock) | selection among carriers differing in load, with a race carrier; a fixed averaged alarm that captured evaluators silence and independent ones would not; capture before evidence; or, in place of (e), appropriable control under $[H_\gamma]$, correction growing below the rate law, and capture completed before the host reaches the alarm band | Proposition 4.13 |
| $[H_\gamma]$, $[H_f]$ | AI lowers the variable cost of control; AI lowers the per-unit cost floor too | Assumption 4.2 |

Former letter codes are kept as aliases for readers of earlier versions: (reach) was H0; (decisive), (fast), (unsaturated) were P1–P3, a label now used for the entries of the parameter matrix of S7 (P1–P23); (variety grows), (coverage lags), (residual floor), (shared failure) were (X), (U), (φ), (φ-s); legible, overrulable, exercised, resourced were U1–U4; (hazard–damage link) was $(\mathrm{A_G})$; the conditions (a)–(e) of Proposition 4.13 were H1–H5, and (clock) is new in V6.2, as are (safe fallback), (bounded own risk) and (advantage keeps pace) as names.

## S2. The conditions of winning

### Proof of Proposition 2.2

*Steady state.* At equal standing every $p_i=1/n$, so each stock satisfies $X^{j\ast}=(1-d_j)X^{j\ast}+\kappa_j\Omega/n+k_{0j}$, that is, $X^{j\ast}=(\kappa_j\Omega/n+k_{0j})/d_j$.

*Linearization.* Write $x_i^j=X_i^j-X^{j\ast}$ and split it into its mean over carriers, $\bar x^j$, and the deviation $y_i^j=x_i^j-\bar x^j$, with $\sum_iy_i^j=0$. At the symmetric point $\partial p_i/\partial\sigma_i=(n-1)/n^2$ and $\partial p_i/\partial\sigma_k=-1/n^2$, so $dp_i=\frac1n\big(d\sigma_i-\frac1n\sum_kd\sigma_k\big)=\frac1n\sum_jv_jy_i^j$, where $v_j=\partial\sigma/\partial X^j\ge0$ at $X^\ast$; the common parts cancel. Hence
$$\bar x^{j\prime}=(1-d_j)\bar x^j,\qquad y_i^{\prime}=\mathcal Jy_i,\qquad \mathcal J=\mathcal D+\tfrac1n\mathbf u\mathbf v^\top,$$
with $\mathcal D=\operatorname{diag}(1-d_j-\delta_{f,j})$, $\mathbf u=(u_j)$, $u_j=(1-\beta)\Omega\kappa_j$, and $\mathbf v=(v_j)$. The mean deviations decay since $0<d_j\le1$. Every carrier's deviation vector evolves by the same matrix $\mathcal J$, a rank-one perturbation of a diagonal matrix: every stock is fed by the one win share, which is why the channels add.

*Spectral radius.* $\mathcal J\ge0$ because $d_j+\delta_{f,j}\le1$ and $\mathbf u,\mathbf v\ge0$. If $u_jv_j=0$ for all $j$, the spectral radius of $\mathcal J$ is $\max_j(1-d_j-\delta_{f,j})<1$, and $\Gamma=0$. Otherwise an eigenvalue $\Lambda$ of $\mathcal J$ that is not a diagonal entry of $\mathcal D$ satisfies the secular equation
$$1=\frac1n\sum_j\frac{u_jv_j}{\Lambda-(1-d_j-\delta_{f,j})}. \tag{S1}$$
For real $\Lambda$ above every diagonal entry of the channels with $u_jv_j>0$, the right side decreases strictly from $+\infty$ to $0$, so there is exactly one such root $\Lambda^\ast$; by Perron–Frobenius the spectral radius of the nonnegative matrix $\mathcal J$ is the larger of $\Lambda^\ast$ and the diagonal entries of the channels with $u_jv_j=0$, which are below one. So the spectral radius is below one iff $\Lambda^\ast<1$, that is, iff the right side at $\Lambda=1$ is below one: $\frac1n\sum_ju_jv_j/(d_j+\delta_{f,j})<1$.

*Simplification.* Since $r_j=v_jX^{j\ast}$, $\frac1nu_jv_j=(1-\beta)\frac{\kappa_j\Omega}{n}\frac{r_jd_j}{\kappa_j\Omega/n+k_{0j}}=(1-\beta)r_j\omega_jd_j$. So the condition is $\Gamma<1$, which proves (1); for $\Gamma>1$, the deviations $y_1=\mathbf z$, $y_2=-\mathbf z$ along the Perron vector $\mathbf z$ grow by $\Lambda^\ast>1$ a period. Part (2) is read off $\Gamma$.

*The price of standing.* Let an actor's relative net payoff today be $y$, reinvested so that its stock deviations rise by $\kappa_jy$. From the next period its relative deviation evolves by $\mathcal J$, and its winnings exceed the mean by $(1-\beta)\Omega\,dp=\frac{(1-\beta)\Omega}{n}\mathbf v^\top x_t$ per period. The present value is $\frac\delta n\mathbf v^\top\sum_{t\ge0}(\delta\mathcal J)^t\mathbf u\,y$. The series converges iff the spectral radius of $\delta\mathcal J$ is below one, which by the secular argument applied to $\delta\mathcal J$ holds iff $\frac\delta n\sum_ju_jv_j/(1-\delta(1-d_j-\delta_{f,j}))<1$, that is, iff $\Gamma_\delta<1$. There, with $A=I-\delta\mathcal D$, Sherman–Morrison gives $(A-\frac\delta n\mathbf u\mathbf v^\top)^{-1}\mathbf u=A^{-1}\mathbf u/(1-\frac\delta n\mathbf v^\top A^{-1}\mathbf u)$, so the present value is $\frac{\Gamma_\delta}{1-\Gamma_\delta}y$ with $\Gamma_\delta=\frac\delta n\sum_ju_jv_j/(1-\delta(1-d_j-\delta_{f,j}))$, which is the stated form; it grows without bound as $\Gamma_\delta\to1^-$. Finally $\delta(d_j+\delta_{f,j})<1-\delta(1-d_j-\delta_{f,j})$ for $\delta<1$, so $\Gamma_\delta\le\Gamma$, strictly if $\Gamma>0$, and $\Gamma_\delta\to\Gamma$ as $\delta\to1$.

*Beyond the domain.* The Perron root of $\delta\mathcal J$ is $\delta\Lambda^\ast$, so $\delta\Lambda^\ast<1$ iff $\Gamma_\delta<1$. Take one channel (with several, the same argument runs along the Perron direction). The map (1) is increasing in a carrier's own stock, so a positive relative perturbation $\varepsilon$ stays positive; write it $y_{t+1}=f(y_t)$ with $f(0)=0$, $f'(0)=\Lambda^\ast$, write the actor's extra winnings $w(y)$ with $w'(0)>0$, and $PV(\varepsilon)=\sum_{t\ge1}\delta^tw(y_t)$. Fix $\eta>0$ small enough for the estimates below on $|y|\le\eta$.
- *If $\delta\Lambda^\ast<1$:* fix $\Lambda'$ with $\Lambda^\ast<\Lambda'<1/\delta$. While $|y_t|\le\eta$, $|y_t|\le C\Lambda'^t\varepsilon$ and the error of linearization is $O(y_t^2)$; after $T=\ln(\eta/\varepsilon)/\ln\Lambda'$ periods the tail is at most $\delta^T\sup|w|/(1-\delta)=O(\varepsilon^{\ln(1/\delta)/\ln\Lambda'})=o(\varepsilon)$, since the exponent exceeds one. So $PV(\varepsilon)/\varepsilon\to\Gamma_\delta/(1-\Gamma_\delta)$: the first-order price holds on the whole domain $\Gamma_\delta<1$, including $\Gamma>1>\Gamma_\delta$, where equal standing is unstable and discounting still wins; there the convergence is slow, of order $\varepsilon^{-\ln(\delta\Lambda^\ast)/\ln\Lambda^\ast}$.
- *If $\delta\Lambda^\ast>1$:* choose $\Lambda''<\Lambda^\ast$ with $\delta\Lambda''>1$. For $t\le T=\ln(\eta/\varepsilon)/\ln\Lambda''$, $y_t\ge c\Lambda''^t\varepsilon$, so $PV(\varepsilon)\ge c'\sum_{t\le T}(\delta\Lambda'')^t\varepsilon\ge c''\eta\,\delta^T=c'''\varepsilon^{\ln(1/\delta)/\ln\Lambda''}$, an exponent below one, and $PV(\varepsilon)/\varepsilon\to\infty$. Conversely, with $\Lambda'>\Lambda^\ast$ and $T'=\ln(\eta/\varepsilon)/\ln\Lambda'$, the sum up to $T'$ is at most of order $\varepsilon(\delta\Lambda')^{T'}=\eta\,\delta^{T'}$ and the tail at most $\delta^{T'}\sup|w|/(1-\delta)$, both $O(\varepsilon^{\ln(1/\delta)/\ln\Lambda'})$, so $PV(\varepsilon)\to0$: the value of standing is continuous at equal standing.
- *If $\delta\Lambda^\ast=1$:* the same bound gives $PV(\varepsilon)/\varepsilon\ge c\ln(1/\varepsilon)$.

The value of standing is therefore continuous at equal standing with infinite slope when $\Gamma_\delta\ge1$. The printed multiplier $\Gamma_\delta/(1-\Gamma_\delta)$, negative there, means nothing, and no finite horizon recovers a first-order price, since the price over a horizon $T$ grows without bound in $T$.

*Proof of (4).*
- *Rank one survives.* In a symmetric Markov equilibrium the equilibrium win share is $p^{\rm eq}_i(X)=P(X_i;\{X_k\}_{k\ne i})$, symmetric in the others' stocks. Linearize at equal standing: $dp_i=\sum_ja_j\,dX_i^j+\sum_jb_j\sum_{k\ne i}dX_k^j$. Since $\sum_ip_i\equiv1$, perturbing one carrier's stock $j$ alone gives $a_j+(n-1)b_j=0$. Hence $dp_i=\sum_ja_j\frac n{n-1}\big(dX_i^j-\overline{dX^j}\big)$: the equilibrium share responds to relative deviations only.
- *Same dynamics.* The deviation dynamics are therefore exactly $\mathcal D+\frac1n\mathbf u\mathbf v^{\rm eq\top}$, with $v^{\rm eq}_j=a_jn^2/(n-1)$ and $r^{\rm eq}_j=v^{\rm eq}_jX^{j\ast}$, which is the definition in the statement, scaled as $r_j=v_jX^{j\ast}$ is. Mean deviations still decay at $1-d_j$, because $\sum p\equiv1$. By premise $\mathbf v^{\rm eq}\ge0$, so the Perron argument of (1) applies unchanged, and (2) is read off. If some $v^{\rm eq}_j<0$, a catch-up stronger than the structural advantage, $\mathcal J$ is no longer nonnegative and $\Gamma^{\rm eq}$ alone does not decide.
- *Price in the deterministic share model.* For a differentiable continuation value, apply the envelope theorem at equal standing; if differentiability has not been established, the calculation identifies a candidate derivative only. Its own intensity is optimal, so the intensity's response drops out at first order in every period, and the derivative of the value follows the state with the actor's own intensity held as a number and the rivals' at their Markov policies. The deviation dynamics move the state along relative shifts (own stocks up by $\varepsilon$, each other carrier's down by $\varepsilon/(n-1)$), which leave totals unchanged; since $\sum p\equiv1$ whatever the actor does, the state stays on relative shifts, and along one $dp_i=(r^{\rm pr}_j/(nX^{j\ast}))\,y^j$ by the definition of $r^{\rm pr}_j$, which is taken along this shift. A move of the actor's stock alone would not serve: $\sum p\equiv1$ keeps a relative shift relative, but with the actor's own intensity held it does not make a common shift of all stocks leave $p_i$ unchanged, so the own-only derivative differs from the relative one wherever the rivals' policies depend on the level of the stocks and not only on their distribution. In a static contest $p_i=X_ie_i/\sum_kX_ke_k$ with effort cost $e/X^s$, rivals at their equilibrium efforts at the shifted state, the two agree at $s=0$ and at $n=2$, $s=1$ give $r^{\rm pr}/r=1.00$ for the own-only move and $1.50$ for the relative shift, a common shift moving $p_i$ by $-0.25$ per unit [num: VF]. For $r^{\rm eq}$ the identity $a_j+(n-1)b_j=0$ makes them agree. With non-negative private derivatives, the discounted linearized transition is stable on $\Gamma^{\rm pr}_\delta<1$, by the same spectral argument as in (3). The derivative of the differentiable continuation value is then the Sherman–Morrison expression of (3) with $\mathbf v^{\rm pr}$ in place of $\mathbf v$, plus, per period, $-\ell\sum_{k\ne i}de_k/dy$ along the same relative shift when intensity $e$ imposes a shared loss $\ell$ per unit: a lead that makes rivals spend less is worth $\ell$ per unit of their reduction. $\square$

*Lineage.* The stability calculation is standard (X1 gives the lineage); what is new is the organization, with provision and diffusion inside the gain, and its consequence for objectives (Section 3.5).

*Numerics.* Block LG: across 4,000 random two-channel systems the spectral radius of the numerical Jacobian agrees with the criterion in every case (four within $10^{-3}$ of the boundary set aside); nonlinear simulations from a $10^{-4}$ perturbation grow or decay as $\Gamma$ is above or below one, including a case with $r=0.8$ in which a reflexive channel lifts $\Gamma$ to 1.1; the present value of a perturbation in the nonlinear model matches $\Gamma_\delta/(1-\Gamma_\delta)$ to a relative error below $2\times10^{-6}$, and converges to it in twenty systems with $\Gamma\ge1>\Gamma_\delta$. Block CH: two reviewers' independent scripts, with one to three stocks entering strength by logarithm, linear and square-root forms, find no mismatch in 2,996 and 1,489 random systems, and the price on three mixed stocks matches to $7\times10^{-7}$. Block PD: in an outside reviewer's boundary case ($n=2$, $\kappa\Omega=1$, $r=2$, $d=0.2$, $\delta=0.9$, so $\Gamma=2$, $\Gamma_\delta=9/7$ and $\Lambda^\ast=1.2$) the present value is positive at every $\varepsilon$, while the printed multiplier is $-4.5$; the fitted exponent of $PV(\varepsilon)$ is 0.5628, 0.5758, 0.5776, 0.5778 for $\varepsilon=10^{-2},\dots,10^{-8}$, against the predicted $\ln(1/0.9)/\ln1.2=0.5779$. Inside the domain but above $\Gamma=1$ ($\delta=0.8$, $\Gamma_\delta=0.889$), $PV/\varepsilon$ rises from 7.163 to 7.987 as $\varepsilon$ falls from $10^{-4}$ to $10^{-12}$, converging to the formula's 8.000 at the predicted rate $\varepsilon^{0.224}$; at $\delta\Lambda^\ast=1$ it grows by a constant 8.42 every two decades, a logarithm. Block MP: Remark S2.2.

*Bounds.*
- $\Gamma$ is evaluated at equal standing. Off parity the reflexive channel's gain is higher: the return to influence over correctors grows with the square of share (Proposition 4.3), and the conversion that selection cultivates is proportional to $\sum_kp_k^{1+(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$ (Proposition 4.5(4)). With $\eta=1$, $\xi_v=0$ and four carriers, a leader share of 0.6 gives $\sum_kp_k^3=0.223$ against $1/16$ at parity, about 3.6 times [num: arithmetic of Proposition 4.5(4)].
- The certificate of Proposition 4.10(2), a row sum of conversion gains below one, has the same form, but is evaluated at a captured state, not at equal standing; the two are of one form, not one theorem.

**Remark S2.1 (Time to decisiveness) [thm; num].** In the linearized single-channel loop a relative gap grows by the factor
$$\Lambda^\ast=1+(d_K+\delta_f)(\Gamma-1)$$
a period; with several channels, by the Perron root $\Lambda^\ast$ of (S1), which is not a function of $\Gamma$ alone. **$\Gamma$ decides the sign of a gap's growth; the turnover of the stocks, through $\Lambda^\ast$, its speed.** A gap grows from $\varepsilon_0$ to a decisive level $\bar\Delta$ in about $T_{\rm dec}=\ln(\bar\Delta/\varepsilon_0)/\ln\Lambda^\ast$ periods.
- From 1% to 50% at $\Gamma=3$, $d_K=0.2$, $\delta_f=0$: 11.6 periods in the linearization, 13 in the nonlinear model. At $\Gamma=1.2$, a channel turning over at $d_K+\delta_f=0.5$ needs 41 periods and one turning over at 0.02 needs 980. With two channels at $\Gamma=1.2$, turnovers $(0.3,0.02)$ give $\Lambda^\ast=1.0088$ and $(0.1,0.1)$ give $1.0200$; $(0.5,0.02)$ gives $1.0092$ and 427 periods [num: TD].
- With diffusion the nonlinear gap can settle short of decisive: with two carriers at $\Gamma=1.5$, $d_K=\delta_f=0.05$, $r=3$, a 1% gap measured as the leader's excess over the mean, here $(X_1-X_2)/(X_1+X_2)$, settles at 0.447 and has not reached 50% after 10,000 periods, where the linearization predicts 80 [num: TD]. The measure and the number of carriers matter: measured as the leader's excess over the other carrier, $X_1/X_2-1$, the measure of Remark S5.2, the same two carriers go from 1% to 50% in 80 periods and settle at 1.618; and with four carriers, the leader 1% above the others, its excess over the mean reaches 50% at period 77 [num: VF].

This is the channel through which a larger loop gain raises the breakout probability $p_d$ of Section 5.2. Where punishment is triggered by detection and acts before decisiveness, $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$: the probability that a breakout is not detected and answered within the time $T_{\rm dec}$ it needs to become decisive (Remark 5.5). In the interfaces of S13 it is $\Lambda^\ast$, not $\Gamma$, that sets $T_{\rm dec}$. *Proof.* With one channel, $y'=(1-d-\delta_f)y+(1-\beta)\kappa\Omega\frac{r}{nX^\ast}y$, and $(1-\beta)\kappa\Omega r/(nX^\ast)=(1-\beta)r\omega d=(d+\delta_f)\Gamma$. V6.1's factor $1+d_K(\Gamma-1)$ omitted $\delta_f$: at $d_K=0.2$, $\delta_f=0.1$, $\Gamma=4/3$ it gives 1.067 where the exact factor is 1.100 [num: TD]. $\square$

**Remark S2.2 (Structural and equilibrium gain) [thm; num].** Proposition 2.2(4) keeps the form of the loop gain when rivals respond; this remark gives its numbers. The contest is two-actor: each round is a Tullock lottery with chosen effort, and the winner converts its prize into capacity by (1); equilibria are symmetric Markov-perfect, computed by value iteration with a global best-response gap at parity below $10^{-16}$ [num: MP]. That a lead discourages the follower is the discouragement effect of dynamic contests (Harris and Vickers 1987; Konrad and Kovenock 2009); what is added here is its effect on the loop gain and on the price of standing.
1. *Two elasticities and two valuation problems.* Local stability is decided by $r^{\rm eq}$, which counts every actor's response to a shift of standing; the deterministic local price calculation uses $r^{\rm pr}$, which holds the actor's own effort at its equilibrium level. The lottery's finite-jump continuation premium is obtained from its Bellman equation. The two elasticities differ by the actor's own response, which the envelope theorem removes from the local price derivative and not from the dynamics. The envelope calculation therefore does not use $\Gamma^{\rm eq}_\delta/(1-\Gamma^{\rm eq}_\delta)$ [thm]. Neither elasticity substitution alone proves a formula for the lottery premium, which can remain finite even when that expression is negative or infinite [num: MP]. In the lottery the expected relative deviation at equal standing evolves by $1+d(\Gamma^{\rm eq}-1)$, matched to five digits; with small jumps ($d=0.02$) and no shared loss the computed price equals $\Gamma^{\rm pr}_\delta/(1-\Gamma^{\rm pr}_\delta)$ to four digits in five cases. With others' strategies held fixed instead, the price at the paper's jumps equals $\Gamma_\delta/(1-\Gamma_\delta)$ where strength is linear in the stock ($r=1$), so what follows at $r=1$ comes from the rivals' response, not from the size of the jumps [num: MP]. At other $r$ the lottery's finite-jump value of a win is a different object from the first-order price, sampling winner and loser states at finite separation: the outside review of V6.2 finds 1.031 against the structural 1.082 at $r=1.5$, $\Gamma=0.6$, $d=0.2$, $\delta=0.97$, with opponents fixed (independently reproduced in V7's mathematical regression checks). The magnitudes below are therefore numerical properties of the computed lottery equilibria. The deterministic expression can differ even inside its private finite-gain domain: at $\Gamma=0.45$ the computed price is 0.513 while the formula with measured $r^{\rm pr}$ gives 0.653, its private gain below one (the same review).
2. *A static factor.* In a Tullock contest among $n$ with effort, a carrier's equilibrium payoff $\Omega w_i^2$ responds to its weight $\alpha_i$, with its own effort held and its rivals' responding, as $2\Omega(n-1)^2/(\alpha n^3)$, against $\Omega(n-1)/(n^2\alpha)$ with all efforts held (a common scaling of the weights changes nothing in this contest, so the move of its own weight gives the derivative along a relative shift that defines $r^{\rm pr}$): $r^{\rm pr}/r=2(n-1)/n$, which is 1, 1.333, 1.5, 1.667 and 1.8 for $n=2,3,4,6,10$ [thm; num: MP]. A two-period game whose second-period effort responds to capacity gives the same factor [num: MP]. It is a within-period response of rivals and vanishes at $n=2$; the amplification below, at $n=2$, is dynamic, from the convexity of the value in the state. Neither is "the" factor.
3. *Magnitudes* ($n=2$, $d=0.1$, $\delta=1/1.1$, so that $\Gamma_\delta=\Gamma/2$) [num: MP]:

| Structural $\Gamma$ | $r^{\rm eq}/r$ | $\Gamma^{\rm eq}$ | Computed price ÷ structural price | Comparison with the deterministic expression using measured $r^{\rm pr}$ |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.2 | 1.09 | 0.22 | 1.05 | within 0.2% |
| 0.3 | 1.18 | 0.35 | 1.10 | within 0.5% |
| 0.4 | 1.41 | 0.56 | 1.24 | within 2.3% |
| 0.45 | 2.48 | 1.11 | 1.77 | not an accurate approximation |
| 0.6 | 4.72 | 2.83 | 2.66 | not an accurate approximation |
| 0.8 | 5.09 | 4.08 | 2.79 | not an accurate approximation |

In these computed cases with $\Gamma^{\rm eq}>1$, the first-order expression of Proposition 2.2(4) is a poor approximation to the lottery premium (0.64 against 0.51 at a structural gain of 0.45). That numerical association does not make $\Gamma^{\rm eq}=1$ a domain boundary for a lottery-price theorem. With a shared loss of intensity ($\ell=0.5$, $\Gamma=0.3$) the computed price is 0.2201 and the envelope formula, with the rivals' shared-loss term, gives 0.2221. At larger jumps ($d=0.2$) the computed price at parity is 1.12 times its structural value at $\Gamma=0.3$, 2.4 to 2.6 times at $\Gamma=0.6$, 2.19 times at $\Gamma=0.9$, and 4.75 times at $\Gamma=0.6$ with a shared loss; at a structural $\Gamma=0.6$ the equilibrium gain is 1.96, and the spread of standing after 60 periods is 0.198 against 0.120 under the structural lottery. With more patience ($\delta=0.97$, $r=1.5$, $\Gamma=0.6$, $d=0.2$) the factor reaches 7.87; with larger jumps ($r=1$) $r^{\rm eq}/r$ reaches 5.41 at $\Gamma=0.9$ [num: MP].
4. *Multiplicity* [num: MP; held for outside review]. Where the contest runs in many small rounds, value iteration started from zero and from a step at parity converges to different fixed points. At $\Gamma=0.2$, $d=0.05$, the first gives the smooth equilibrium (price 0.1169, 1.05 times the structural value) and the second a winner-take-all one (price 1.392 on a grid of 3,000 cells and 1.410 on 6,000, about 12.5 times the structural value), both converged to $10^{-11}$; at $\Gamma=0.3$ the two prices are 0.1969 and 1.551. At $d=0.1$ the step start returns to the smooth equilibrium. The same structural gain then admits an equilibrium in which a lead is decisive and one in which it is not, and the belief that a lead is decisive selects the equilibrium in which it is. What needs outside review: that the winner-take-all fixed point is an equilibrium of the continuous-state game and not an artefact of the grid; the size of round and the gain at which it appears; and whether it exists in the deterministic version of (1). If it survives that review, it gives formal content to the sense in which Ψ is partly made true (Section 3.8) [conj].
5. *Catch-up* [num: MP; held for outside review]. With a flow cost of standing below the top strong enough that a laggard tries harder ($L_{\rm sub}=100$), $r^{\rm eq}/r$ falls to 0.993 at $\Gamma=0.3$, so the strategic correction changes sign there, and to 3.42 at $\Gamma=0.6$, where it does not. These are reversals of the win-share elasticity correction; a payoff penalty for falling behind also values standing directly, so a smaller elasticity need not mean a smaller total continuation premium. Where catch-up is so strong that a carrier's equilibrium win share falls with one of its own stocks, the premise of Proposition 2.2(4) fails and $\Gamma^{\rm eq}$ alone does not decide (proof of (4) above). Which contests in a given field are of the discouraging and which of the catching-up kind is an empirical question.
6. *Off parity.* With $r=1$, where the structural price is the same at every state, the laggard's price at a share of 0.25 is 57% of the structural value at $\Gamma=0.3$ and 25% at $\Gamma=0.6$; the leader's at 0.75 is 153% at $\Gamma=0.3$. At the larger jumps the laggard's price at a quarter share is 0.065 against 1.62 at parity, and negative ($-0.039$) with a shared loss [num: MP]. The laggard stops contesting standing: the exclusion of Proposition 2.3(2), seen from the laggard's side.

The form of Proposition 2.2 survives strategic response; its number does not. In the two-actor contests computed where a lead discourages the follower, $r^{\rm eq}/r$ is 1.09 at a structural gain of 0.2 and 1.18 at 0.3, and about 2.5 to 5.4 at structural gains from 0.45 to 0.9, where $\Gamma^{\rm eq}$ exceeds one and equal standing breaks although $\Gamma<1$ [num: MP]. The structural gain is a benchmark from which the equilibrium gain departs upward where leads discourage and downward where catching up is rewarded.

**Remark S2.3 (A countervailing channel with delay) [thm; num].** Let one channel be reinforcing and one countervailing, so that in $\mathcal J=\mathcal D+\frac1n\mathbf u\mathbf v^\top$ the products $g_j=u_jv_j/n$ have opposite signs: a stock that success provokes and that lowers the provoker's strength, such as the mobilization of those harmed against a leader, or correction that arrives late. $\mathcal J$ is then not nonnegative, the criterion of Proposition 2.2(1) does not apply, and a net gain $\sum_jg_j/(d_j+\delta_{f,j})$ below one does not ensure stability. With two channels the characteristic polynomial is $(\Lambda-\mathcal D_1)(\Lambda-\mathcal D_2)-g_1(\Lambda-\mathcal D_2)-g_2(\Lambda-\mathcal D_1)$; when its roots are complex, their modulus is $\sqrt{\det\mathcal J}=\sqrt{\mathcal D_1\mathcal D_2+g_1\mathcal D_2+g_2\mathcal D_1}$. Take $g_1=0.5$ with $\mathcal D_1=0.6$, and $g_2=-0.05$ with $\mathcal D_2=0.95$: the net gain is $1.25-1=0.25$, the roots are $1\pm0.1225i$, of modulus $\sqrt{1.015}=1.0075$, and deviations oscillate with growing amplitude and a period of 51.6 rounds, reaching 292 times a unit start after 600 [num: CH]. A balancing loop that answers late overshoots. Correction therefore cannot be subtracted from $\Gamma$, and the regulatory cycle of Remark 4.7 can diverge as well as lock in. What a balancing channel does to the loop is a question of its delay, not only of its gain.

**Proposition S2.4 (Which levers lower the loop gain) [thm].** *Let stock $j$ act through strength $h_j(X^j)$, so that $r_j=h_j'(X^{j\ast})X^{j\ast}$, and let $\xi_{r,j}=d\ln r_j/d\ln X^j$ at equal standing be the level elasticity of decisiveness. Ratio channels ($h=r\ln X$, as capacity in a Tullock contest) have $\xi_r=0$; linear level channels ($h=aX$, as rule support in the field model of Section 4) have $\xi_r=1$.*
1. *Sharing $\beta$ and diffusion $\delta_{f,j}$ lower $\Gamma$ and $\Gamma_\delta$ in every channel.*
2. *A faster rate of decay or correction $d_j$ changes $\Gamma$ with the sign of $\delta_{f,j}/(d_j+\delta_{f,j})-\xi_{r,j}$, and $\Gamma_\delta$ with the sign of $(1-\delta+\delta\delta_{f,j})/(1-\delta+\delta d_j+\delta\delta_{f,j})-\xi_{r,j}$. Correction therefore lowers both gains wherever $\xi_{r,j}\ge1$, as in level channels. In ratio channels it leaves $\Gamma$ unchanged without diffusion, raises it with diffusion, and always raises $\Gamma_\delta$: a stock that turns over faster is made more of recent winnings.*
3. *Provision not won, $k_{0j}$, lowers the gain through stock $j$ iff $\xi_{r,j}<1$. In a linear level channel it adds the same to everyone and changes nothing; where decisiveness rises faster than in proportion to the stock ($\xi_{r,j}>1$) it raises the gain.*
4. *The convertible share $\kappa_j$ raises the gain through stock $j$ iff $1+(\xi_{r,j}-1)\omega_j>0$: through ratio channels only when $k_{0j}>0$, and through level channels always.*

*Proof.* At equal standing $X^\ast=c/d$ with $c=\kappa\Omega/n+k_0$, and $\omega=(\kappa\Omega/n)/(dX^\ast)$, so each channel's term of (2) is $(1-\beta)r\omega\frac d{d+\delta_f}=(1-\beta)\frac{\kappa\Omega}n\cdot\frac{r(X^\ast)}{X^\ast}\cdot\frac1{d+\delta_f}$, and in $\Gamma_\delta$ the last factor is $\delta/(1-\delta+\delta d+\delta\delta_f)$. (1) $\beta$ enters only through $1-\beta$ (the mean inflow $\kappa\Omega/n$ does not depend on it), and $\delta_f$ only through the last factor. (2) $d\ln(r/X^\ast)/d\ln X^\ast=\xi_r-1$ and $d\ln X^\ast/d\ln d=-1$, so the term's elasticity in $d$ is $1-\xi_r-d/(d+\delta_f)=\delta_f/(d+\delta_f)-\xi_r$, and in $\Gamma_\delta$ it is $1-\xi_r-\delta d/(1-\delta+\delta d+\delta\delta_f)$, the stated sign; both thresholds are below one when $d>0$. (3) $k_0$ moves only $X^\ast$, and $r/X^\ast$ falls in $X^\ast$ iff $\xi_r<1$. (4) The term's elasticity in $\kappa$ is $1+(\xi_r-1)\,d\ln X^\ast/d\ln\kappa=1+(\xi_r-1)\omega$. $\square$

*Check (CH).* The growth factor of the relative mode, simulated in the nonlinear model for five channel forms (logarithmic, linear, $X^{0.5}$, $X^2$, $\ln(1+X)$), with and without diffusion, agrees in sign with the rule in every case, and $\Gamma$ read off it equals (2) to $10^{-3}$. Logarithmic channel: $\Gamma=0.8571\to0.9643$ when $d$ rises from 0.2 to 0.3 with $\delta_f=0.1$, and unchanged at 1.2857 without diffusion. Linear channel: $1.125\to0.750$. Raising $k_0$ from 0.1 to 0.2: the logarithmic channel's gain falls from 1.2857 to 1.0000, the linear channel's stays at 1.125, the $X^2$ channel's rises from 1.75 to 2.25. A ratio channel's $\Gamma_\delta$ is 0.853, 1.157 and 1.409 at $d=0.1,0.2,0.4$ ($\delta=0.9$) while its $\Gamma$ is unchanged. An independent spectral computation ($n=2$, $\kappa\Omega=1$, $k_0=0.2$, $\delta_f=0.1$): raising $d$ from 0.1 to 0.6 takes a ratio channel's gain from 0.71 to 1.22 and its spectral radius from 0.943 to 1.157, so equal standing becomes unstable, while it takes a level channel's gain from 3.0 to 0.86 and its radius from 1.40 to 0.90 [num: CH].

The proposition is why the levers of Section 2.2 attach to channels: provision to capacity, correction and non-appropriability to rule support, sharing and diffusion to all. Making winnings decay faster pushes the wrong lever; changing what winning converts into pushes the right one. The field model's correction rate in $G_{ij}=a_ib_{ij}/(c_id_i)$ is of the level kind; the depreciation of capacity in (1) is of the ratio kind. A shared symbol $d$ is not a shared mechanism, and the tables of Section 7.1 and S7 split the row: correction of rule support lowers the gain and the drift of the working objective; faster turnover of capacity leaves the gain unchanged without diffusion and raises it with diffusion, raises the discounted gain and so narrows the working objective, and, where $\Gamma>1$, opens leads faster, since the gap factor of Remark S2.1 rises with $d$. Provision acts through capacity.

**Remark S2.5 (What $\Gamma>1$ asks, at illustrative values) [num].** $\Gamma$ is not estimated for any channel. For a single ratio channel with nothing shared and no provision ($\beta=0$, $\omega=1$), (2) gives $\Gamma=rd/(d+\delta_f)$, so leads compound iff $r>1+\delta_f/d$. At the diffusion rate used to illustrate what a lead buys (0.1 a month; Remark S8.2) and depreciation of 0.02 to 0.05 a month, that requires decisiveness between 3 and 6; at diffusion of 0.05 a month, between 2 and 3.5; where diffusion is blocked, $r>1$ suffices [num: CH]. Provision raises each threshold by the factor $1/\omega$, and sharing by $1/(1-\beta)$; in contests where a lead discourages the follower, strategic response lowers it (Remark S2.2). A channel of rule support is of the level kind: its decisiveness is $aZ^\ast$, which correction lowers (Proposition S2.4), so no bound of this form applies to it, and its gain is higher at the present concentration of standing than at parity (Bounds above). These values are not observations; they show which parameter carries the inequality in each channel. What can be observed is not $\Gamma$ but the ratio of conduct's response to shared harm and to private cost, $1+\theta$, which provision raises in ratio channels; Test Γ (Section 7.4) registers that observable.

### What the loop gain unifies, and what it does not

| Result | A comparative static of $\Gamma$ or $\Gamma_\delta$? |
|---|---|
| Compounding (Proposition 2.3) | Yes: the single-channel case |
| Which levers lower the gain (Proposition S2.4) | Yes: comparative statics of (2), channel by channel |
| Rivals who respond (Proposition 2.2(4), Remark S2.2) | The same form, with $r^{\rm eq}$ for stability and, where $p_i$ is a share, $r^{\rm pr}$ along the relative shift for the price; in the lottery the price is computed, and the magnitudes are not functions of the structural $\Gamma$ |
| The feedback benchmark (Remark S2.6) | Of the same form, not a case: a cross-coupled pair through which success and control feed each other, where Proposition 2.2 is a rank-one perturbation through the single win share; both are stable iff a loop gain is below one |
| Influence over correctors and certificates (Proposition 4.3, Proposition 4.10) | Of the same form, evaluated at a captured state |
| Working objective, visibility of the host, levies, reneging (Proposition 3.9, Proposition 3.5) | Yes, through the working self-model: through $\Gamma_\delta$ with a fixed prize, and through the ratio of relative to common return when the prize grows with capacity |
| Talk, between two parties and among three or more (Proposition 5.16) | Yes, through the working self-model: under a common self-model the effective bias between sender and receiver rises as the self-model narrows |
| The breakout probability and the decisive value in the enforcement condition | Partly: $\Gamma$ decides whether a gap grows and $\Lambda^\ast$ how fast (Remark S2.1); whether a position beyond sanction exists, and detection lags, do not depend on it |
| A countervailing channel with delay (Remark S2.3) | No: the deviation map is not nonnegative, and stability is not decided by a net gain |
| Tempo and coverage (Proposition 5.12, Theorem 5.13) | No: they concern the absolute growth of capability |

This is why the core speaks of interconnected conditions with one structural loop gain at their core, and not of a single pressure from which every signature follows.

### Proof of Proposition 2.3

(1) With one stock and $\sigma_i=r\ln K_i$, $r_K=r$ and $\omega=\kappa_c\Omega/(\kappa_c\Omega+nk_0)$. The condition $\Gamma>1$ reads $(1-\beta)r\kappa_c\Omega d_K>(\kappa_c\Omega+nk_0)(d_K+\delta_f)$; with $S^\ast=(\kappa_c\Omega+nk_0)/d_K$ this is $\kappa_c\Omega((1-\beta)r-1)>nk_0+\delta_fS^\ast$, and $\Gamma<1$ is the reverse. With $k_0=\delta_f=0$ it is $(1-\beta)r>1$, independent of $\kappa_c$. At equality $\Gamma=1$, the linearization is neutral, and neither Proposition 2.2 nor this proposition decides.

(2) *The laggard's inflow.* With the others at $\bar K$ and the laggard at $K$, the mean is $\bar X=(K+(n-1)\bar K)/n$, so diffusion adds $-\delta_f(K-\bar X)=\delta_f\frac{n-1}n(\bar K-K)$ to the laggard's inflow; V6.1 dropped this term. Adding the contest inflow, provision and depreciation gives $\phi$.

*(i)* At the cap $\phi(\bar K)=\kappa_c\Omega/n+k_0-d_K\bar K=d_K(K^\ast-\bar K)$. For $\bar K<K^\ast$ this is positive, so by continuity $\phi>0$ just below the cap and a laggard there returns. At $\bar K=K^\ast$, $\phi(K^\ast)=0$, and the sign just below the cap is that of $-\phi'(K^\ast)$, with
$$\phi'(K^\ast)=\frac{(1-\beta)\kappa_c\Omega\,r(n-1)}{n^2K^\ast}-d_K-\delta_f\frac{n-1}n=d_K\Big[\Gamma_0\frac{n-1}n-1\Big]-\delta_f\frac{n-1}n,$$
using $\kappa_c\Omega/n=d_K\omega K^\ast$. This gives (i). The equality of the cap is the interesting boundary: without diffusion, for $1<\Gamma<n/(n-1)$ equal standing is unstable without a cap, yet a cap at exactly $K^\ast$ holds a laggard, because the cap stops the leaders from pulling away.

*(ii)* With the others held, the laggard's capacity follows the scalar map $K'=\min\{K+\phi(K),\bar K\}$, so it reaches the cap from $K$ iff $\phi>0$ on $(K,\bar K)$, and from every start iff $\phi>0$ on $(0,\bar K)$. As $K\to0^+$ with $r>1$ the contest term is $O(K^r)=o(K)$, so $\phi(K)=\phi(0^+)-d_KK+o(K)$ with $\phi(0^+)=\beta\kappa_c\Omega/n+k_0+\delta_f(n-1)\bar K/n$; if this is zero, $\phi<0$ near zero and laggards below a threshold are excluded. If it is positive, exclusion needs $\phi$ to dip below zero somewhere, which $\beta\kappa_c\Omega/n+k_0\ge d_K\bar K$ rules out: $\phi$ is at least the linear function $\beta\kappa_c\Omega/n+k_0+\delta_f\frac{n-1}n(\bar K-K)-d_KK$, which decreases in $K$ and is non-negative at $\bar K$, hence positive on $(0,\bar K)$.

*Leaders held.* A leader's inflow at the cap is at least $\kappa_c\Omega/n+k_0-d_K\bar K-\delta_f\bar K/n$, since its win share is at least $1/n$ and its loss to diffusion is $\delta_f(\bar K-K)/n\le\delta_f\bar K/n$; this is non-negative iff $\bar K\le(\kappa_c\Omega+nk_0)/(nd_K+\delta_f)$, the Scope's bound. $\square$ At $\bar K=K^\ast$ with diffusion the bound fails, and the leaders are held only locally: with the laggard a small $x$ below the cap, a leader's net inflow is $(x/n)(d_K\Gamma_0-\delta_f)$ to first order, so for $\delta_f>d_K\Gamma_0$ the leaders leave the cap with the laggard. Part (i) holds to first order with every carrier moving nonetheless, in two cases. If $\delta_f<d_K\Gamma_0$, the leaders' first-order inflow is positive, so they stay at the cap and (i) applies as proved. If $\delta_f>d_K\Gamma_0$, then $\Gamma=\Gamma_0d_K/(d_K+\delta_f)<\Gamma_0/(1+\Gamma_0)<1$, so equal standing without the cap is stable and every carrier returns to $K^\ast$; and the condition of (i) for return holds of itself, since $\delta_f/d_K>\Gamma_0$ gives $1+\delta_f(n-1)/(nd_K)>1+\Gamma_0(n-1)/n>\Gamma_0(n-1)/n$. On the knife edge $\delta_f=d_K\Gamma_0$ the first order is silent, and nothing is claimed there. $\square$ The local statements of (i) were also checked in simulations in which every carrier moves under the cap, and agree in every case computed (below); over 299 further random settings with diffusion, 243 below the knife edge and 56 above it, the leaders stay at the cap in every setting of the first case, and in every setting of the second $\Gamma<1$ and every carrier returns to $K^\ast$ [num: CE, VF].

*Growth variants.* (a) *Fixed stakes, growing capacity.* Without depreciation, total capacity $S$ grows by $\kappa_c\Omega+nk_0$ a period, and the share deviation has multiplier $[S(1-\delta_f)+\kappa_c\Omega(1-\beta)r]/(S+\kappa_c\Omega+nk_0)$. Equal standing is therefore unstable at a date iff $\kappa_c\Omega((1-\beta)r-1)>\delta_fS+nk_0$ with $S$ the current total, a condition that changes with time. (b) *Stakes proportional to capacity.* With $\Omega_t=gS_t$, $k_0=0$ and depreciation $d_K$, total capacity grows by the factor $G=1-d_K+\kappa_cg$ a period, and the share deviation has multiplier $m=\big[(1-d_K-\delta_f)+\kappa_cg(1-\beta)r\big]/G$. So equal shares are unstable iff $\kappa_cg((1-\beta)r-1)>\delta_f$, a condition constant in time, and without diffusion iff $(1-\beta)r>1$. That is the condition under which the working objective narrows when the prize is proportional to capacity (Proposition 3.9): without diffusion, narrowing and instability coincided in 24 of 24 simulated cases [num: SP]. With diffusion the two thresholds part: equal shares become unstable only at $(1-\beta)r>1+\delta_f/(\kappa_cg)$, while the objective narrows from a lower threshold, derived in S3; at $r=1.17$, $\delta_f=0.03$ the objective narrows while equal shares are stable [num: SP].

*Numerics (CE).*
- *Cap equality.* Ten cases of the local condition at $\bar K=K^\ast$, simulated with every carrier moving under the cap, each predicted and observed in agreement. Among them, $n=4$, $r=2$: with $\delta_f=0.3$ the laggard returns, with $\delta_f=0.1$ it falls to a stalled $0.48K^\ast$, with $\delta_f=0$ it is excluded; with provision $k_0=0.05$ and $\Gamma_0(n-1)/n=1.026$ it falls away and stalls at $0.41K^\ast$. An outside reviewer's case ($n=2$, $r=3$, cap at $K^\ast=2.5$, no sharing, provision or diffusion): from $(2.499,2.5)$ the laggard falls to $2.4\times10^{-32}$. Over 400 random settings the criterion at $\bar K=K^\ast$ predicts the simulated outcome in 400; without diffusion, a laggard at a cap equal to $K^\ast$ returns iff $\Gamma<n/(n-1)$ in four further cases.
- *Strict caps.* Caps at $0.98K^\ast$ and $0.9K^\ast$ at $\Gamma=3$ restore a laggard just below; over 400 random settings a cap at $0.97K^\ast$ is restored in 400.
- *Global criterion.* $\phi>0$ on $(0,\bar K)$ agrees with simulation from 1% of the cap in 444 of 446 random settings with the leaders provably held; in the two others the exclusion threshold (0.7% and 0.2% of the cap) lies below the start, so the criterion, which speaks of every start, is right.
- *Diffusion as a source.* Diffusion draws a laggard toward the mean, since what one carrier learns others can learn. The same reviewer's case $n=2$, $r=2$, $d_K=0.2$, $\delta_f=0.6$, cap $K^\ast=2.5$, no sharing or provision: $\phi$ stays positive on $(0,\bar K)$, its minimum about $10^{-4}$, and $(0.01,2.5)$ goes to $(2.5,2.5)$. *Not a guarantee:* with $\beta=k_0=\delta_f=0$, $r=2$, a laggard starting at 0.3 of a cap at $0.9K^\ast$ is excluded; with $\delta_f=0.05$ alone it falls and stalls at 0.16 of the cap, since $\phi(0^+)=0.056>0$ but $\phi$ dips to $-0.013$.
- *Each inflow suffices alone.* $n=5$, $r=1.5$, cap 0.8: with none of the three, laggards starting at 0.05 and 0.2 are excluded; with $\beta=0.1$, $k_0=0.05$ or $\delta_f=0.15$ alone, all return.
- *A cap below $K^\ast$ (SM).* $n=5$, $r=1.5$, a cap of 1.5 below $K^\ast=2$, and a laggard starting at 0.1: with neither sharing nor provision the laggard is excluded; with $\beta=0.02$ or $k_0=0.002$ it stalls near 0.06; with $\beta=0.2$ or $k_0=0.03$ all reach the cap. The exact condition predicts each case.

**Remark S2.6 (A local feedback benchmark) [thm].** With $u$ a small deviation in a carrier's success and $w$ a deviation in its favourable control over the rules, let $u'=(1-d_u)u+aw$ and $w'=bu+(1-d_w)w$, with cross-gains $a,b\ge0$ and decays $0<d_u,d_w\le1$. The pair is stable exactly when $ab<d_ud_w$, that is, when the loop gain $(a/d_u)(b/d_w)$ is below one. This is the stability condition of Richardson's arms-race model with control over rules in place of armaments (Richardson 1960). It is of the same form as Proposition 2.2, a loop gain below one, but not a case of it: here success and control feed each other directly across a pair, there every stock is fed through the single win share. Its use is that each coefficient names a channel that can be measured. Instability does not prove capture or harm, and stability does not prove that an excursion stays within a safety constraint.

*Proof.* The characteristic polynomial is $(\Lambda-1+d_u)(\Lambda-1+d_w)-ab=0$, with roots $1-\frac{d_u+d_w}2\pm\sqrt{\frac{(d_u-d_w)^2}4+ab}$. The larger root is below one iff $ab<d_ud_w$; then the square root is below $(d_u+d_w)/2$, so the smaller root exceeds $1-(d_u+d_w)\ge-1$. $\square$ The statement is an algebraic identity; block FB keeps it as a regression check against numerically computed spectral radii [num: FB].

## S3. One system

### Hosts harsher than the forgiving one

The host of Section 3.1 is forgiving: per unit it regenerates fastest when most depleted, and it has no threshold of its own. Real shared conditions, such as trust, institutions, information environments and skills, are closer to hosts that degrade by themselves below a threshold, and they are vectors of coupled components. For a threshold host a load later removed can leave it collapsed, and with two coupled components collapse can begin at half the load at which one would fail while their average reads as healthy. Comparison results transfer in one direction only. A *harsher* host, one whose regeneration per unit is no greater than $\rho(1-V)$ at every level, such as $\rho_A(V/A-1)(1-V)$ with $\rho_A=\rho A/(1-A)$, makes every *crossing* result hold a fortiori. *Viability* results do not transfer, and need a margin even on the forgiving host: Theorem 10.4 keeps the trajectory above the floor only with a liability margin set by the host, the floor, the initial composition and the speed of selection; without it the forgiving host dips below its floor and recovers, while a host that degrades by itself below a threshold does not recover (S10). A host normalized to the same peak regeneration is not harsher in this sense, since above about a third of capacity it regenerates faster.

### Proof of Lemma 3.3

(1) A relative rule satisfies $\mathcal F(p,\pi+G\mathbf 1)=\mathcal F(p,\pi)$ at every instant, so shares solve the same equation whatever $G(V(t))$ is; a common term leaves every $\pi_k-\pi_l$, and so the sign of relative growth, unchanged.

(2) An equal additive cost is a common term among those who bear it. Costs $c_k$ differing across carriers change $\pi_k-\pi_l$ by $c_l-c_k$, which a payoff-monotone rule selects on: the carrier the constraint costs least gains share among the compliers, and a carrier that alone bears a cost loses share to every other.

(3) *Within a type.* An evader of type $k$ pays the expected sanction $\bar F_k$ instead of the cost $c_k$, so its payoff exceeds that of a complier of its own type by $c_k-\bar F_k$, and under a payoff-monotone rule the evading variant grows relative to the complying one iff $c_k>\bar F_k$. *Survival and prevailing.* If $\bar F_k\ge c_k$ for every type that can evade, no evading variant grows against its own type's compliers, so the compliant share stays bounded away from zero. If every inequality is strict, then under cumulative selection (Definition 3.2), in which the log-ratio of two variants moves at least in proportion to the integral of their payoff gap, as under replicator dynamics, each evading variant's share relative to its own type's complying variant tends to zero, so the compliant share tends to one whatever the payoffs across types, which only re-sort the compliers. *Not necessary across types.* A type whose evasion pays, $c_k>\bar F_k$, is still eliminated, evaders included, if another type's compliers earn more than its evaders. *Falling sanction.* With one type and $\bar F(x)$ continuous and strictly decreasing in the evading share $x$, $\dot x=x(1-x)\big(c-\bar F(x)\big)$ under replicator dynamics, and the sign of $\dot x$ is that of $c-\bar F(x)$ under any payoff-monotone rule. A root $x^\dagger\in(0,1)$ of $\bar F(x)=c$ exists iff $\bar F(0)>c>\bar F(1)$, by the intermediate value theorem; it is unique, and unstable because $c-\bar F$ is increasing, so full compliance and full evasion are both stable and $x^\dagger$ separates their basins. Otherwise $c-\bar F$ has one sign on $(0,1)$: compliance is globally stable if $\bar F(1)\ge c$, evasion if $\bar F(0)\le c$. $\square$

*Numerics (CI).* With costs $(0.3,0.1)$ relative growth rates are $(-0.1,+0.1)$; with an equal cost 0.2, $(0,0)$; a universal cap at 0.4 on loads $(1,0.6,0.2)$ costs the three carriers $(0.6,0.2,0)$ and reverses their ranking. With $c=0.3$ evaders take over when the expected sanction is 0.25 or 0.27 and vanish when it is 0.35 or 0.4. Detection funded by compliers, $\bar F(x)=q_0(1-x)F$ with $q_0=0.8$ and $F=0.6$, gives the tipping share $1-c/(q_0F)=0.375$: starts at evading shares 0.05 and 0.2 return to full compliance, and 0.4 and 0.6 go to full evasion. The endpoints decide, as stated: with $c=0.3$, $\bar F=0.5+0.5(1-x)$, which never falls below the cost, restores compliance from every start; $0.24(1-x)$, which starts below it, leads to evasion from every start; $0.48(1-x)$ and $0.6-0.4x$ are bistable with tipping shares 0.375 and 0.75, and $1.2(1-x)$, integrated independently, with 0.75. Two types with compliance costs 0.1 and 0.2: with sanctions 1 and 1, compliance goes to one and all of it is the cheaper type, the dearer complier displaced (from equal shares the compliant share is 0.99999997 at $t=20$): incidence re-sorting the compliers; with sanctions 1 and 0.15 the second type's evasion pays, yet its evaders lose to the first type's compliers and compliance still goes to one; with sanctions 1 and 0.05 the second type's evaders earn most and evasion takes over. Equal costs leave the compliers' relative shares where they started. A common liability $\tau=1.5>\nu=1$ drives the high-load share to zero; $\tau=0.5$ drives it to one.

### Proof of Proposition 3.4

With $\phi_k=\phi_l$, $\pi_k-\pi_l=(\nu-\tau_{\rm eff})(e_k-e_l)>0$ and $G$ cancels. Under replicator dynamics $\dot E=\operatorname{Cov}_p(e,\pi)=(\nu-\tau_{\rm eff})\operatorname{Var}_p(e)+\operatorname{Cov}_p(e,\phi)$, so the mean load rises unless restraint is correlated strongly enough with other advantages; a rising mean does not imply that the maximal-load carrier gains share (shares $1/3$, loads $(0,0.9,1)$, other advantages $(0,10,0)$, $\nu=1$: the mean load rises at 1.09 while the maximal-load share falls at 0.99). With all $\phi_k$ equal, replicator dynamics give $\frac{d}{dt}\ln(p_k/p_l)=(e_k-e_l)(\nu-\tau_{\rm eff})$, and any cumulative rule gives at least $\varkappa$ times that while $\nu>\tau_{\rm eff}$, so $B(t)\to\infty$ sends the combined share of maximal-load carriers to one and $E\to\bar e$. For $\epsilon>0$, eventually $\dot V\le V[\rho(1-V)-h_V(\bar e-\epsilon)]$, so $\limsup V\le(1-h_V(\bar e-\epsilon)/\rho)^+$; let $\epsilon\to0$, and (reach) puts the bound below $V_{\min}$. Under replicator dynamics, if $B(\infty)<\infty$, each $\ln(p_k/p_l)$ converges. $\square$

*Why cumulative.* Payoff-monotonicity alone fixes the sign of relative growth, not its size, and does not carry the second sentence. Under the relative, payoff-monotone rule $\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{logit}x=(\Delta\pi)^3$, with a race carrier of load 1 against one of load 0, $\nu=1$ and $\tau_{\rm eff}=\nu-1/(1+t)$, the cumulative advantage $B(t)=\ln(1+t)$ grows without bound, yet the race share $x$, from 0.001, never exceeds 0.0017, and the host of R1's example ($\rho=0.05$, $h_V=0.08$, floor 0.3) stays above 0.99; under replicator dynamics on the same path the share passes one half and the host falls to 0.017 by $t=2000$ [num: VF]. The rule is autonomous and of the form of Definition 3.2; what fails is the rate bound.

What carriers value decides which strategies they try, not which ones grow: this is evolutionary suicide, under stated conditions (Rankin, Bargum and Kokko 2007).

### Proof of Proposition 3.5, and measuring $\theta$

With $\Delta\bar\pi_{-i}=(n\bar\Delta-\Delta\pi_i)/(n-1)$, $\Delta J_i=\frac{n-1-\theta}{n-1}\Delta\pi_i+\frac{n\theta}{n-1}\bar\Delta$; writing $\Delta\pi_i=\bar\Delta+(\Delta\pi_i-\bar\Delta)$ the coefficient of $\bar\Delta$ is $1+\theta$. (1): each $\pi_j$ appears in $n-1$ averages, so $\sum_iJ_i=(1+\theta)\sum_i\pi_i$. (2): at $\theta=-1$ objectives sum to zero at every profile; at $\theta=n-1$ each objective is the total, so a total-maximizing profile admits no profitable deviation. (3): $\pi_k-\sum_jp_j\pi_j=(1-p_k)[\pi_k-\sum_{j\ne k}\frac{p_j}{1-p_k}\pi_j]$; and $\pi_i+\lambda(\pi_i-\bar\pi)=(1+\lambda\frac{n-1}n)\pi_i-\lambda\frac{n-1}n\bar\pi_{-i}$. (4): a rebated levy is a transfer with $\bar\Delta=0$ and own part $-\tau(n-1)/n$; withholding a promised $\beta\Omega$ from the $n-1$ others gives own part $\beta\Omega$ and $\bar\Delta=0$, hence $\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/(n-1)$; if the promised share was to be divided among all $n$, the gain is $\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/n$ [num: SM(1)]. (5): $\Delta\pi_i=g-H/n$ and the others' $-H/n$, so $\Delta J_i=g-(1+\theta)H/n$. An act of spite that costs its taker $c_y$ and the others $\Delta_y$ in total pays iff $\theta<-(n-1)c_y/\Delta_y$. $\square$ (TC: 4,000 random games, an identity check.)

**Measuring the weight.** In the contest of Section 3.4 the symmetric intensity depends on the private cost $c$ and the shared loss $\ell$ only through $c+(1+\theta)\ell$, so the ratio of its response to the shared loss to its response to private cost is exactly $1+\theta$, the weight of the common channel; read from numerical equilibria it is 0.50004, 0.99996, 0.56997 and 1.99960 against $1+\theta=0.5$, 1, 0.57 and 2 [num: SP]. This is the observable Test Γ needs (Section 7.4). Because the working $\theta$ of Proposition 3.9 rises when provision lowers the won share of capacity, the observable rises with provision: in the checked case, from 0.58 with the whole of capacity won to 0.81 with half of it provided [num: SP]. That is the sign Test Γ must use. A second identification sets a change in the shared loss, which enters through the common channel, against a rebated levy, which enters through the differential one: the ratio of the two responses is $n(1+\theta)/(n-1-\theta)$ [num: TH]. If actors perceive only a share $\psi$ of the shared loss, both ratios are scaled by $\psi$, so $\theta$ and $\psi$ are confounded: with half perceived, a true $\theta=0$ reads as $-0.5$ by the first ratio and $-0.43$ by the second [num: SM(3)]. Separating $\theta$ from $\psi$ needs a third perturbation that changes the visibility of shared harm without changing its size, or an independent measure of perceived harm. The identification fails exactly where the theory says shared harm goes unseen.

### Full statement and proof of Proposition 3.7

*Let $n$ symmetric developers with $\tau_{\rm eff}=0$ and the host at steady state maximize $u_k=\pi_k+\lambda(\pi_k-\bar\pi)$, with $G(V)=g_VV$, $h_V\bar e<\rho$ and $\Upsilon=g_Vh_V/(\rho\nu)$; write $w_\lambda=1+\lambda(n-1)/n$. (1) Restraint is dominant if $w_\lambda<\Upsilon/n$, depletion if reversed. (2) If $\Upsilon>1$, joint payoff is maximized by full restraint while the equilibrium is full depletion whenever $\lambda>(\Upsilon-n)/(n-1)$, at every $\lambda$ when $n>\Upsilon$. (3) Every developer then loses $\nu\bar e(\Upsilon-1)$. (4) Under (reach) the equilibrium host is below the floor.*

*Proof.* $\pi_k-\bar\pi$ gives $G$ weight zero, so $\partial u_k/\partial e_k=w_\lambda\nu-g_Vh_V/(n\rho)$, independent of others' choices. Joint payoff at a common $e$ has slope $n\nu(1-\Upsilon)$. Payoffs are $\nu\bar e+g_V(1-h_V\bar e/\rho)$ at full depletion and $g_V$ under restraint. $\square$

A classical commons needs each actor's share of the loss to be small; by (2), selection on relative standing depletes this one even among a privileged few, since full depletion is the equilibrium at every $\lambda$ when $n>\Upsilon$ and once $\lambda>(\Upsilon-n)/(n-1)$ otherwise.

**Remark S3.1 (Thresholds and repetition need observation).** If crossing the floor costs each a common loss $L$, a developer pivotal for crossing restrains when $L>(w_\lambda-\Upsilon/n)\nu\bar e$, and the game becomes coordination at the threshold (Barrett 2013). Joint restraint is sustained by reversion whenever $\delta\ge(w_\lambda-\Upsilon/n)/(w_\lambda-\Upsilon/n+\Upsilon-1)$: for $\Upsilon=3$, $n=4$ the cutoff is 0.11 at $\lambda=0$, 0.47 at $\lambda=2$ and 0.95 at $\lambda=50$. Uncertainty about the threshold turns coordination back into a dilemma (Barrett and Dannenberg 2012), and noisy monitoring makes reversion costly (Green and Porter 1984).

### Proof of Proposition 3.6

Write $\pi_i=w_i\Omega_e-ce_i-\ell\mathcal E+w_i\Omega_s$: the first three terms are the divisible part, the last the positional part. (1) An objective with one weight $\theta$ on all payoffs is $J_i=\Omega(1-\frac{\theta}{n-1})w_i-(c+(1+\theta)\ell)e_i+$ terms independent of $e_i$: a contest with prize $\Omega(n-1-\theta)/(n-1)$ and unit cost $c+(1+\theta)\ell$, whose symmetric pure equilibrium for $r\le n/(n-1)$ gives $e(\theta)$ (Pérez-Castrillo and Verdier 1992). At a symmetric profile $\pi^{\rm e}=\Omega_e/n-e(c+n\ell)$ and $\pi^{\rm s}=\Omega_s/n$, so $W=(1+\kappa)\pi^{\rm e}+(1+\kappa_s)\pi^{\rm s}$, which falls with $e$ and therefore rises with $\theta$. (2) As a function of $e_i$ with the others fixed, $W_i$ is a contest objective with prize $\big[\Omega_e(n-1-\kappa)+\Omega_s(n-1-\kappa_s)\big]/(n-1)$ and unit cost $c+(1+\kappa)\ell$, since the others' divisible winnings fall by what $i$ wins and their positional winnings count with weight $\kappa_s$: it is the objective that weighs the others' divisible payoffs by $\kappa$ and their positional payoffs by $\kappa_s$, and its best response maximizes $W_i$. Lowering the divisible weight below $\kappa$ raises the marginal value of intensity by $\Omega_e\,(\partial w_i/\partial e_i)/(n-1)+\ell>0$ per unit of weight, so the chosen intensity moves away from the welfare maximizer whenever it moves. On the positional part, $\kappa_s\le0$ makes the positional prize count at least fully: a rival's positional gain is the holder's loss, and relative concern there is accurate. (3) A finite-population evolutionarily stable intensity maximizes $\pi_i-\bar\pi_{-i}$ (Schaffer 1988), the case $\theta=-1$, in which the shared loss carries weight $1+\theta=0$; at $r=1$ it dissipates the whole prize (Hehenkamp, Leininger and Possajennikov 2004), so with $\ell>0$ every actor's payoff is negative. For $r>1$ asymmetric equilibria with lower total intensity also exist in a band below $n/(n-1)$; the symmetric one is used throughout. $\square$ (RH: the closed forms, and a grid best response.)

**Which preferences evolve.** It depends on the population: with unobserved preferences in large random populations, material self-interest (Ok and Vega-Redondo 2001; Dekel, Ely and Yilankaya 2007); with observed preferences and at least three actors in this contest, a commitment to a narrower objective (Güth and Yaari 1992; Heifetz, Shannon and Spiegel 2007); with high observability and assortative matching, cooperative preferences (Alger and Weibull 2013). Local competition favours spite (Alger, Weibull and Lehmann 2020), and an observable commitment to aggression is itself a race (Dixit 1987; Fershtman and Judd 1987). That competition selects organizations that behave *as if* they maximize (Alchian 1950) is part of this theory, not an objection to it: Proposition 3.6(3) is the selection that produces the conduct of Ψ whether or not anyone holds the doctrine (Section 3.8).

### Full form and proof of Proposition 3.9

*Full form.* In the deterministic share model of Proposition 2.2 let the prize depend on the total stocks $S^j=\sum_iX^j_i$, with $g_j=\partial\Omega/\partial S^j\ge0$, and take as reference a symmetric steady state or a balanced path along which the linearized coefficients are constant. For nonlinear lifetime values also impose the discounted-tail condition below. The relative and the common loop gain, evaluated at the reference state, are
$$\Gamma_\delta=(1-\beta)\sum_j\frac{\delta\kappa_jr_j\,\Omega/S^j}{1-\delta(1-d_j-\delta_{f,j})},\qquad \Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta=\sum_j\frac{\delta\kappa_jg_j}{1-\delta(1-d_j)}.$$
At a steady state $d_jS^j=\kappa_j\Omega+nk_{0j}$, so $\kappa_j\Omega/S^j=\omega_jd_j$ and $\Gamma_\delta$ is the discounted loop gain of Proposition 2.2(3); with a fixed prize every $g_j=0$ and $\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta=0$. Diffusion enters the relative gain and not the common one, because it acts only on differences between actors.

*Discounted-tail condition.* For every unit direction $v$ of initial payoff perturbation, including mixtures of common and relative changes, write $w_t(\varepsilon v)$ for a carrier's actual winnings at future date $t$. Assume discounted winnings are finite for all sufficiently small perturbations, finite-date winnings are differentiable at zero as functions of the payoff vector, and
$$\lim_{T\to\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to0}\sup_{\|v\|=1}\sum_{t>T}\delta^t\left|\frac{w_t(\varepsilon v)-w_t(0)}{\varepsilon}\right|=0.$$
This bounds the discounted difference-quotient tails uniformly over directions, so the local common and relative derivatives combine into the stated first-order objective. Differentiate each finite sum, then send its horizon to infinity: the displayed condition makes the omitted tail vanish uniformly, so the sum of the linearized winnings equals the derivative of nonlinear lifetime winnings. Without this condition or another justification for that interchange, the closed forms below value the linearized system only. The condition holds for a fixed prize on $\Gamma_\delta<1$ by S2's bounded-winnings tail argument. In the one-stock proportional-prize model of Proposition S3.2(2), total capacity and the prize grow at $\Lambda_{\rm com}$, while normalized winnings are bounded; the same argument uses effective discount $\delta\Lambda_{\rm com}<1$ and relative multiplier $\Lambda_{\rm rel}/\Lambda_{\rm com}$, whose product is $\delta\Lambda_{\rm rel}<1$. The common perturbation is exactly linear there.

*A stationary counterexample without the tail condition.* Set $n=2$, $\kappa=d=\delta=1/2$, $r=1$, $\beta=k_0=\delta_f=0$, and $\Omega(S)=S^2$. At the symmetric steady state $S=1$, the coefficients are constant and the gains are $\Gamma_\delta=1/3$, $\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta=2/3$. The linearized prices are therefore $1/2$ and $2$. But any positive common perturbation gives $S_{t+1}=(S_t+S_t^2)/2$ with $S_0>1$, which increases without bound. Once $S_t\ge4$, the total at least doubles per period and discounted winnings $2^{-t}S_t^2/2$ grow by at least a factor two. Actual lifetime winnings are infinite for every such perturbation, although the reference value is finite. Thus constant reference coefficients and gains below one do not replace the tail condition.

*Decomposition.* Split a change in today's net payoffs into its common part $\bar\Delta$ and the relative part $y=\Delta\pi_i-\bar\Delta$, and let every actor convert net payoff into stock $j$ at the rate $\kappa_j$. To first order the two parts evolve separately: the prize depends on the totals, and a relative shift leaves the totals unchanged.

*Relative part.* The actor's stocks rise by $\kappa_jy$ and the others' fall by as much in total. Relative deviations follow the Jacobian $\mathcal J=\mathcal D+\frac1n\mathbf u\mathbf v^\top$ of S2, with $\mathcal D=\operatorname{diag}(1-d_j-\delta_{f,j})$ and $\frac1nu_jv_j=(1-\beta)\kappa_jr_j\Omega/S^j$ at the reference state. By the Sherman–Morrison argument of S2, applied to $\delta\mathcal J$, the discounted sum of the linearized winnings is $\lambda_{\rm rel}y$ with $\lambda_{\rm rel}=\Gamma_\delta/(1-\Gamma_\delta)$, finite iff $\Gamma_\delta<1$. The discounted-tail condition makes this the derivative of actual future winnings as well. Since the totals are unchanged, the others' winnings fall by $\lambda_{\rm rel}y$ in total.

*Common part.* Every actor's stock $j$ rises by $\kappa_j\bar\Delta$, so the total $S^j$ rises by $n\kappa_j\bar\Delta$ and the prize by $n\sum_kg_k\kappa_k\bar\Delta$, of which each actor receives $1/n$ at equal standing. The common deviations $c^j$ per actor therefore follow $c^{j\prime}=(1-d_j)c^j+\kappa_j\sum_kg_kc^k$, with the rank-one Jacobian $\operatorname{diag}(1-d_j)+\boldsymbol\kappa\mathbf g^\top$, on which diffusion does not act, and each actor's winnings rise by $\sum_kg_kc^k_t$ in period $t$. By Sherman–Morrison the discounted sum in this linearized system is $\lambda_{\rm com}\bar\Delta$ with $\lambda_{\rm com}=\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta/(1-\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta)$, finite iff $\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta<1$. The discounted-tail condition is needed to identify it with the nonlinear continuation derivative.

*Matching.* The others' average future winnings change by $\lambda_{\rm com}\bar\Delta-\lambda_{\rm rel}y/(n-1)$, so with today's change from Proposition 3.5 the total change in the objective with weight $\theta_0$ is
$$\Delta J_i=(1+\theta_0)(1+\lambda_{\rm com})\,\bar\Delta+\frac{n-1-\theta_0}{n-1}(1+\lambda_{\rm rel})\,y .$$
Matching it to $(1+\theta)\bar\Delta+\frac{n-1-\theta}{n-1}y$ up to a positive factor gives (9), and $1+\lambda=1/(1-\Gamma)$ for each gain gives its second form. Explicitly, with $\varrho=\frac{(1+\theta_0)(1-\Gamma_\delta)}{(n-1-\theta_0)(1-\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta)}$, $\theta=\big(\varrho(n-1)-1\big)/(1+\varrho)$. The map $\theta\mapsto(1+\theta)/(n-1-\theta)$ is increasing on $[-1,n-1)$, so $\theta<\theta_0$ iff $\lambda_{\rm rel}>\lambda_{\rm com}$, and $\theta$ rises with $\theta_0$: no held self-model short of $\theta_0=n-1$ escapes the narrowing.

(1) With a fixed prize, $\lambda_{\rm com}=0$ and $\varrho=(1+\theta_0)(1-\Gamma_\delta)/(n-1-\theta_0)$, so $1+\theta_\Gamma=(1+\theta_0)\,n(1-\Gamma_\delta)/\big(n-(1+\theta_0)\Gamma_\delta\big)$, equivalent to the stated formula; as $\Gamma_\delta\to1^-$, $\varrho\to0$ and $\theta\to-1$. In Proposition 3.5 the host's visibility $(1+\theta)/n$ and the point of enough $(1+\theta)H/n$ fall with $\theta$, while a rebated levy $\tau(n-1-\theta)/n$ and the gain from breaking a promise $\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/(n-1)$ rise. (2) With one stock, $\Omega=gS$ and $k_0=\delta_f=0$, $\kappa_c\Omega/S=\kappa_cg$, so $\Gamma_\delta=\delta(1-\beta)r\kappa_cg/\big(1-\delta(1-d_K)\big)$ and $\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta=\delta\kappa_cg/\big(1-\delta(1-d_K)\big)$, whose ratio is $(1-\beta)r$ at every $\delta$; Proposition S3.2 gives the comparison over every horizon, the instability condition, and the effect of diffusion. $\square$

**Proposition S3.2 (A prize that grows with capacity) [thm].** Where the prize is proportional to total capacity, looking ahead narrows the working self-model exactly where leads compound, until diffusion opens a band between the two; a part of the prize that does not grow with capacity adds to the relative return only. *In the one-channel model of Proposition 2.3 without provision, let a share $\beta$ of the prize be divided equally, and let actors convert net payoff into capacity at the rate $\kappa_c$ and discount at $0<\delta<1$.*
1. *Two periods. If first-period net payoffs become second-period capacity and the second-period prize is $\Omega_2=\Omega_{20}+gS$, with $S=\sum_iK_i$, then at equal standing $\lambda_{\rm rel}=\delta\kappa_c(1-\beta)r\,\Omega_2/S$ and $\lambda_{\rm com}=\delta\kappa_cg$, so $\lambda_{\rm rel}/\lambda_{\rm com}=(1-\beta)r\big(1+\Omega_{20}/(gS)\big)$, which is $(1-\beta)r$ when $\Omega_{20}=0$.*
2. *Infinite horizon. If $\Omega=gS$ and capacity depreciates at $d_K$ and diffuses at $\delta_f$, then along the balanced path, on which $S$ grows by the factor $\Lambda_{\rm com}=1-d_K+\kappa_cg$ a period while a relative deviation grows by $\Lambda_{\rm rel}=1-d_K-\delta_f+(1-\beta)r\kappa_cg$, and where $\delta\Lambda_{\rm com}<1$ and $\delta\Lambda_{\rm rel}<1$,*
$$\lambda_{\rm rel}=\frac{\delta(1-\beta)r\kappa_cg}{1-\delta\Lambda_{\rm rel}},\qquad \lambda_{\rm com}=\frac{\delta\kappa_cg}{1-\delta\Lambda_{\rm com}},$$
*that is, $\lambda=\Gamma/(1-\Gamma)$ for $\Gamma_\delta=\delta(1-\beta)r\kappa_cg/\big(1-\delta(1-d_K-\delta_f)\big)$ and $\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta=\delta\kappa_cg/\big(1-\delta(1-d_K)\big)$. The working self-model narrows iff $(1-\beta)r>1+\delta\delta_f/\big(1-\delta(1-d_K)\big)$, and equal shares are unstable iff $(1-\beta)r>1+\delta_f/(\kappa_cg)$.*
3. *Without diffusion both conditions are $(1-\beta)r>1$, and over every horizon a relative unit is worth more than a common one exactly when $(1-\beta)r>1$. With diffusion the narrowing threshold lies below the instability threshold by $\delta_f(1-\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta)/(\kappa_cg)>0$: between them looking ahead narrows the self while equal shares are stable.*

*Proof.* (1) Future winnings are $w_i=\Omega_2\big[(1-\beta)p_i+\beta/n\big]$ with $p_i=K_i^r/\sum_jK_j^r$, and each actor reinvests $x_j=\kappa_c\Delta\pi_j$. At equal standing $\partial p_i/\partial K_i=r(n-1)/(nS)$, $\partial p_i/\partial K_j=-r/(nS)$ and $\partial\Omega_2/\partial K_j=g$, so $\Delta w_i=(1-\beta)r\,\Omega_2\,(x_i-\bar x)/S+g\bar x$; discounting gives the two returns, and the matching of the main proof gives (9). (2) A relative unit raises the actor's capacity by $\kappa_c$ and lowers the others' by as much in total; since $\Delta p_i=ru_i/S$ for a deviation $u$ summing to zero, its own winnings rise by $(1-\beta)rg$ per unit of capacity deviation, and the deviation is multiplied each period by $\Lambda_{\rm rel}$. A common unit raises every capacity by $\kappa_c$; each actor's winnings rise by $g$ per unit of common capacity, and that deviation is multiplied by $\Lambda_{\rm com}$. Summing the discounted geometric series gives the closed forms. Narrowing is $\lambda_{\rm rel}>\lambda_{\rm com}$, that is $(1-\beta)r(1-\delta\Lambda_{\rm com})>1-\delta\Lambda_{\rm rel}$, which rearranges to $(1-\beta)r\big(1-\delta(1-d_K)\big)>1-\delta(1-d_K)+\delta\delta_f$; shares are unstable iff $\Lambda_{\rm rel}>\Lambda_{\rm com}$, that is $\kappa_cg\big((1-\beta)r-1\big)>\delta_f$. (3) Without diffusion the ratio of the relative to the common increment in period $t$ is $(1-\beta)r\,(\Lambda_{\rm rel}/\Lambda_{\rm com})^{t-1}$, and both factors exceed one exactly when $(1-\beta)r>1$, so every partial sum compares the same way. The gap between the thresholds is $\delta_f/(\kappa_cg)-\delta\delta_f/\big(1-\delta(1-d_K)\big)=\delta_f(1-\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta)/(\kappa_cg)$, positive inside the domain. $\square$
