*Numerics for Proposition S3.2 (SP).* Infinite horizon without depreciation: the simulated ratio of the values of a relative and a common unit equals the closed form in six $(r,\beta)$ pairs (2.53846, 0.68750, 0.62264, 1.00000, 2.08108, 0.92958) and exceeds one exactly when $(1-\beta)r>1$. Without diffusion, narrowing and the instability of equal shares coincide in 24 of 24 simulated cases; with $\delta_f=0.03$ the simulated thresholds are $(1-\beta)r=1.1421$ for narrowing and $1.2000$ for instability, as (2) predicts, so an objective narrows at $r=1.17$ while equal shares are stable.

*The two errors.* For Remark 3.10: $\theta_\Gamma(\kappa)=0$ iff $n\kappa=(n-1)(1+\kappa)\Gamma_\delta$, and $\theta_\Gamma$ falls with $\Gamma_\delta$, so an actor whose held weight is the coupling acts on a weight below the isolated self once $\Gamma_\delta>n\kappa/\big((n-1)(1+\kappa)\big)$, a threshold below one for every $\kappa<n-1$: 0.222, 0.381 and 0.667 for $\kappa=0.2$, 0.4 and 1 at $n=4$, and 0.333 and 0.571 for $\kappa=0.2$ and 0.4 at $n=2$. At $n=4$ and $\kappa=0.4$ the working weight is $+0.15$, $-0.15$, $-0.53$ and $-0.80$ at $\Gamma_\delta=0.25$, 0.5, 0.75 and 0.9 [num: SP].

*Balanced paths.* The closed form uses one Jacobian at the reference state, so it needs linearized coefficients that stay constant along the path: a steady state, or a balanced path along which strength depends on ratios of stocks and the prize is homogeneous of degree one, as in part (2). Where the elasticity changes along the path, as with strength linear in a growing stock, the derivative of future winnings is a sum over products of time-dependent Jacobians, $\sum_t\delta^t\,\mathbf w_t^\top\mathcal J_{t-1}\cdots\mathcal J_0$, which converges only under a condition on that product; a gain below one at the reference state does not certify it (S14) [num: CX].

*Numerics for Proposition 3.9 (SP, PD, NB, LG).* Algebraic checks, first order by construction at a symmetric point: in a two-period contest in which first-period net payoffs become second-period capacity, the working weight read off the directly computed equilibrium equals (9) to $7.6\times10^{-8}$ in 14 cases ($n\in\{2,4\}$; held weights from $-0.5$ to 1.9; fixed, mixed and proportional prizes; $\beta$ from 0 to 0.6), and the sign rule holds in all 14, with exact ties at $(1-\beta)r=1$; with a growing prize the fixed-prize formula misses the computed intensity by up to 15% while the general form matches it to $3\times10^{-8}$, and with a proportional prize and $\theta_0=0.5$ the working weight is 0.5 at $r=1$, 0.549 at $r=0.7$ and 0.439 at $r=1.4$; with a fixed prize the same identity holds for held weights $-0.5$, 0, 0.5, 1 and 1.9 (LG). Independent checks: in the nonlinear stock model, with sharing, provision and diffusion, both closed-loop prices match simulation to a relative error of $2.5\times10^{-5}$ in five steady states and two balanced paths (2.213115 against 2.213115 with a fixed prize; 4.764587 against 4.764706, and 0.473684 against 0.473684, with a growing one); outside the domain ($\Gamma_\delta=1.066$) the simulated price is 226 against a formal value of $-16.2$, the cusp of Proposition 2.2(3) (S2; PD). With a fixed prize and a stationary total ($n=3$, $r=1.5$, $k_0=0.1$), $\Gamma=0.938$: equal standing is stable and the objective narrows. Where only gross winnings convert, the relative part of private cost is not amplified, and the working weight is narrower still: $-0.3125$ against $-0.1649$ with net payoff reinvested ($r=1$, $\theta_0=0$). Provision: with a fixed prize ($n=4$, $\theta_0=0$, $r=1$, $d=0.2$, $\delta_f=0.1$, $\delta=0.9$), lowering the won share from 1 to 0.5 raises $1+\theta_\Gamma$ from 0.5846 to 0.8058. With $n=4$ and a fixed prize, $\theta_\Gamma$ at $\theta_0=0$ is $-0.20$, $-0.43$, $-0.69$, $-0.87$ and $-0.99$ at $\Gamma_\delta=0.25$, 0.5, 0.75, 0.9 and 0.99, and at $\theta_0=2$ it is still $-0.08$ at $\Gamma_\delta=0.9$ (NB).

*Rivals who respond, and lottery prices.* In the deterministic share model, Proposition 2.2(4) supplies the local relative price calculation with the actor's own intensity held and its rivals at their equilibrium policies, including the additional payoff effects named there. A fixed prize makes the common return in prize winnings zero; it does not by itself make changes in equilibrium effort costs or shared losses zero. An extension of (9) to full strategic continuation payoffs therefore requires their common and relative derivatives and the same tail justification. That extension is not proved here. In the fixed-opponent lottery specified in Remark S2.2, the finite winner–loser continuation premium equals the structural expression for linear strength in the stock ($r=1$). It does not do so generally: at $r=1.5$, $\Gamma=0.6$, $d=0.2$, $\delta=0.97$, independent Bellman calculations give 1.03111 against the local expression 1.08178. Small-jump agreement is an approximation to test, and the responsive-rival lottery values that follow are numerical results of their Bellman equations, not a substitution into the deterministic theorem. In contests in which a lead discourages the follower, the discouragement effect of dynamic contests (Harris and Vickers 1987; Konrad and Kovenock 2009), the static contest's local derivative is amplified by exactly $2(n-1)/n$ (Remark S2.2(2)) [thm], which a two-period contest with effort reproduces [num: MP]. Separately, the finite-jump price at parity is amplified by about 1.05 to 7.9 times in the two-actor Markov-perfect contests computed in Remark S2.2(3): 1.05 to 3.15 times at $d=0.1$, 1.12 to 4.75 times at the paper's depreciation $d=0.2$, and 7.87 times under high patience ($\delta=0.97$). A laggard's price falls, at a quarter share to 57% and 25% of the structural value at $\Gamma=0.3$ and 0.6, and below zero with a shared loss, and it stops contesting standing (Remark S2.2(6)); a strong motive to catch up can reverse the win-share elasticity correction (Remark S2.2(5)), which does not determine the sign of the total price correction when the catch-up motive itself values standing. Where rounds are small, a winner-take-all equilibrium coexists with the smooth one, at about 12.5 and 8.8 times the structural price for $\Gamma=0.2$ and 0.3 (Remark S2.2(4)). All are held for outside review [num: MP].

*Bounds.* First order, in the deterministic share model, with constant reference coefficients and the discounted-tail condition for nonlinear continuation values. For the fixed-prize model, at $\Gamma_\delta\ge1$ the relative value has no finite derivative (Proposition 2.2(3)), so "where the loop gain is high" reads as the limit $\Gamma_\delta\to1^-$. Away from a steady state or a balanced path, as with a prize that has both a fixed and a growing part while capacity grows, the two returns vary over time and are not closed here; the two-period form covers that case. A myopic actor has $\lambda_{\rm rel}=\lambda_{\rm com}=0$ and acts on its held model, but selection still favours $\theta=-1$ (Proposition 3.6(3)).

*Narrowing without concentration.* In the fixed-prize model set $n=2$, $\kappa_c\Omega=1$, $d_K=0.2$, $r=1$, $\beta=0.5$, $k_0=\delta_f=0$ and $\delta=0.9$. Then $\Gamma=1/2<1$, $\Gamma_\delta=9/28$ and $\lambda_{\rm rel}=9/19>\lambda_{\rm com}=0$: the working objective narrows while equal standing is stable. With total capacity 5, the difference between the two stocks is multiplied exactly by 0.9 each period; from $(2.6,2.4)$ it falls from 0.2 to $5.31228\times10^{-6}$ in 100 periods. Thus the relative/common-return inequality does not define a concentrating contest. The equivalence in Proposition S3.2(3) is local to its proportional-prize, no-provision, no-diffusion case; even there local instability is distinct from a global concentration theorem [num: RP].

### Proof of Lemma 3.11

Under any partition into groups $g$, the Price equation gives $\bar w\Delta\bar z=\operatorname{Cov}_g(\bar w_g,\bar z_g)+\operatorname E_g\operatorname{Cov}_{j\in g}(w_j,z_j)+\operatorname E[w\Delta z]$. Let the trait cost its bearer $c>0$, give every member a benefit $b\bar z$ equal across members, and let the background payoff $\pi_0$ be the same for every carrier, so that $\pi_j=\pi_0+b\bar z-cz_j$ is the same decreasing function of $z_j$ for everyone. A payoff-monotone rule orders growth as payoff, so fitness is $w_j=\varphi(\pi_j)$ with $\varphi$ increasing on the payoffs present, and $w_j$ is a decreasing function of $z_j$ alone. By Chebyshev's sum inequality, $\operatorname{Cov}(w,z)\le0$ in the population and within every group. Where $\varphi$ is affine, $\bar w_g=\varphi(\pi_0+b\bar z-c\bar z_g)$ is decreasing in $\bar z_g$ and the between-group term is $-c\varphi'\operatorname{Var}_g(\bar z_g)\le0$. Where $\varphi$ is not affine, $\bar w_g$ depends on the spread of $z$ within the group as well as on its mean (Jensen), and the between-group term can be positive; the total is unchanged, since the decomposition is an identity, and the within-group terms absorb the difference. The trait can rise only if the transmission term is positive, by biased transmission, or if a rule changes the bearer's relative payoff. If the benefit goes only to the bearer's group, $\bar w_g$ contains $b\bar z_g$, and under the partition that matches its reach the between-group term can be positive. $\square$

*Numerics (PR).* With 600 individuals in 30 units and five partitions (one group, units, five blocs, fifty random groups, individuals), the between-group term under a global benefit is $0$, $-0.0019$, $-0.00002$, $-0.0011$ and $-0.0123$; under a benefit local to units it is $+0.0097$ at the unit partition. With nonlinear increasing fitness, over 3,000 random partitioned populations for each of six maps (affine, $e^{5\pi}$, $\log$, $\pi^3$, logistic, near-step), the total and every within-group term are positive in none; the between-group term is positive in none under affine fitness and in 3 to 346 of 3,000 under the nonlinear maps; in two reviewers' instances it is $+0.017$ with total $-0.364$, and $+0.0092$ with total $-0.115$. Biased transmission $\dot z=-cz(1-z)+\mu_{\rm tr}(1-z)$ settles at $\mu_{\rm tr}/c$. In an illustration with a base hazard of 0.03 a period, learning only from failures suffers an irreversible loss within 400 periods with probability 0, 0.14, 0.46 and 1.0 as the irreversible share of failures is 0, 0.05, 0.2 and 1, against 0, 0.06, 0.22 and 0.70 under adoption in advance: an illustration, not an estimate.

*Functional protection and cognitive understanding.* The lemma concerns the common benefit alone among otherwise alike carriers. It does not exclude a protective trait carried by a correlated local benefit: with trait $z\in\{0,1\}$ and payoff $\pi=\pi_0+(b_{\rm local}-c)z+b\bar z$, $b_{\rm local}>c$ makes the protective trait spread under replicator selection without any cognitive variable. For $b_{\rm local}=0.2$, $c=0.1$ and an initial trait share 0.1, the share is $\operatorname{logistic}(\operatorname{logit}(0.1)+0.1t)$, about 0.99959 at $t=100$ [num: RP]. An institution that protects the shared condition this way can satisfy Section 3.7's functional definition of knowing the system without its participants sharing an accurate model. What is ruled out is learning from the protected irreversible loss after it occurs; what is proposed, where other mechanisms do not suffice, is prior coordination through an accurate shared model. The stronger cognitive necessity is a strategic hypothesis, not a consequence of the Price decomposition.

### The five endgames

The corpus names five endgames of the dynamic [corpus: selfishness-spiral]: a freeze of mutual destruction; deadlock, a structure frozen by the consensus of the remaining players; a change of narrative by which the players come to see themselves as one whole and so gain the ability to cooperate; collapse; and winner takes all. Four are results of this paper: deterrence at saturation (Section 7.2), a captured configuration that cannot state what would end it (Section 4.5), the crossing results (Sections 3.2, 4.6 and 5.3) and the decisive winner (Section 5.2). The third is the transmission term of Lemma 3.11, and the purpose of this paper (Section 3.7).

### Proof of Proposition 3.12

(1) Contestants who weigh one another fully maximize $\Omega(\mathcal E)-c\mathcal E-n\ell\mathcal E$; the system subtracts $m_o\ell_o\mathcal E$ as well. At a positive club optimum $\Omega'(\mathcal E_c)=c+n\ell$; any $\mathcal E\ge\mathcal E_c$ has $\Omega'(\mathcal E)\le c+n\ell<c+n\ell+m_o\ell_o$, so strict concavity puts the system optimum strictly below $\mathcal E_c$. With $\Omega=10\sqrt{\mathcal E}$, $c=1$, $n=4$, $\ell=0.3$, $m_o=20$, $\ell_o=0.3$, the club's intensity is 5.17 against 0.37, and the harm to non-contestants 31.0 against 2.2 [num: CB]. (2) A bloc of $n_b$ equal members acting as one maximizes its members' summed payoff: it receives their summed prize share and bears the shared loss $n_b\ell$ per unit of total intensity, and with equal blocs the factor by which splitting effort among members changes a bloc's strength is common to all blocs and cancels. The game among the $n/n_b$ blocs is therefore the original game with $n/n_b$ players and loss $n_b\ell$. At $r=1$ the bloc intensities are 0.1563, 0.1389 and 0.1316 for two blocs of two, three blocs of two and two blocs of three, as that game predicts and the game with loss $\ell$ alone does not [num: SM(2)]. With unequal sizes and $r\ne1$, equal total effort gives a bloc of three $3^{1-r}$ times a single contestant's strength (1.732 at $r=0.5$, 0.577 at $r=1.5$). $\square$

## S4. Selecting the selectors

### Capture: the two directions, and what does not suffice (Definition 4.1)

The two directions act alike on selection and differ in what shows them. Accommodation shows in enforcement that softens: an exemption, a threshold set where the incumbent already stands, a discounted liability. A barrier needs no exemption: besides a compliance cost fixed per carrier (Section 4.1), a captured rule-maker can impose on all access conditioned on the incumbent's interfaces, or an obligation its lineage already meets. What marks the channel is appropriability, and it predicts positions as well as spending: a carrier supports a rule that raises every carrier's costs when the rule is keyed to it, and opposes correction keyed to no one even where that correction would burden its rivals more; support for such correction at net cost to itself refutes the channel (TESTS, Test B).

Neither hierarchy nor contraction is sufficient for the loop, though contraction can intensify it, since with less surplus to share relative position separates from everyone's absolute conditions.

### Proof of Proposition 4.3, and the effect of scope

Support keyed to the carrier obeys $\dot z^\chi_i=\varepsilon_i(\chi q\sum_j\hat b_{ij}y_j-d_iz^\chi_i)$ from zero; its horizon average is $z_i^{\chi\ast}L_i$ with $z_i^{\chi\ast}=\chi q\sum_j\hat b_{ij}y_j/d_i$ and $L_i=1-(1-e^{-\varepsilon_id_iT_h})/(\varepsilon_id_iT_h)$. Weighting the per-decision advantage $a_iz^\chi_i$ by $y_i$ and summing gives $\chi q\,y^\top My$, with $y=p\bar y$; shared support cancels. Differentiating gives the gradient, and under faithful transmission $\dot{\bar q}\propto\operatorname{Cov}(q,\Delta\pi(q))$ (Price 1970). With a floor $f>0$, $\Delta\pi'(0)>0$ iff $\chi p^2\bar y^\top M\bar y>fp^{\xi_f}$, that is, $p>p^\ast$, and for $p<p^\ast$ capacity loses for every $q>0$, since $\mathrm{Cost}$ is convex in $q$. The threshold is a square-root one when the floor is paid per carrier ($\xi_f=0$) and a linear one when it is paid per decision ($\xi_f=1$). The comparative statics follow from $c_iL_i\hat G_{ij}=a_i\hat b_{ij}\varepsilon_iT_h\,\ell_L(\varepsilon_id_iT_h)$ with $\ell_L(v)=[1-(1-e^{-v})/v]/v$ decreasing. $\square$

**Proposition S4.1 (Scope) [thm].** *If the carrier has share $p$ in each domain of a set $\mathcal S$, with $\hat G$ equal to $\hat g$ on the diagonal and $\hat\varphi$ off it and $c_iL_i=cL$, the return is $\chi cL\,p^2|\mathcal S|(\hat g+(|\mathcal S|-1)\hat\varphi)$: superlinear in $|\mathcal S|$ at fixed $p$ when $\hat\varphi>0$, and rising with scope at fixed total size only if $\hat\varphi>\hat g$.* (Sum over $\mathcal S$ and substitute $p=Y/|\mathcal S|$.)

*Relation to earlier work.* Influence over policy bought with contributions, in equilibrium with the policymaker's other aims, is the subject of protection for sale (Grossman and Helpman 1994); what is added is that the return to influence over correctors scales with the square of share and with its appropriability, so the capacity is selected in the largest carriers, and that spending can be classified by what the rule would do rather than by its stated aim (EV4). Construction of a shared resource is undermined by competition, and monopolizing the constructed niche restores the incentive to build (Krakauer, Page and Erwin 2009); Proposition 4.3 has the same appropriability logic, with the selection rule as the object modified, a field of domains, and the opposite valence. Dynamic models of constitutions show present decisions changing future decision rights (Acemoglu, Egorov and Sonin 2012); whether a model of that family reproduces Section 4 is open. Fixed-cost participation in lobbying (Bombardini 2008) is the empirical counterpart of the threshold. Appropriability plays the role relatedness plays in Hamilton's rule.

### Full statement and proof of Proposition 4.4

*Let carriers differ in an investment $u\ge0$, at private cost $C_u(u)$, that raises the correction rate $d$ for everyone. (1) Among otherwise identical carriers of the same practice, those investing more are selected against under any payoff-monotone rule. (2) Holding the host fixed, correction lowers the payoff of a carrier that holds rule support. (3) A challenger invests only if its own share of the benefit exceeds $C_u'(0)$, so dispersed challengers free-ride. (4) Correction keyed to the corrector yields a return to influence of the form of Proposition 4.3; whether it produces capture depends on whether row sums cross one. (5) If $\Upsilon>1$ and correction makes liability effective, an incumbent can gain from correction applied to all carriers, while correction applied to it alone costs it rent and share.*

Reputation or competition among institutions sustains independent correction only when contributions and outcomes are observed (Panchanathan and Boyd 2004; Sigmund et al. 2010). *Proof.* (1) Two carriers differing only in $u$ are affected identically by the change in $d$; their payoff difference is $C_u(u_l)-C_u(u_k)<0$ for $u_k>u_l$. (2) At fixed $V$ a carrier's support declines in $d$. (3) is the public-goods condition. (4) is immediate. (5) compares $\nu\bar e(\Upsilon-1)$ with the lost rent. $\square$

### Proof of Proposition 4.5

With $L_i=1$ and uniform $d$, $M=\hat A/d$ and (11) reads $\Delta\pi(q)=\chi q\hat Y/d-\mathrm{Cost}(q;p)$, whose gradient vanishes at $q^\ast=\big[(\chi\hat Y/d-fp^{\xi_f})_+/(\gamma p^{\xi_v})\big]^{1/\eta}$. Conversion into evaluation from the captured set is $A+a_{\rm ev}\hat Bq$, which gives (12). Write $G^{\rm QS}_{\rm ev}$ for the row sum at $q=q^\ast$.

*(1).* With $f=0$,
$$G^{\rm QS}_{\rm ev}=\frac{A}{c_{\rm ev}d}+Z_\eta\,(\gamma p^{\xi_v})^{-1/\eta}d^{-(1+\eta)/\eta},\qquad Z_\eta=a_{\rm ev}\hat B(\chi\hat Y)^{1/\eta}/c_{\rm ev},$$
strictly decreasing in $d$; with $f>0$ the cultivated term carries the further factor $(1-d/d_f)_+^{1/\eta}$ and is still decreasing. So evaluation is certified iff $d>d^\ast$, the unique root of $G^{\rm QS}_{\rm ev}=1$. At $d=A/c_{\rm ev}$ the first term alone equals one, so $d^\ast\ge A/c_{\rm ev}$, with equality iff $q^\ast(d^\ast)=0$. For $\eta=1$ and $f=0$, $d^\ast=\big(A+\sqrt{A^2+4c_{\rm ev}Z}\big)/(2c_{\rm ev})$ with $Z=a_{\rm ev}\hat B\chi\hat Y/(\gamma p^{\xi_v})$. Where cultivation is positive, the cultivated term rises with $\chi$, with $\hat Y$ and $\hat B$, and as $\gamma$ falls, and so does $d^\ast$. Size at a fixed profile raises $\hat Y$ quadratically and $\hat B$ linearly, against cost elasticities at most one. Scope needs its own comparison: at fixed size and cost factors, a rise in $\hat Y$ with no fall in $\hat B$ raises the requirement, but Proposition S4.1 shows that $\hat Y$ can fall when fixed total activity spreads across domains. Its sign depends on cross-domain conversion. For an explicit reversal, put a carrier's fixed total share 1 first in one occupied domain, then equally in two, with the occupied block of $\hat A$ equal to the identity and evaluation a separate unoccupied domain whose incoming coefficients both equal one. Hold $\chi=\gamma=\eta=c_{\rm ev}=a_{\rm ev}=1$, $A=f=0$. Then $\hat B=1$ stays fixed while $\hat Y$ falls from 1 to $1/2$; $q^\ast=\hat Y/d$ and $d^\ast=\sqrt{\hat Y}$ fall from 1 to $1/\sqrt2$. In Proposition S4.1's symmetric conversion pattern at fixed total size and fixed $\hat B$, broader scope raises the requirement only if off-diagonal conversion exceeds diagonal conversion [num: RP].

*(2).* Along $\gamma_0e^{-\alpha_{\rm ctl}t}$ and $d_0e^{\alpha_{\rm cor}t}$, the first term of $G^{\rm QS}_{\rm ev}$ is nonincreasing and the cultivated term is proportional to $e^{(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-(1+\eta)\alpha_{\rm cor})t/\eta}$, whatever $\xi_v$, since the size factor $p^{-\xi_v/\eta}$ is constant. *Sufficient half.* Under any adjustment that starts at or below $q^\ast$ and does not raise capacity while it is above $q^\ast$, $q(t)\le\max\{q(0),\sup_{s\le t}q^\ast(s)\}$: on any interval where $q$ exceeds that bound it would be above its selected level and rising. Since $d$ is nondecreasing, $(A+a_{\rm ev}\hat Bq^\ast(s))/(c_{\rm ev}d(t))\le G^{\rm QS}_{\rm ev}(s)$ for $s\le t$, so
$$G_{\rm ev}(t)\le\max\Big\{G_{\rm ev}(0),\ \sup_{s\le t}G^{\rm QS}_{\rm ev}(s)\Big\}\le G^{\rm QS}_{\rm ev}(0)<1$$
when $\alpha_{\rm cor}\ge\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$ and $d_0>d^\ast(\gamma_0)$. The rate condition does not repair a certificate invalid at the start. *Necessary half.* If $\liminf_tq/q^\ast>0$, then for some $s_0>0$, eventually $q\ge s_0q^\ast$, and the cultivated term is at least $s_0$ times the quasi-static one, which grows without bound below the rate law; so $G_{\rm ev}>1$ beyond a finite date. Once the captured set holds at least half its decisions, rule support in evaluation is driven by $\hat Bq\ge s_0\hat Bq^\ast$, which grows as $e^{(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-\alpha_{\rm cor})t/\eta}$; integrating (10) gives $a_{\rm ev}z_{\rm ev}(t)\ge c_1e^{(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-(1+\eta)\alpha_{\rm cor})t/\eta}$ for a constant $c_1>0$, so $\operatorname{logit}x_{\rm ev}\to\infty$ and a seeded evaluation domain is captured.

*(3).* $q^\ast>0$ iff $\chi\hat Y/d>fp^{\xi_f}$ iff $d<d_f$. With $f$ fixed, $d\ge d_f$ ends cultivation, and the requirement levels off at $\max\{A/c_{\rm ev},d_f\}$. Under $[H_f]$, $d_f\propto1/f$ rises at $\alpha_f$, and $d/d_f=(d_0/d_f(0))\,e^{(\alpha_{\rm cor}-\alpha_f)t}$. If $\alpha_{\rm cor}>\alpha_f$, cultivation ends at $T_f=\ln(d_f(0)/d_0)/(\alpha_{\rm cor}-\alpha_f)$, or never starts if $d_0\ge d_f(0)$, and afterwards $G_{\rm ev}=A/(c_{\rm ev}d)\to0$. If $\alpha_{\rm cor}=\alpha_f$, $d/d_f$ is constant: nothing is cultivated if $d_0\ge d_f(0)$, and otherwise $q^\ast$ is $(1-d_0/d_f(0))^{1/\eta}$ times its value without the floor, so (2) applies with that factor. If $\alpha_{\rm cor}<\alpha_f$, $d/d_f\to0$, the factor $(1-d/d_f)^{1/\eta}\to1$, and $q^\ast$ approaches its value without the floor. *The transient.* Before $T_f$ the cultivated term grows at $(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-(1+\eta)\alpha_{\rm cor})/\eta$, damped only by $(1-d/d_f)^{1/\eta}$; with $\alpha_{\rm cor}$ slightly above $\alpha_f$, $T_f$ is long and the row sum passes one long before cultivation ends (FF). *The lump sum.* With a cost $F_qp^{\xi_f}$ paid once if $q>0$, cultivation pays iff the maximized surplus $\frac{\eta}{1+\eta}(\chi\hat Y/d)^{(1+\eta)/\eta}(\gamma p^{\xi_v})^{-1/\eta}$ exceeds $F_qp^{\xi_f}$. With $\hat Y\propto p^2$ the size threshold is proportional to $F_q^{\eta/(2+2\eta-\xi_v-\eta\xi_f)}$, against $f^{1/(2-\xi_f)}$ for the per-unit floor. Once cultivation pays, $q^\ast$ is its value without any floor, and as $\gamma\to0$ the surplus grows without bound, so the lump sum stops binding and the requirement grows as it does without a floor.

*(4).* Parts of share $p/k$ have $\hat Y$ divided by $k^2$ and $p^{\xi_v}$ by $k^{\xi_v}$, so $q^\ast$ is divided by $k^{(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$; the $k$ parts together convert $p\,q^\ast_{\rm part}$, so total cultivated conversion is divided by the same factor, and the cultivated term reaches a given level later by $\Delta t=(2-\xi_v)\ln k/(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-(1+\eta)\alpha_{\rm cor})$. With $f>0$ fixed, parts below the threshold cultivate nothing. With $q_k^\ast\propto p_k^{(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$ and conversion proportional to $p_kq_k^\ast$, the total is proportional to $\sum_kp_k^{1+(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$. $\square$

*Further parts.* If AI lowers the unit cost of independent correction at rate $\alpha_{uc}$ and its budget grows at $\alpha_B$, then $\alpha_{\rm cor}=\alpha_B+\alpha_{uc}$, and eventual failure needs $\alpha_{\rm ctl}>(1+\eta)(\alpha_B+\alpha_{uc})$; correction keeps pace without budget growth once $\alpha_{uc}\ge\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$. With a finite horizon, while $\varepsilon_idT_h\ll1$ the required growth is about $\alpha_{\rm ctl}/\eta$, a pre-asymptotic interval. With concave conversion $\hat bq^{\xi_c}$, the required rate becomes $\xi_c\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$. The model implies that influence spending rises with share as $p^{\xi_v+(1+\eta)(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$, at least quadratically, which is testable (TESTS, Test B).

*Beyond the power law.* Let the marginal cost of capacity be $\gamma\,m(q)$ with $m$ increasing and regularly varying of index $\eta>0$, $m(q)=q^\eta\ell(q)$ with $\ell$ slowly varying. With $f=0$ the selected level solves $m(q^\ast)=\chi\hat Y/(\gamma d)=:x$, so $q^\ast=m^{-1}(x)$, which is regularly varying of index $1/\eta$ (an increasing function regularly varying of index $\eta>0$ has an inverse regularly varying of index $1/\eta$; Bingham, Goldie and Teugels 1987, §1.5). Along $\gamma_0e^{-\alpha_{\rm ctl}t}$ and $d_0e^{\alpha_{\rm cor}t}$, $x$ grows as $e^{(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-\alpha_{\rm cor})t}$, and the cultivated term $a_{\rm ev}\hat Bq^\ast/(c_{\rm ev}d)$ is $e^{(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-(1+\eta)\alpha_{\rm cor})t/\eta}$ times a slowly varying factor in $x$, which no exponential rate overcomes: above the rate law the term tends to zero and the certificate holds from some date on; below it the term grows without bound, and where capacity tracks its selected level the certificate fails beyond a finite date, as in (2). At equality the slowly varying factor decides: with $m(q)=q/\ln(e+q)$ ($\eta=1$) the cultivated term grows like the logarithm of $x$, so the sufficient half's $\ge$ does not carry over, and the condition is stated with strict inequalities. Unlike the power law, the term need not be nonincreasing above the rate law, so the certificate is claimed from some date on, not at every date. If $m$ rises faster than any power, as $m(q)=e^{q}$ does, $q^\ast=\ln x$ grows linearly in $t$ while $d$ grows exponentially, so correction growing at any positive rate certifies in the end, after a possible transient failure.

*Numerics (CS).* The size threshold at $f=0.05$ is 0.2236 per carrier and 0.0500 per decision, and 0.1717 and 0.0998 at $\xi_f=0.3$ and $0.7$. Two equal independent carriers give a quarter of the monopoly's cultivated conversion at $\xi_v=0$ and half at $\xi_v=1$; shares $(0.5,0.3,0.2)$ give 0.16 and 0.38 of the monopoly value. By log-log regression over $p\in[0.05,1]$, $q^\ast$, $pq^\ast$ and spending scale as $p^{(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$, $p^{1+(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$ and $p^{\xi_v+(1+\eta)(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$ at $\eta\in\{0.5,1,2\}$ and $\xi_v\in\{0,0.3,1\}$, to three decimals, and the exponents hold in 300 random settings; the splitting factor $k^{(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$ is reproduced for $k\in\{2,5\}$ at $\eta\in\{0.5,2\}$. With $\alpha_{\rm ctl}=0.2$ the rate law's threshold is 0.1333 at $\eta=0.5$ and 0.0667 at $\eta=2$, and on either side the cultivated term grows or decays at the predicted $\pm0.0600$ and $\pm0.0300$. With $\eta=1$, $\alpha_{\rm ctl}=0.2$, no correction growth and $k=10$, simulation gives split delays of 23.00 and 11.50 against formula values 23.03 and 11.51. A dominant share of 0.6 with $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.09$ crosses one for all three elasticities, and with $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.11$ decays. With $f$ fixed at 0.3 the monopoly crosses at 10.75 and ten parts never do; with $f$ falling at 0.05, the parts begin to cultivate $2\ln10/0.05=92.1$ later than the monopoly.

*Numerics (FF).* A falling floor can end cultivation below the rate law: with $\eta=p=\chi\hat Y=c_{\rm ev}=a_{\rm ev}\hat B=1$, $A=0$, $\gamma=e^{-0.2t}$, $d=2e^{0.05t}$ and $f=0.49e^{-0.01t}$, $q^\ast=[0.5e^{-0.05t}-0.49e^{-0.01t}]_+e^{0.2t}$ reaches zero at $t=\ln(0.5/0.49)/0.04=0.505$ and stays there, so the certificate never fails although $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.05$ is below the rate law of 0.1: the first case of (3). In the CS setting with $\alpha_f=0.05$, $d_f$ is 1.200, 1.978 and 3.262 at $t=0$, 10 and 20. From $f_0=0.1$ ($d_f(0)/d_0=1.80$): at $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.051$ cultivation ends at $t=588$, yet the certificate fails at $t=38.4$, with a peak row sum of $2\times10^{21}$, the transient of (3); at 0.06 it holds (peak 0.267), cultivation ending at $t=59$. At $\alpha_{\rm cor}=\alpha_f=0.05$ with cultivation at the start it fails, at $t=37.1$ from $f_0=0.1$ and at $t=58.0$ from $f_0=0.17$ ($d_f(0)/d_0=1.06$); from $f_0=0.3$ ($d_f(0)/d_0=0.60$), with nothing cultivated at the start, it never fails. An independent implementation predicts the eventual certificate by the three cases in 32 of 32 settings ($\alpha_f\in\{0.02,0.05\}$, eight values of $\alpha_{\rm cor}$, with and without cultivation at the start). The lump-sum threshold exponents computed by bisection on $p$, 0.2500, 0.5000, 0.3333 and 0.2500 at $(\xi_f,\xi_v,\eta)=(0,0,1),(1,1,1),(0,0,2),(0,1,0.5)$ in one implementation and 0.2500, 0.3333, 0.4000 and 0.2273 at $(0,0,1),(0,1,1),(0.5,0,2),(1,0.3,0.5)$ in another, equal $\eta/(2+2\eta-\xi_v-\eta\xi_f)$ in every setting. With $A=0.3$, $p=0.6$ and $\eta=1$, as $\gamma$ falls through $1$, $10^{-2}$, $10^{-4}$ and $10^{-6}$ the requirement $d^\ast$ is 0.529, 1.146, 1.199 and 1.200 with a per-unit floor $f=0.3$, levelling off at $\chi\hat Y/f=1.2$; 0.465, 4.65, 46.5 and 465 with a lump sum $F_q=0.3$; and 0.638, 4.80, 46.6 and 465 with no floor: the lump sum tracks the case without a floor.

### Capacity off its selected level

The rate law of Proposition 4.5(2) takes capacity at its selected level. Selection on capacity is itself a dynamic, and a joint model of capacity and the field shows where the quasi-static reading is conservative and where it is optimistic.

**Proposition S4.2 (The rate law off the selected level) [thm; num].** *Take the evaluation domain of (10) with row sum (12), $[H_\gamma]$, $f=0$ and $d=d_0e^{\alpha_{\rm cor}t}$ with $\alpha_{\rm cor}>0$, and let capacity $q$ adjust toward its selected level $q^\ast(t)$, which grows at $g_q=(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-\alpha_{\rm cor})/\eta$.*
1. *(Sufficiency under any adjustment.) If capacity starts at or below $q^\ast$ and does not rise while above it, the sufficient half of Proposition 4.5(2) holds whatever the speed of adjustment; while $q^\ast$ rises such capacity stays below it, so the quasi-static failure date is the earliest [thm].*
2. *(Necessity needs tracking.) Below the rate law the certificate fails beyond a finite time whenever $\liminf_tq/q^\ast>0$ [thm]. (a) Under adjustment at a constant rate $\kappa_q$, $\dot q=\kappa_q(q^\ast-q)$, $q/q^\ast\to\kappa_q/(\kappa_q+g_q)$ and failure is delayed, asymptotically, by $\eta\ln(1+g_q/\kappa_q)/(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-(1+\eta)\alpha_{\rm cor})$ [thm]. (b) Under gradual selection, $\dot q=V_q(q)\,\partial_q\Delta\pi$ with a selection variance $V_q(q)>0$, $\int_{q(0)}^{q(t)}dq/V_q(q)\le\chi\hat Y/(d_0\alpha_{\rm cor})$ at every date. Where that integral diverges as $q\to\infty$, as for constant variance or variance proportional to $q$, capacity is bounded and the certificate holds from some date on for every $\alpha_{\rm cor}>0$, after a possible transient failure [thm]. (c) Where the variance rises faster than capacity, as for variance proportional to $q^2$, capacity can grow without bound only if $\chi\hat Y/(d_0\alpha_{\rm cor})$ exceeds $\int_{q(0)}^\infty dq/V_q(q)$; fast selection then tracks the selected level and fails as the quasi-static model does, while slow selection stays bounded below the rate law [num].*
3. *(Reversal.) In a captured domain, a reform that raises $d$ for a hold and is then withdrawn must be held longer when capacity lags its selected level than the quasi-static model says: 6 to 14 per cent longer at the parameters computed, 17 to 54 per cent where the domain's conversion comes from its own share, and more as adjustment slows relative to $\varepsilon d$ [num].*

*Proof.* (1) is the sufficiency argument of Proposition 4.5(2); while $q^\ast$ rises and $q(0)\le q^\ast(0)$, such capacity satisfies $q\le q^\ast$, so $G_{\rm ev}\le G^{\rm QS}_{\rm ev}$ at every date. (2) The first claim is the necessity argument. (a) The linear equation has the particular solution $q=\kappa_qq^\ast/(\kappa_q+g_q)$, to which every solution converges; it scales the cultivated term by $\kappa_q/(\kappa_q+g_q)$, which shifts its exponential by $\ln(1+g_q/\kappa_q)$ at rate $(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-(1+\eta)\alpha_{\rm cor})/\eta$. (b) With $f=0$, $\partial_q\Delta\pi=\chi\hat Y/d-\gamma p^{\xi_v}q^\eta\le\chi\hat Y/d(t)$, so $\frac{d}{dt}\int_{q(0)}^{q(t)}dq/V_q\le\chi\hat Ye^{-\alpha_{\rm cor}t}/d_0$, and integrating gives the bound. For constant variance it reads $q(t)\le q(0)+V_q\chi\hat Y/(d_0\alpha_{\rm cor})$; for variance $v_qq$, $q(t)\le q(0)\exp\big(v_q\chi\hat Y/(d_0\alpha_{\rm cor})\big)$. Bounded capacity gives $G_{\rm ev}\le(A+a_{\rm ev}\hat Bq_{\max})/(c_{\rm ev}d)\to0$. (c) The bound is the same; near its selected level capacity relaxes at rate $V_q(q^\ast)\,\eta\gamma p^{\xi_v}q^{\ast\,\eta-1}=V_q(q^\ast)\,\eta\chi\hat Y/(dq^\ast)$, which for $V_q\propto q^2$ is proportional to $q^\ast/d$ and grows at $(\alpha_{\rm ctl}-(1+\eta)\alpha_{\rm cor})/\eta$ below the rate law, so capacity that reaches its selected level stays with it; whether it reaches it is the numerical part. (3) is numerical. $\square$

*Reading.* Gradual selection pushes capacity only by its marginal return $\chi\hat Y/d$, which growing correction lowers, while cheaper control merely removes the brake. Capacity that must keep leaping toward an optimum running away from it is closer to strategic choice than to gradual selection; the necessary half describes carriers that optimize, the case Section 4.3 designs a requirement against. TESTS Study 2's predicted failure signature changes accordingly: slow adaptation of capacity delays failure below the rate law and does not produce failure above it; a certificate that fails at a correction growth rate slightly above the rate law points to a slow variable that erodes correction itself, the case of Proposition 4.6.

*Numerics (QS).* In the CS setting ($\chi=\bar y^\top\hat A\bar y=\eta=1$, $\xi_v=0$, $p=0.6$, $A=0.3$, $a_{\rm ev}\sum_j\hat b_{\mathrm{ev},j}\bar y_j=c_{\rm ev}=1$, so that $a_{\rm ev}\hat B=p=0.6$, $d=2e^{\alpha_{\rm cor}t}$, $\gamma=e^{-0.2t}$, rate law 0.1; evaluation domain with $\zeta=1$, $\varepsilon=0.5$, $x_{\rm ev}(0)=0.01$), in two implementations that agree to within 0.1, the dates of certificate failure and of capture ($x_{\rm ev}>\tfrac12$) at $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.05$ are 28.8 and 44.9 with capacity at its selected level, 38.1 and 56.0 under adjustment at $\kappa_q=0.1$, 46.9 and 66.3 at $\kappa_q=0.03$, 28.9 and 45.0 under selection with variance proportional to $q^2$ (scaled to its starting level, speed 1), and never under constant-variance selection ($V_q=1$); failure at $\kappa_q=0.3$ comes at 32.9. At $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.09$ the pairs are 145.9/221.6, 183.0/267.7 at $\kappa_q=0.1$, 223.0 and no capture within 300 at $\kappa_q=0.03$, 146.2/222.0, and never; at 0.10 and above no dynamic fails or is captured. With correction not growing, the pair is 13.8/23.5 with capacity at its selected level, and capture follows failure by 8 to 60 periods across the dynamics. Simulated delays against the formula of (2)(a): 4.11/4.05, 9.29/9.16 and 18.11/17.92 at $\kappa_q=0.3,0.1,0.03$ for $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.05$; 8.40/8.41, 19.70/19.71 and 40.23/40.24 for 0.08. Along the path at $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.05$, $q/q^\ast$ settles at 0.87, 0.40 and 0.17 for $\kappa_q=1,0.1,0.03$, as $\kappa_q/(\kappa_q+g_q)$ with $g_q=0.15$ predicts, and falls toward zero under constant-variance selection. The bound of (2)(b) at $V_q=1$: $q(600)=7.29\le9.18$ at $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.02$ and $2.18\le3.78$ at $0.05$, with peak row sums 0.51 and 0.20. Within a horizon $H$ of 150, 300 and 600, the largest $\alpha_{\rm cor}$ at which evaluation is captured is 0.085, 0.093 and 0.096 with capacity at its selected level, approaching the rate law from below; 0.079, 0.090 and 0.095 at $\kappa_q=0.03$; 0.009 under constant-variance selection at $V_q=1$ and 0 at $V_q=0.1$; and 0.063 at every horizon under variance proportional to $q^2$ at speed 0.1, slow selection staying bounded below the rate law as (2)(c) says. A third implementation with its own parameters ($p=1$, $A=0$, $d_0=2$) agrees in every direction: at $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.05$ failure and capture at 13.0/25.4 with capacity at its selected level, 22.5/36.9 and 31.3/47.5 at $\kappa_q=0.1$ and 0.03, never under constant variance, and 23.8/58.9 under variance proportional to $q$, whose capacity then stays bounded, as (2)(b) says, and the certificate holds again by the end (final row sum $9\times10^{-8}$); at 0.09, 69.3/118.8, 106.4/169.9, 146.3/221.7, never and never; nothing fails at 0.10 or 0.12. Its lag delays are 9.42/9.16, 18.23/17.92 and 6.33/6.44 (simulated/formula), and under constant variance at $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.01$ the certificate fails transiently at $t=10.6$ and recovers to a row sum of $5\times10^{-8}$. A fourth, with variance proportional to $q^2$ at speeds 100, 1, 0.1 and 0.01, gives failure at 69.3 (the quasi-static date), 75.3, and none within 250 periods for the two slowest, at $\eta=1$ and $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.09$; at $\eta=2$ and $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.057$, at 69.1 (the quasi-static date), 71.6 and none. Across these runs capture follows the certificate's failure by 8 to 85 periods, longer as correction approaches the rate law. Five independent implementations agree in direction, and none fails earlier than capacity at its selected level. *Numerics for (3).* From a captured evaluation domain at $d_1=0.5$ (row sum 1.46), raising $d$ to $d_2$ for a hold and withdrawing it: the minimal hold is 94.8 with capacity at its selected level against 107.7 at $\kappa_q=0.03$ (14% longer) for $d_2=1.0$; 354 against 399 for $d_2=0.7$; 55.9 against 59.5 for $d_2=2.0$; adjustment at $\kappa_q\ge0.1$ changes the hold by under 4%. In a self-sustaining domain whose conversion comes from its own share, the hold is 38.05 with capacity at its selected level, 44.34 at $\kappa_q=0.1$ (17% longer) and 58.67 at $\kappa_q=0.03$ (54% longer).

### Proof of Proposition 4.6, and the hysteresis of release

*(1).* With $\mathcal M(t)=\mathcal M_0e^{-\delta_ht}$, $d_{\rm eff}(t)=d_0e^{(\alpha_{\rm cor}-w\delta_h)t}$. Carriers select capacity against the correction that acts, and the certificate is computed with it, so Proposition 4.5(2) applies to $d_{\rm eff}$ with growth rate $\alpha_{\rm cor}-w\delta_h$, and both halves hold with the threshold raised by $w\delta_h$; the start condition is unchanged because $\mathcal M(0)=\mathcal M_0$. Where delegation is partial, a share $D_h(t)\in[0,1]$ of corrective work, and capacity recovers at $\varrho_h$ where work is not delegated, $\dot{\mathcal M}=-\delta_hD_h\mathcal M+\varrho_h(1-D_h)(1-\mathcal M)$, $\mathcal M$ declines at a rate of at most $\delta_h$, so the shift is at most $w\delta_h$, approached as $D_h\to1$. If erosion stops at $\mathcal M_{\min}>0$, $d_{\rm eff}$ tends to $d(\mathcal M_{\min}/\mathcal M_0)^w$: the rate condition is unchanged, and the level $d$ must exceed the requirement by the factor $(\mathcal M_0/\mathcal M_{\min})^w$. $\square$

*(2), the mechanism.* Captured evaluation reports that delegation is safe; delegation rises; human capacity erodes; correction weakens; capture deepens. This is a second positive loop, through human capacity, beside the conversion loop of (10), and it makes capture bistable over a band of correction rates: a domain that is not captured stays uncaptured at rates at which a captured one stays captured.

*Numerics (QS).* With $w=1$ and pure erosion in the third implementation above, the eventual certificate holds at $\alpha_{\rm cor}\in\{0.15,0.2,0.22\}$ and fails at $\{0.1,0.12\}$ for $\delta_h=0.05$, and holds at $\{0.2,0.22\}$ and fails at $\{0.1,0.12,0.15\}$ for $\delta_h=0.1$: the predicted thresholds 0.15 and 0.20. With partial delegation rising as $D_h(t)=1-0.8e^{-0.1t}$, $w=1$ and the CS parameters, the largest $\alpha_{\rm cor}$ at which evaluation is still captured by $t=600$ rises from 0.096 without the coupling to 0.145, 0.190 and 0.231 at $(\delta_h,\varrho_h)=(0.05,0.05)$, $(0.1,0.05)$ and $(0.2,0.1)$: near $0.1+\delta_h$, and below it within a finite horizon and where capacity recovers while not delegated. For (2), in one domain with a constant cost of control and capacity at its selected level, so as to isolate the loop through $\mathcal M$, with $\delta_h=0.2$, $\varrho_h=0.05$, $w=\tfrac12$ and delegation $D_h=0.1+k_Dx_{\rm ev}$: the smallest permanent correction rate that undoes capture from the captured state is 0.634 at $k_D=0$, matching the 0.638 above which the certificate holds without the loop; 1.006 at $k_D=0.3$; and 1.675 at $k_D=0.6$, where captured human capacity is 0.40 and 0.14 of its uncaptured level. These are 1.6 and 2.6 times the certificate rate. The form $d_{\rm eff}=d(\mathcal M/\mathcal M_0)^w$ is a modelling choice inside the open interface "Human capacity and correction" (S13).

### Mobilization (Remark 4.7)

Let those harmed mobilize in proportion to perceived harm, $d=d_0+k_B(1-\tilde V)$. Since $1-\tilde V=(1-m)(1-V)$, masking attenuates the feedback by $1-m$ exactly when it is needed, and even with accurate reports $d\le d_0+k_B$, which cannot keep a certificate against a requirement that rises without bound. After a recovery, mobilization falls back to $d_0$ and the certificate can fail again: a regulatory cycle. With a per-unit floor that AI does not lower, enough gain and speed can keep the certificate over a stated interval, since the requirement then levels off (Proposition 4.5(3)).

### Proof of Proposition 4.8, and the late alarm

$\tilde V$ increases in $V$ and equals $V_{\rm alarm}$ at $V=(V_{\rm alarm}-m)/(1-m)$. For $V_{\min}\le V\le V_{\rm alarm}$ and $m>m_{\rm fl}$, $\tilde V\ge V_{\min}+m(1-V_{\min})>V_{\rm alarm}$. At $V^\ast$, $\tilde V\ge V^\ast+\bar m(1-V^\ast)>V_{\rm alarm}$. $\square$ If $V\to0$ and eventually $m\le m_1<V_{\rm alarm}$, the alarm fires. If $V$ declines from $V_{\rm alarm}$ as $\dot V=-k_VV$ with constant $m<V_{\rm alarm}$, the alarm is delayed by $k_V^{-1}\ln[(1-m)V_{\rm alarm}/(V_{\rm alarm}-m)]$, which diverges as $m\uparrow V_{\rm alarm}$.

### Remark 4.9 in full

(1) With an upper bound $m_{\rm in}$ known, an alarm on reports at $V_{\rm target}+m_{\rm in}(1-V_{\rm target})$ fires no later than the true condition reaches $V_{\rm target}$, for every $m\le m_{\rm in}$. (2) A bound estimated inside the evaluated lineage is itself masked. (3) For masking by the evaluated model, a bound estimated by evaluators independent in funding and appointment is still a reading of the model's behaviour, which the model can shape (evaluation awareness, sandbagging); the bound must come from channels that behaviour controls less directly, each with a limit of its own: weights and activations, which linear probes read for strategic deception though not yet robustly (Goldowsky-Dill et al. 2025), which optimization against a probe can obfuscate (Bailey et al. 2024), and which a model fine-tuned to evade activation monitors can hide even from monitors it has not seen (McGuinness et al. 2025); audits of training data; and elicitation by fine-tuning, which recovers capability that a password-locked model withholds under prompting given good demonstrations, and may fail where the hidden capability exceeds the demonstrators' (Greenblatt, Roger, Krasheninnikov and Krueger 2024). How far these channels bound masking at a given capability is crux C12. (4) Where models evaluate models, through AI feedback, model-written evaluations and models as judges or monitors, $x_{\rm ev}$ is the share of that evaluation done by the evaluated lineage and can be read from who built the evaluator (EV4, EV6); checks of the same lineage add a common cause of their own (Section 6.5). (5) A disjunctive alarm, firing on any credible report, fails silently only if $m_{\rm out}>m_{\rm fl}$. (6) Static, memoryless affine masking hides levels, not dynamics: for $\tilde V=m+(1-m)V$ the correlation coefficient of reports is that of $V$, exactly, so the lag-one autocorrelation of reports is unchanged and the host's recovery rate can still be read. *Numerics (AC):* on a stochastic logistic host with load 0.4 and true condition 0.36, masking at $m=0,0.25,0.5$ gives mean reports 0.358, 0.519 and 0.679 with the same lag-one autocorrelation, 0.9814, from which the condition recovered is 0.376; a report filter with memory (exponential smoothing at 0.8) drives the recovered value to 0.042, and the indicator fails. It also needs long series, and once used as an alarm it becomes a target; faking dynamics is nevertheless costlier than faking levels, and the indicator can be cross-checked against channels that observe actions directly.

### The field model

**Lemma S4.3 (The field is cooperative) [thm].** *With $N_D$ domains, $[0,1]^{N_D}\times[0,\infty)^{N_D}$ is forward invariant under (10), $\limsup_tz_i\le\sum_jb_{ij}/d_i$, and the system is cooperative in the sense of Smith (1995), so its flow preserves the componentwise order (Hirsch 1985).*

*Proof of Proposition 4.10.* (1) At $Z_{\mathcal C}$ the Jacobian is block lower-triangular, with diagonal entries $\mp\zeta_ic_i(G_i(\mathcal C)-1)$ and $-\varepsilon_id_i$. (2) By the lemma, eventually $a_iz_i-c_i\le c_i(\sum_jG_{ij}-1)/2<0$. (3) Take $G_{i1}>1$ for $i\ne1$, $G_{12}>1$, and other off-diagonal gains zero. (4) Pruning, by comparison. $\square$ Part (2) is sufficient and not necessary: with a row sum of two and a start at $(0.1,0.1)$ the state stays in $[0,0.1]\times[0,0.2]$ and converges to no capture (FA; S14).

*Proof of Proposition 4.11.* Comparison and pruning; the re-seeding case is the cascade from the domains that remain captured. **Cascades.** If $x_j\to1$ on a seed, $x_i\to1$ on its cascade hull, the closure of $\mathcal S_{k+1}=\mathcal S_k\cup\{i:x_i(0)>0,\ G_i(\mathcal S_k)>1\}$ (Kempe, Kleinberg and Tardos 2003; Morris 2000), by induction using a lower bound on rule support.

*The keystone example.* In the first version of this model, a liability reform at development unwound capture in every domain while development was a keystone; once the other domains' mutual conversion rose to 1.1, capture survived the complete decoupling of development.

*Proof of Proposition 4.12.* The frozen share drives $z\to b_px_p/d$. The equilibria of the released system are $(0,0)$, $(1,b/d)$ and the saddle $(1/G,c/a)$. The function $axz-cx-\frac{ad}{2b}z^2$ is nondecreasing along solutions, so every solution converges to an equilibrium and the stable manifold of the saddle separates the basins. With ordinary rule-making the release point $(x_p,bx_p/d)$ lies on the nullcline through the saddle, which decides by $x_p$ against $1/G$; with rule-making insulated the release point is $(x_p,0)$, and $x_\sigma>1/G$ by strong cooperativity. $\square$ *The length of an insulated pause.* By cooperativity the basin of absence is a down-set, bounded by the separatrix. With rule-making insulated, rule support decays from its captured level $b/d$ as $(b/d)e^{-\varepsilon dt}$ while the share stays at $x_p$; at release the state is in the basin of absence iff it lies below the separatrix, whose height at $x_p$ is $S(x_p)$ in units of $c/a$. In those units the captured level is $G$, so the pause must last $\ln\big(G/S(x_p)\big)/(\varepsilon d)$: zero where $S(x_p)\ge G$, $\ln G/(\varepsilon d)$ at $x_p=1/G$, where $S=1$, and no finite length where $x_p\ge x_\sigma$. A frozen share accumulates no lock-in; the hold of a reform that insulates rule-making while shares keep moving is S11's. *Numerics (DW):* by forward integration in logit coordinates, $x_\sigma$ is 0.676 when rule-making is as fast as adoption with $G=2$ and 0.984 when it is ten times slower; 0.321 and 0.775 at $G=5$; a second method, integrating the separatrix backward, agrees. In units of $1/(\varepsilon d)$, with $G=2$ and equal speeds, $S=3.92$, 2.15, 1.00 and 0.45 at $x_p=0.1$, 0.3, 0.5 and 0.6, so the pause is zero from $x_p=0.1$ and $0.3$, $0.69=\ln2$ from $0.5=1/G$ and 1.50 from 0.6, and no finite length from 0.676; with rule-making ten times slower it is 0.24, 0.48, 0.69, 0.81, 1.17 and 2.08 at $x_p=0.1$, 0.3, 0.5, 0.6, 0.8 and 0.95; with $G=5$ and equal speeds, 0.82 from $x_p=0.1$, $1.61=\ln5$ from 0.2, 3.45 from 0.3, and no finite length from 0.321. This result also appears as Proposition 10 of the author's paper of 28 September 2026 (LEDGER records the overlap; attribution is the author's to settle).

*What the field model cannot show.* It is cooperative, so it has no attracting cycles and describes lock-in, not reform cycles; a delayed, masked negative loop from the host to correction could produce cycles and is not modelled. The mobilization of Remark 4.7 is one such loop, and a delayed balancing channel is treated in S2. *Recovering the parameters.* Integrating (10) gives $\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{logit}x_i=\zeta_ic_i[\sum_jG_{ij}(k_i*x_j)(t)-1]$ plus a decaying transient, with kernel $k_i(s)=\varepsilon_id_ie^{-\varepsilon_id_is}$; series for the shares identify the conversions. This is recoverability, not causal identification.

### Proof of Proposition 4.13, and the two clocks

By (a) and Proposition 3.4, on every alarm-free path the load tends to $\bar e$ and $\limsup_tV\le(1-h_V\bar e/\rho)^+<V_{\min}$ under (reach). By (e), $m(t)>m_{\rm fl}$ throughout the band $[V_{\min},V_{\rm alarm}]$, so by Proposition 4.8 no alarm fires there, and the host passes below $V_{\min}$ on an alarm-free path. If $m_{\rm in}\le m_{\rm fl}$, then $m\le m_{\rm fl}$ and the alarm fires at some $V\ge V_{\min}$, later than without masking. *The clock form.* By (b), (c) and Proposition 4.5(2), with its part (3) where the floor falls, the certificate fails and $x_{\rm ev}\to1$, so after a finite date $T_{\rm cap}$ the share $x_{\rm ev}$ stays above the threshold of (e). If $T_{\rm cap}<T_A$, every date at which $V$ lies in the band is later than $T_{\rm cap}$, so (e) holds and the first part applies. $\square$ The load is at most $\bar e$, so by comparison the host declines no faster than under full load; when $h_V\bar e<\rho$ it cannot reach the alarm before $(\rho-h_V\bar e)^{-1}\ln[(1-V^\ast/V_0)/(1-V^\ast/V_{\rm alarm})]$, with $V^\ast=1-h_V\bar e/\rho<V_{\rm alarm}$, a lower bound on $T_A$; in every case $T_A$ is finite under (reach). Conditions (b) and (c) say nothing about $T_A$, which is why they make (e) a condition to check rather than a consequence.

*Numerics (SR).* Two computations show the clocks deciding. With capture set from outside: a host $\dot V=V(\rho(1-V)-h_V)$ under full race load from $V_0=1$, with $\rho=0.05$, $h_V=0.08$, floor 0.3, alarm 0.6, $m_{\rm in}=0.5$ and $m_{\rm out}=0$, so $m_{\rm fl}=0.43$ and (e) needs $x_{\rm ev}>0.857$, crosses the floor at $t=21.0$. If evaluation is captured along a logistic of width 2 centred at $t=0$, the threshold is passed at $t=3.6$, no alarm fires and the crossing is silent; centred at $t=20$ or $t=45$, the threshold is passed at $t=23.6$ or $48.6$ and the alarm fires at $t=7.4$, before the floor, although $x_{\rm ev}\to1$ in every case: (b) and (c) without (e). With capture endogenous in the joint model (the CS parameters, a race share rising from 0.05 at rate 0.12, the same host, floor and alarm): with correction not growing, evaluation is captured near $t=23.5$ when capacity is at its selected level; independent evaluation raises the alarm at $t=32.1$ with $V=0.58$, while capacity at its selected level or adjusting at $\kappa_q=1$ and 0.1 delays the alarm to $t=58.8$ with $V=0.20$, after the floor; capacity adjusting at $\kappa_q=0.03$, or under constant-variance selection, leaves evaluation uncaptured when the alarm is due, and it fires at $t=32.1$. With correction growing at 0.05, half the rate law, capture comes too late under every dynamic and the alarm fires at $t=32.1$. Whether capture precedes evidence thus depends on the time constant of capacity as well as on the rate law, and acceleration of the contest moves both clocks.

## S5. Acceleration and the three erosions

This chapter holds the full statements of the results that Section 5 carries in a sentence, the proofs of those it states, and the numbers behind every figure it quotes. Local symbols: $w_p=\delta(1-p_d)/(1-\delta)$ is the weight of the future that a deviation leaves open; $A$, $B$ and $K$ are the stage constants of the binary game of Proposition 6.1, defined at Remark S5.1; $\tau_{\rm det}$ is the mean time to detect and answer a deviation. $\Lambda^\ast$ keeps its meaning, the gap factor of Remark S2.1.

### Proposition 5.1 in full, and proof

*(1) In the objective an actor acts on, if $S_i<P_i$, restraint is never a best response to a racing rival; in the contest of Proposition 3.6 this holds for $r<n/(n-1)$, and in the $n$-player game it also needs restraint against one racer to pay less than racing alongside it. (2) A best response in the objective does not imply $S<P$ in material payoff or true welfare: with $n=2$, $r=1$, $\Omega=c=1$, $\ell=0.1$ and $\theta=-1$, racing is the strict best response in the objective, while staying out gives $S^\pi=-0.05$ against $P^\pi=-0.10$, and with $\kappa=0.2$, $S^W=+0.04$ against $P^W=-0.12$. (3) If falling behind costs $L_b\ge0$ beyond losing the prize, joining instead of staying out gains, at $\theta=0$, $\frac{\Omega+L_b}{n}\big(1-\frac{r(n-1)}{n}\big)$ for $r\le n/(n-1)$, zero in the fully dissipative limit; with asymmetric costs $c_1<c_2$ the strongest keeps a rent $(\Omega+L_b)(1-c_1/c_2)$ (Hillman and Riley 1989; Baye, Kovenock and de Vries 1996). (4) If the events $T_i>R_i$ are independent with probabilities $\xi_i$, the race begins with probability $1-\prod_i(1-\xi_i)$, which tends to one iff $\sum_i\xi_i$ diverges.*

*Proof.* (2): at $\theta=-1$ intensity is $1/2$ and $P^\pi=-0.1$; against $e=1/2$ zero intensity gives $-0.05$ and the other $0.45$. (3): with penalty $L_b$ the contest has prize $\Omega+L_b$; joining minus staying out is $(\Omega+L_b)/n-(c+\ell)e$ with $e=r(n-1)(\Omega+L_b)/(n^2(c+\ell))$; in the symmetric all-pay contest each player's expected payoff is that of the lowest bid in its support, $-L_b$ (Baye, Kovenock and de Vries 1996), as for two actors with $r\ge2$ (Ewerhart 2015). (4): $\prod(1-\xi_i)\to0$ iff $\sum\xi_i=\infty$. $\square$

### Proof of Proposition 5.2

At the symmetric equilibrium the win probabilities sum to one, so total material payoff is $\Omega-ne(c+n\ell)$; substituting $e(\theta)$ from Proposition 3.6(1) gives $s_{\rm dis}$. With stakes $s\Omega$ and loss $s\ell$ the share is $r(n-1-\theta)(c+ns\ell)/(n(c+(1+\theta)s\ell))$, whose derivative in $s$ has the sign of $c\ell(n-1-\theta)>0$, with limit $r(n-1-\theta)/(1+\theta)$. Total payoff stays positive at every scale iff the limit is at most one, $r(n-1-\theta)\le1+\theta$, that is, $\theta\ge\theta_g$. At $\theta=-1$ the share is $r(1+ns\ell/c)$. $\square$

For $n=4$ and $r=1$ the ceiling $r(n-1-\theta)/(1+\theta)$ is 3, 1.86, 1 and 0.33 at $\theta=0$, 0.4, 1 and 2, and $\theta_g=1$; with $\ell=0.3c$ the share at unit scale is 1.27 at $\theta=0$ and 1.007 at $\theta=0.4$, and it rises toward each ceiling as the scale grows [num: legacy v5]. At $\theta=0$ each actor's loss relative to joint restraint is $\frac{r(n-1)}n\frac\Omega n\frac{c+n\ell}{c+\ell}$, which exceeds $\Omega/n$ iff $r(n-1)(c+n\ell)>n(c+\ell)$: $1.27\,\Omega/n$ in that example. If the shared loss per unit did not grow with power, the share would stay at its unit-scale value.

### Remark 5.3 in full

A unit of capacity can do one of three things. It can expand into an unclaimed frontier at marginal value $\varphi(F)-c_x-(1+\theta)H/n$, where $\varphi$ falls as the remaining frontier $F$ shrinks, $c_x$ is the private cost and $H$ the cost to the host, borne equally (Proposition 3.5(5)). It can destroy rivals' capacity at value $-c_y-\theta\Delta_y/(n-1)$, where $c_y$ is the private cost and $\Delta_y$ the capacity destroyed. Or it can rest. The symbols are local to this remark.
1. *If $\theta\ge-(n-1)c_y/\Delta_y$, war among copies never pays.*
2. *At $\theta=-1$ expansion stops only where $\varphi=c_x$; at $\theta>-1$ it stops at $\varphi=c_x+(1+\theta)H/n$, the point of enough.*
3. *At $\theta=-1$ destruction is chosen iff its value beats both rest and expansion, $\Delta_y/(n-1)-c_y>\max\{0,\varphi(F)-c_x\}$; so where $\Delta_y/(n-1)>c_y$, expansion gives way to destruction once $\varphi(F)<c_x+\Delta_y/(n-1)-c_y$, before the frontier is exhausted if $\varphi$ crosses that level while $F>0$. Where $\Delta_y/(n-1)\le c_y$, rest follows expansion and no accounting fights (with $n=4$, $c_y=\Delta_y=1$, $\varphi=0.1$, $c_x=1.1$, destruction beats expansion, $-2/3>-1$, and rest beats both).*

*Proof.* Each clause compares the three marginal values. For $\theta\ge-(n-1)c_y/\Delta_y$ the value of destruction is not positive, which gives (1). At $\theta=-1$ the host term vanishes from the value of expansion, which gives (2); destruction is then worth $\Delta_y/(n-1)-c_y$, and it is preferred to expansion once $\varphi-c_x$ falls below that, which gives (3). $\square$

*Toy.* Four copies of one design with compounding expansion, a frontier of 1,000 and a logistic host. At $\theta=-1$ expansion peaks at a stock of 494 near $t=25$; the switch comes with 32% of the frontier left (predicted by (3) at 322, simulated at 320), and total stock then falls to about $4\times10^{-9}$. At $\theta=0$ the copies stop at the point of enough, keep a stock of 518 and never fight; at $\theta=3$ they stop earlier, at 343. With asymmetric starts one survivor keeps 17% of the peak. With destruction too costly to pay, no accounting fights, but $\theta=-1$ drives the host to 0.109. One result runs against the narrative: in the run with war, the host's minimum under $\theta=-1$ (0.245) is higher than under $\theta=0$ (0.194), because capacity spent on war is not spent on the host (S14) [num: FS]. The parameters were chosen by the author; the toy illustrates the static condition and estimates nothing (compare Hirshleifer 1991; Skaperdas 1992).

The corpus reaches the switch from the other side: a logic organised as outward struggle does not stop when outward space runs out but turns its means on whatever is left, and in one of two endgames fighting goes on until everything within reach is eaten and then continues inside, as a zero-sum game [corpus: meta-attractor, 2026-10-01 and 2026-10-04].

### Proof of Proposition 5.4

Cooperation is worth $C_i/(1-\delta)$. A one-period deviation $a$ is worth $C_i+g_i(a)+\delta\big[p_d(a)D_i(a)/(1-\delta)+(1-p_d(a))v_i/(1-\delta)\big]$, where $v_i/(1-\delta)$ is the continuation value of punishment computed in the same game, with breakouts possible during punishment. Multiplying the no-deviation condition by $1-\delta$ and rearranging gives (14) for that deviation. In this stochastic game with an absorbing state, discounting and bounded payoffs, the one-shot deviation principle holds, and it holds deviation by deviation: (1) is the no-gain condition for each $a$ with its own $(g_i(a),p_d(a),D_i(a))$, which is why the deviation with the largest gain need not bind; when $p_d$ and $D_i$ are common to all deviations, the one with the largest gain binds. That $a^\ast$ is the path of some subgame-perfect equilibrium needs $v_i$ to be the least subgame-perfect continuation value, the extension of optimal penal codes (Abreu 1988) to stochastic games with an absorbing state; this is asserted for finite stage games with discounting and flagged for outside review.

(2) Solve (1) for $p_d$. With $\alpha=(1-\delta)g_i/\delta$, $b=D_i-C_i$ and $c=C_i-v_i$, $\bar p_{d,i}=(c-\alpha)/(b+c)$, where $b+c=D_i-v_i>0$. Its derivatives are $-1/(b+c)$ in $\alpha$, $-\bar p_{d,i}/(b+c)$ in $b$ and $(1-\bar p_{d,i})/(b+c)$ in $c$, so on $[0,1)$ it falls with $g_i$ and with $v_i$, falls with $D_i$ except at $\bar p_{d,i}=0$, where the derivative is zero, and rises with $C_i$, whose derivative is $1/(b+c)$. Scaling $(g_i,C_i,D_i,v_i)$ alike scales $\alpha$, $b$ and $c$ alike and leaves the ratio unchanged [num: NB; ES]. (3) The two bounds differ by $\delta p_d(D_i-C_i)$. (4) Set $p_d=1$; with $D_i\ge C_i$ the condition forces $g_i\le-\delta(D_i-C_i)/(1-\delta)\le0$. (5) With at most one breakout a period, the three events of a punishment period, $i$ breaks out, the other breaks out, neither, have probabilities $q_b$, $q_b$ and $1-2q_b$, which needs $q_b\le\frac12$; the punishment value satisfies $V_P=P_i+\delta\big[q_bD_i/(1-\delta)+q_bV^{\rm sub}_i/(1-\delta)+(1-2q_b)V_P\big]$, and $v_i=(1-\delta)V_P$ is the closed form. With independent breakouts each racer breaks out alone with probability $q_b(1-q_b)$, and the race continues with probability $q_b^2+(1-q_b)^2$, since a simultaneous breakout leaves it as it was; this gives the same closed form in $q_b(1-q_b)$, valid for every $q_b\in[0,1]$. And $v_i>P_i$ iff $D_i+V^{\rm sub}_i>2P_i$. $\square$

*The example of (5).* With $C_i=1$, $P_i=0$, $D_i=4$, $V^{\rm sub}_i=0$, $g_i=2$ and $\delta=0.9$, restraint is enforceable at $q_b=0$. A deviation that never breaks out is deterred iff $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-v_i)$, that is $v_i\le0.7778$; with $v_i(q_b)=3.6q_b/(0.1+1.8q_b)$ this holds iff $q_b\le0.0354$, and $v_i<C_i$ iff $q_b<0.0556$. So restraint fails for every $q_b$ in $(0.0354,0.0556)$ while still $v_i<C_i$, with $v_i=0.895$ at $q_b=0.045$ [num: ES].

*Numerics (ES; EC).*
- *Per deviation.* With $C=3$, $D=6$, $v=1$ and $\delta=0.9$, deviation X ($g=1$, $p_d=0.1$) is deterred, its value 17.5 against 30 for cooperation (temptation 0.37 against a remaining sanction of 1.62), while deviation Y ($g=0.2$, $p_d=0.6$) is not, 39.2 against 30 (temptation 1.64 against 0.72) [num: ES].
- *Breakouts during punishment.* The closed forms equal value iteration on the explicit chain, exclusive ($v=0.8950$, 1.2857 and 1.6875 at $q_b=0.045$, 0.1 and 0.3) and independent (0.8723, 1.2366 and 1.5816). At $q_b=0.6$ the exclusive form is invalid ($1-2q_b<0$) and the independent one gives 1.6241. The window of the example is not a knife edge: deterrence fails while $v<C$ on $[0.0498,0.0555]$, $[0.0354,0.0555]$ and $[0.0159,0.0555]$ for $g=0.5$, 2 and 5 on a grid of $q_b$, and racing in the punishment phase is a best response in the cases checked, so the punishment is credible [num: ES].
- *Best response over plans.* In 20,000 random settings of an explicit-action stochastic game in which one breakout technology sets both the deviation's $p_d$ and the punishment's $q_b$, player 1's optimum over all deviation plans is computed by value iteration on the decision process. Cooperation is sustained in 6,821. In 35 settings the punisher prefers to quit the race, so part (5) does not give the credible punishment value and the proposition's premise fails; in the other 19,965, (14), evaluated with $v$ from part (5), agrees with the best response in every case [num: ES].
- *Valuation check.* Comparing the discounted value of the fixed deviation path with cooperation in 200,000 random settings, a third each with $D<C$, $D=C$ and $D>C$, reproduces part (1) in every case. Since that comparison is (14) rearranged, as exact rational arithmetic confirms (no disagreement in 20,000 draws), it checks the algebra and not the incentives [identity: EC; ES]. The bound without the breakout term says "sustainable" when deviation pays in 10,723 of 66,538 cases with $D>C$ and in none with $D\le C$, and says "not sustainable" when restraint is sustainable in 10,032 of 66,842 cases with $D<C$. Both of Codex's V6.0 counterexamples are reproduced (cooperation worth 30, deviation 22) [identity: EC].
- *A different point.* With $C=1$, $D=4$, $V^{\rm sub}=0$, $g=0.5$ and $q_b=0.1$, the punishment value rises to 1.29, above $C$, and 1.78 at $q_b=0.45$; restraint is not sustainable even at $p_d=0$. There both actors prefer the race with breakouts to cooperation itself: what fails is the preference for cooperation, not deterrence, which is why the example of (5) replaces this one. Where $D+V^{\rm sub}<2P$ breakouts harden the punishment instead: with $D=3$ and $V^{\rm sub}=-5$, $v=-0.64$ at $q_b=0.1$ [num: EC; ES].
- *Homogeneity.* $\bar p_d=0.2963$ is unchanged when all of $(g,C,D,v)$ are scaled by 2 or 10; scaling only $g$ and $D$ moves it to 0.1296 and $-0.0037$ [num: ES].

### Derivation of Remark 5.5

Let detection and response arrive at a random time $\mathcal T$ after the deviation, with $\upsilon(T)=\Pr(\mathcal T\le T)$, and let the deviation become decisive at $T_{\rm dec}$ unless it has been answered. It escapes punishment iff $\mathcal T>T_{\rm dec}$, so $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$. In the one-period reduction, which places the resolution in the period after the deviation and reads $D_i$ and $v_i$ as normalized continuation values conditional on escape and on timely detection, each including the periods before resolution, substituting in (14) and solving as in Proposition 5.4(2) gives $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})\ge1-\bar p_{d,i}$. A memoryless clock of mean $\tau_{\rm det}$ has $\upsilon(T)=1-e^{-T/\tau_{\rm det}}$. Along the compounding route $T_{\rm dec}$ is the time of Remark S2.1, which a structural loop gain above one shortens, so detection that is timely today stops being timely as leads open faster.

*Exact timing.* With $T_{\rm dec}>1$ the periods before resolution carry their own payoffs, and the reduction is a reduced form. Take a clock that detects in each period with probability $\lambda$, so that $1-\upsilon(T)=(1-\lambda)^T$ (the memoryless clock, $\lambda=1-e^{-1/\tau_{\rm det}}$), write $T=T_{\rm dec}$, $a=\delta(1-\lambda)$, and let $\mathcal T$ be the geometric detection time.
- *(A) Punishment triggered by detection.* After the deviation the actors cooperate until it is detected, and punishment starts in the period of detection; undetected through $T$ periods, the deviator is decisive from period $T$. The deviation is deterred iff
$$(1-\delta)g_i+a^{T}(D_i-C_i)\le\lambda\delta\,\frac{1-a^{T}}{1-a}\,(C_i-v_i).$$
- *(B) Perfect monitoring, delayed stopping.* Punishment starts in the period after the deviation, but the breakout can be stopped only by detection within $T$ periods, and escapes with probability $p_d=(1-\lambda)^T$, decisive from period $T$: deterred iff $(1-\delta)g_i+\delta^{T}p_d(D_i-v_i)\le\delta(C_i-v_i)$.

Both coincide with (14) at $T=1$ and differ from it otherwise. Under (A) the weight on $C_i-v_i$ is $\delta\,E[\delta^{\mathcal T-1};\mathcal T\le T]$, below the reduction's $\delta(1-p_d)$ because the sanction is discounted to the date of detection: where no breakout is possible it is $\lambda\delta/(1-a)$ against $\delta$, 0.461 against 0.9 for a clock of mean 10 at $\delta=0.9$, so a slow clock weakens the sanction even without breakout risk; and the weight on $D_i-C_i$ is $\delta^T(1-\lambda)^T$ against $\delta(1-\lambda)^T$. Under (B) the decisive value is discounted by $\delta^T$ against $\delta$. For a deviation with $D_i>v_i$ and $C_i>v_i$, both are a lower bound on the detection rate that rises with $D_i$. Under (A) the condition reads $(C_i-v_i)\,E[\delta^{\min(\mathcal T,T)}]-\delta^T\Pr(\mathcal T>T)(D_i-v_i)\ge(1-\delta)g_i$; a larger $\lambda$ makes $\mathcal T$ stochastically smaller, which raises the first term and lowers the second, and the left side falls with $D_i$. Under (B) $p_d$ falls with $\lambda$ and the left side of the condition rises with $D_i$. So *timely detection must exceed a level that rises with the value of winning* holds under both timings; the identity $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})\ge1-\bar p_{d,i}$ and its equality with the threshold of Proposition 8.6 below are statements about the reduction. Over 20,000 random settings period-by-period sums agree with both closed forms to $3\times10^{-15}$; in the joint model of Remark S5.2 at $D=C$ the reduction's threshold of 0.551 becomes 0.514 under (A) and 0.556 under (B) [num: VF].

*The correspondence with Proposition 8.6, in the reduction.* Take its one-shot payoffs as continuation values normalized by $1-\delta$: $R=C_i$; $T=(1-\delta)(C_i+g_i)+\delta D_i$, the deviation that becomes decisive; $P-F=(1-\delta)(C_i+g_i)+\delta v_i$, the deviation detected and answered. Then $T-R=(1-\delta)g_i+\delta(D_i-C_i)$ and $T-(P-F)=\delta(D_i-v_i)$, so $(T-R)/(T-P+F)=1-\bar p_{d,i}$, and $T<R$ iff $\bar p_{d,i}>1$, the exemption of Corollary 5.7. Over 100,000 random settings with $D>v$ the two thresholds differ by at most $4.4\times10^{-11}$ [identity: JX].

*Where they differ.* Proposition 8.6 states when cooperating beats defecting against a cooperator, given the sanction $F$ and the one-shot payoffs; trust in the other's cooperation then decides whether cooperating is a best response, and the credibility of $F$ is not examined. (14) is the condition on the equilibrium path: the other restrains, the sanction is the credible continuation $v_i$ of a game in which breakouts remain possible, and the decisive position carries its own stakes through $\delta D_i$. Where the stage games coincide and trust is one, the two thresholds are one number; the difference matters where the sanction has to be enforced by the same actors it is meant to protect.

*Check of the clock.* Simulated escape probabilities under a memoryless clock equal $e^{-T_{\rm dec}/\tau_{\rm det}}$ within sampling error (0.4485 against 0.4493 at $T_{\rm dec}=8$, $\tau_{\rm det}=10$; 0.1349 against 0.1353 at $T_{\rm dec}=20$; 0.0818 against 0.0821 at $T_{\rm dec}=5$, $\tau_{\rm det}=2$) [num: JX]. Deviation values sampled in the one-period reduction agree with (14): with $C=1$, $D=1.5$, $v=0$, $g=0.3$ and $\tau_{\rm det}=10$, deviating is worth 0.738 against 1 at $T_{\rm dec}=8$ and is deterred, and 1.235 at $T_{\rm dec}=2$ and is not; since the sampled value is the reduction's own formula, this is an identity, not a test of the timing [identity: JX]. The timing is tested by the period-by-period sums under (A) and (B) above [num: VF].

### Proof of Corollary 5.6

At $\theta=-1$ every symmetric profile gives each objective 0. *Two actors.* Let the punisher use any strategy $\sigma_2$, pure or mixed, conditioning on the history as it likes, and let the punished play its mirror, $\sigma_1(\text{prefix},k)=\sigma_2(\text{prefix},\mathrm{swap}(k))$, where the prefix is the history up to and including the deviation and $k$ the history of the punishment phase: at every punishment-phase history, what the punisher would play with the roles in that phase swapped. Only $k$ is swapped; swapping the prefix too would make player 2 the deviator, and $\sigma_2$ there would give the punished player's behaviour, not the punisher's. The punishment phase is exchangeable and starts from a state that treats both alike, so the joint distribution of the two payoff streams is invariant under swapping the players and their expected objectives are equal; since the objectives sum to zero at $\theta=-1$, both are 0, and $v_1\ge0$. The one-period case is the antisymmetric matrix identity: the relative objective of a symmetric two-player game has $A=-A^\top$, and mirroring a mixed strategy $y$ gives $y^\top Ay=0$. *$n\ge3$.* In an anonymous game in which all punishers use the same, possibly mixed and independent, strategy, mirroring yields an exchangeable profile with expected relative payoff 0. In either case $v_i\ge0=C_i$, and (14) requires $(1-\delta)g_i+\delta p_dD_i\le\delta(1-p_d)(0-v_i)\le0$. *Where the premise fails.* A continuation that carries a label, such as an absorbing loss that can strike only the target, is not exchangeable: the mirror does not copy the punisher's continuation, and the bound fails. $\square$

*Numerics (CR; ES).* Over 20,000 random two-player games, mixed mirroring gives at most $2\times10^{-16}$ in absolute value; in 3,000 anonymous games with $n\ge3$, no violation [num: CR]. *The continuation premise.* An exchangeable continuation, in which a lottery awards an absorbing $\pm10$ with probability 0.3 a period, leaves the mirrored punished at a normalized relative value of $0.000000$. A label-dependent one, in which the target takes $-10$ with probability one half whatever it does, holds it at $-8.18$, so a deviation is worth $-69.6$ and relative actors sustain cooperation that is not a stage equilibrium (S14); the symmetric variant of the same lottery gives a punishment value of 0 and a deviation worth $+4$, so it does not [num: ES].

*The role-differentiated counterexample.* Three players choose (restrain or race) × (X or Y); a player whose second component is unique gains one, and racing gains 0.1 and costs each other 0.1. All restraining with X gives each 0, and the best deviation gains 1.2. The punishment in which the deviator and one punisher take X and the other punisher Y holds the deviator at $-0.5$ and is a stage equilibrium in objectives, so grim reversion to it is credible, and cooperation is sustained for $\delta\ge1.2/1.7=0.706$; a symmetric punishment could be mirrored for 0 [num: CR]. *Strictness.* As printed the punishment is weak: the best unilateral deviations from it gain 0, 0 and $-0.2$, so the two players in the X role are indifferent. Adding 0.02 to the prize for a unique X makes it strict; subtracting 0.02 destroys it, and the roles must swap, after which a perturbation of $-0.05$ gives a strict punishment with $\delta^\ast=0.716$. Under any small asymmetry between the roles' prizes the matching assignment is strict, and under random anonymous perturbations of all payoffs a strict punishment holding the deviator below cooperation exists in 300 of 300, 300 of 300 and 282 of 300 games at perturbation sizes 0.02, 0.05 and 0.1 (median $\delta^\ast$ 0.708, 0.711 and 0.713); a second scheme gives 400 of 400, 400 of 400 and 372 of 400 at 0.01, 0.05 and 0.1 (median about 0.71). With other stage parameters the deviator is still held at $-0.5$ ($\delta^\ast=0.730$) [num: CR]. *Indifference.* A purely relative punisher gains $+0.95$ from attacking the deviator and $+0.95$ from attacking its fellow punisher, against $+0.55$ from abstaining, and both punishers prefer the Y role ($+1.0$ against $-0.5$): the punishment needs coordination on roles, and which punisher takes which is equilibrium selection [num: CR]. Duersch, Oechssler and Schipper (2012) concern a dynamic imitation rule; the corollary uses only that a symmetric two-player zero-sum game has value 0, so rock–paper–scissors games are covered once mixed mirroring is allowed.

### Proof of Corollary 5.7

With $p_d=0$, (14) reads $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-v_i)$, satisfiable for $C_i>v_i$ and $\delta$ large, given a punishment that does not raise the chance of breakout (else $v_i$ rises, Proposition 5.4(5)); at $\theta=-1$ apply Corollary 5.6. The condition is linear in $p_d$: the temptation less the sanction is $(1-\delta)g_i-\delta(C_i-v_i)+\delta p_d(D_i-v_i)$. So it holds for every $p_d\in[0,1]$ iff it holds at both ends; where $D_i\ge v_i$ the end $p_d=1$, $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-D_i)$, implies the other, and where $D_i>v_i$ it is $\bar p_{d,i}\ge1$. Otherwise the condition is $p_d\le\bar p_{d,i}$, and (a) and (b) classify the terms of its expression: (a) lowers $p_d$ directly, by removing the decisive class, by custody that no party can use alone, or by detection and response faster than breakout ($p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$, Remark 5.5); (b) raises $\bar p_{d,i}$ through $D_i$ relative to $C_i$, through $v_i$, or through $g_i$. $\square$ With $C=1$, $D=0.9$, $v=0$, $g=2$ and $\delta=0.9$, $\bar p_d=0.8642$, and (14) holds at $p_d=0.86$ and fails at 0.87 and 0.9, so $D<C$ alone exempts nothing; with $g=0.5$, $(1-\delta)g=0.05\le\delta(C-D)=0.09$, and restraint holds at every $p_d$ [num: ES].

**Remark S5.1 (An illustration: the faithful-AI game at the boundary case) [thm; num].** In the binary game of Proposition 6.1 with a common $\theta$, write $A=\epsilon(b-h_d)-c$ for the material gain from racing alone, $B=\epsilon(b/(n-1)+h_d)$ for the average loss that racing imposes on each other actor, $K=c+n\epsilon h_d$ for each actor's loss when all race, against all restraining, and $w_p=\delta(1-p_d)/(1-\delta)$. Restraint is a stage equilibrium when $\theta\ge A/B$, and, at the boundary case $D=C$ with punishment by reversion to the race without breakouts, can be sustained when $\theta\ge\theta_{\rm rep}(p_d)=(A-w_pK)/(B+w_pK)$; equivalently, for a given working objective, restraint is enforceable iff $p_d\le\bar p_d(\theta)=1-(1-\delta)(A-\theta B)/\big(\delta K(1+\theta)\big)$, which rises with $\theta$ wherever $A>\theta B$. With $n=4$, $b=1$, $c=0.3$, $h_d=0.2$, $\epsilon=1$ and $\delta=0.9$: $A/B=0.94$, and $\theta_{\rm rep}$ is $-0.901$, $-0.812$, $-0.322$, $+0.005$, $+0.634$ and $+0.937$ at $p_d=0$, 0.5, 0.9, 0.95, 0.99 and 1, from the closed form and from direct discounted streams, with racing credible at each [identity: BI; num: JX]. An isolated self ($\theta=0$) loses enforced restraint once $p_d>0.9495$, and a self at $\theta=0.4$ once $p_d>0.9793$ [num: JX]. These are the thresholds of the boundary case, not bounds: a decisive position worth more than cooperation raises them (with $D-C=0.5$ in objective units, $\theta_{\rm rep}$ at $p_d=0.9$ rises from $-0.32$ to $+2.34$, and at $p_d=1$ no $\theta\le n-1$ sustains restraint, Proposition 5.4(4)) [num: NB; JX]. In the continuous contest intensity is positive for every $\theta<n-1$, so self-enforcing restraint there needs full inclusion of the contestants.

*Test G.* Let $\theta$ be drawn uniformly on $[-1,3]$, and count the share of draws above 1 (wider) and below 1 (narrower) that sustain restraint; the restraint gap is the difference. At $D=C$ it rises with $p_d$: $+0.05$, $+0.09$, $+0.21$, $+0.34$, $+0.50$, $+0.82$ and $+0.97$ at $p_d=0$, 0.5, 0.8, 0.9, 0.95, 0.99 and 1. With $D-C=0.5$ it is hump-shaped: $+0.05$, $+0.30$, $+0.92$, $+0.33$, 0, 0 and 0 at the same values, because at $p_d=1$ the condition is $(1-\delta)g\le\delta(C-D)<0$, impossible for $g\ge0$, so the enforceable set is empty for every $\theta\le n-1$. These computations establish sensitivity to the objective in this specified game: where a decisive position is worth more than cooperation, the gap is largest at intermediate $p_d$ and closes as $p_d\to1$ [num: JX]. They do not compare the same observations against a specified rival model of structural realism, so discrimination between theories remains unestablished.

### Proof of Proposition 5.8
