With linear payoffs the objective $(1-s)W+\theta sW/(n-1)$ has slope $W(\theta/(n-1)-1)\le0$ in the share $s$ given away, zero only at $\theta=n-1$; with logarithmic utility the first-order condition of $\ln((1-s)W)+\theta\ln(sW/(n-1))$ gives $s=\theta/(1+\theta)$, which a grid search confirms at $\theta=0.4$, 1, 2 and 3 (0.286, 0.500, 0.667, 0.750) [num: WG]. A winner acting on its true welfare toward what can be divided has $\theta=\kappa_W$ by Proposition 3.6(2), so it gives $\kappa_W/(1+\kappa_W)$; toward a positional prize its accurate weight is $\kappa_s\le0$, and it gives nothing. The promise is Proposition 3.5(4): a share promised to the $n-1$ others is worth breaking by $\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/(n-1)$, and one split among all $n$ by $\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/n$ [num: SM]. $\square$

### Proposition 5.9 in full

*Two actors bargain over a flow of value 1 per period with discount $\delta$; fighting ends bargaining, A then obtaining the flow with its probability of prevailing less a cost $c_A$ per period; A prevails with probability $w_1$ today and $w_2<w_1$ after B's advantage arrives one period later. (1) Let $x$ be A's per-period share after the shift; fighting now, worth $(w_1-c_A)/(1-\delta)$, is rational if $w_1-c_A>(1-\delta)+\delta x$, and only if, when peace gives A the whole flow today. (2) Under (A8), the advantaged side setting the terms, B offers A its reservation value $x=\max(w_2-c_A,0)$. (3) Under other protocols $x$ can reach $\min(w_2+c_B,1)$, and attack can cease to be rational. (4) If $c_A\ge w_1$, war is worth at most zero. A shift spread over enough periods removes the motive.*

*Proof and numbers.* (1) and (2) compare the two discounted streams. (3): with $w_1=0.5$, $w_2=0$, $c_A=c_B=0.2$ and $\delta=0.8$, A attacks under (A8), while if A sets the future terms peace gives A 1.8 and B 3.2, against 1.5 each from war [num: legacy v4.2]. For the gradual shift, with $w$ falling from 0.5 to 0 over $N$ periods, $c_A=0.05$ and $\delta=0.8$, attack is rational at the start for $N\le3$ and never for $N\ge4$, by backward induction on $V_t=\max(W_t,\delta V_{t+1})$ [num: legacy v4.4]. $\square$ The premise is the protocol (A8); the mechanism is the incredibility of a promise the advantaged side has no reason to keep, the same incredibility that Proposition 5.8 states for a winner. In the loop of Section 2 the shift is the opening of a lead, at the speed of Remark S2.1.

### Proof of Proposition 5.10

By Proposition 3.6(1), total intensity is $ne(\theta_t)$, which gives $h_t$; survival is $\prod_te^{-h_t}$ by the chain rule. Sharing a fraction $\beta$ of the divisible prize gives the contest with prize $(1-\beta)\Omega_t$ (Proposition 10.2), linear in $h_t$; a rebated levy enters at $\tau(n-1-\theta_t)/n$. Since $\ell/(c+(1+\theta)\ell)<1/(1+\theta)$, $\sum_tr_t\Omega_t(n-1-\theta_t)/(1+\theta_t)<\infty$ suffices for $\theta_t>-1$ whatever $\ell_t$. A background hazard from capability growth adds to $h_t$, and no lever of the contest removes it: that is the growth channel. $\square$ The closed form agrees with direct evaluation by construction, and the symmetric intensity with a best response on a grid (0.11444) [identity: RH, with an independent grid best response].

### Lemma 5.11 in full

*Fix one irreversible event. If on every surviving history the residual probability in round $k$ is at least $h_k$ with $\sum_kh_k=\infty$, then $\Pr(\Phi_N)\le\prod_{k\le N}(1-h_k)\le\exp(-\sum_{k\le N}h_k)\to0$, by the chain rule, without independence between rounds; if it is at most $\bar h_k<1$ with $\sum\bar h_k<\infty$, survival is at least $\prod(1-\bar h_k)>0$.* With $h_k=0.2/k$ survival is 0.34, 0.22 and 0.14 by rounds 100, 1,000 and 10,000; with $0.2/k^2$ it settles at 0.70 [num: legacy v5].

### Proof of Proposition 5.12

Proportional correction: Hayes (1950). Derivative action extends the range: for $a=1$ and $\tau_c=1.5$, gains $k_p=1.02$, $k_d=0.644$ stabilize the mode, where proportional correction alone diverges [num: legacy v4.2]. For the sensitivity bound, with plant $e^{-s\tau_c}/(s-a)$ and any stabilizing linear time-invariant controller, the complementary sensitivity is stable with value 1 at $s=a$; writing it as $e^{-s\tau_c}$ times a stable function and applying the maximum modulus principle gives $\|\mathcal T\|_\infty\ge e^{a\tau_c}$, and robust stability $\|w\mathcal T\|_\infty<1$ gives $|w(a)|e^{a\tau_c}<1$, necessary only: $w(s)=2(s-a)/(s+a)$ has $w(a)=0$ but $\|w\mathcal T\|_\infty\ge2e^{a\tau_c}$. Evidence cannot be acted on before it exists. $\square$

### What would make coverage lag

Consequences take their time, and tools raise the throughput of observation without making consequences arrive sooner; institutions deliberate at rates set by attention and procedure; learning theoretical models and forming new mechanisms are human processes; and understanding produced with human time, $\dot U=A_UL_U^{\lambda_U}U^{\phi_U}$ with $\phi_U<1$ and bounded labour, grows polynomially (Jones 1995; Bloom et al. 2020). None settles the premise: if AI raises $A_U$ exponentially at rate $g_A$, $U$ grows exponentially at $g_A/(1-\phi_U)$. Where correction leans on AI, the human capacity it rests on can erode, which raises the rate correction needs (Proposition 4.6). Measures: the safety of control protocols at a fixed audit budget across generations, and catch rates of planted defects at fixed human hours, controlled for evaluation awareness. Under strict legibility, (coverage lags) is close to definitional for systems beyond human capability, and the empirical content lies in (residual floor), which a safety case with a numerical bound on the uncovered part would state (Clymer et al. 2024). (variety grows) is what makes (coverage lags) expected on a continuation branch; the theorem does not use it, and R5's list of conditions under which the requirement is not needed keeps it.

### Proof of Theorem 5.13

For $t\ge T_\varepsilon$, (coverage lags) gives $U_t/Y_t\le\varepsilon$ on every surviving history, so by (residual floor) the conditional probability of the event is at least $h(1-\varepsilon)$; the chain rule gives the bound. The growth of $Y_t$ is never used, which is why (variety grows) is a motivation and not a premise. Under (shared failure) the event is one for all, so the bound is every actor's; without it the chain runs over the racer's own exposure. The converse is the upper part of Lemma 5.11 with $\bar h_k=\bar hu_k$. (residual floor) gives a liminf of the hazard of at least $h$, not that the hazard tends to $h$. With $u_t=1/(t+2)^2$ coverage stays behind forever and survival with $h=0.05$ stays above 0.98; with $h=0.05$ and $\varepsilon=0.1$, survival eighty periods after $T_\varepsilon$ is at most 0.025 [num: legacy v5]. $\square$

### Proposition 5.14 in full

*Let hazardous variety be frozen at $Y_\tau$ from $\tau$ while coverage grows. (1) The exposure after $\tau$ is $\int_\tau^\infty(1-U(t)/Y_\tau)^+dt$: $Y_\tau(1-U(\tau)/Y_\tau)^2/(2\dot U)$ for linear coverage, and $(\ln\varrho-1+1/\varrho)/g_U$ for coverage growing at rate $g_U$, with $\varrho=Y_\tau/U(\tau)>1$. (2) Rolled back to $Y\le U$, exposure stops. (3) If a sprint builds capability $\varrho_X$ times the rivals' level and hazardous variety is a fraction $\varphi$ of capability's, frozen exposure is zero when $\varphi\varrho_X\le1$. (4) For linear coverage the same frozen ratio costs more the later the sprint; this fails for some sub-exponential coverage.*

*Proof.* Integrate. For (4), let $\log U$ rise by 0.5 on each interval $[m^2,m^2+1]$ and stay constant otherwise: $\log U\approx0.5\sqrt t=o(t)$, while $\dot U/U=0.5$ inside each interval, so a sprint timed to end inside one costs less than an earlier one (S14). $\square$ With hazardous variety equal to capability's, growth 0.3, linear coverage $1+0.5t$ and $h=0.05$, a sprint to $\tau=10$ leaves survival 0.86; freezing then gives 0.53, and rolling back keeps 0.86 [num: legacy v4.5]. What the freezing winner gives up is permanent superiority: a frozen stock is overtaken by anyone who keeps growing, which is why Section 8.3 treats the sprint-and-freeze path by its incidence and not by this proposition alone.

### Proof of Proposition 5.15

After $t_N$, $U_t\ge U_{t_N}+u(t-t_N)$, so the uncovered share is zero from $t_N+Y_N/u$ on, and Lemma 5.11's upper part bounds survival below. In the growth-channel example, capability frozen at need leaves survival on the channel at 0.717, with coverage catching up at period 14, while capability compounding at 10% a period leaves $1.8\times10^{-9}$ by period 400 [num: PE]. In the contest of Proposition 3.6(1), with costs and the other parameters fixed, symmetric intensity is proportional to the prize for every $\theta<n-1$. Replacing that prize by a bounded value, such as $1-e^{-\Omega}$, therefore bounds intensity as the stakes grow. With $n=4$, $r=1$ and $c=1$, intensities are 0.19, 1.88 and 18.75 for linear payoffs at stakes 1, 10 and 100, against 0.12, 0.19 and 0.19 for the bounded prize [num: SM]. The latter intensity tends to $3/16>0$, while the prize remains strictly increasing at every finite $\Omega$: neither has a finite stopping point. A bounded prize thus establishes a bound on effort at fixed costs; the finite-exposure result uses the separate premise that hazardous variety actually stops growing. $\square$ An absolute objective linear in the stakes is as unsatiated as a relative one, which is why the core states the rule as satiation in the stakes and not as relative against absolute.

### Proof of Proposition 5.16

(1) Let all $n$ actors hold $\theta$, so $J_S=\pi_S+\frac\theta{n-1}\big(\pi_R+\sum_k\pi_k\big)$ and $J_R=\pi_R+\frac\theta{n-1}\big(\pi_S+\sum_k\pi_k\big)$, the sums over the third parties $k$. With $\pi_S=-(y-u-b_0)^2$, $\pi_R=-(y-u)^2$ and $\sum_k\pi_k$ linear in $y$ with total slope $L$, both objectives are quadratic in $y$ with the same curvature, $-2(n-1+\theta)/(n-1)$, negative iff $n-1+\theta>0$. Their ideal points are
$$y_S-u=\frac{2b_0(n-1)+\theta L}{2(n-1+\theta)},\qquad y_R-u=\frac{2\theta b_0+\theta L}{2(n-1+\theta)},$$
which differ by $b_0(n-1-\theta)/(n-1+\theta)$, independent of $L$: what the receiver's action does to third parties enters both objectives alike. Each objective is then, up to a positive factor and terms that do not depend on $y$, the uniform-quadratic payoff of Crawford and Sobel with that difference as the bias, and their condition for an informative equilibrium applies to it. Numerically maximized ideal points match the formula in 19 combinations of $n\in\{2,3,4,6\}$ and $\theta$, to $1.5\times10^{-8}$ [num: TK].

(2) At $\theta=-1$ with $n$ actors, $J_k=\pi_k-\frac1{n-1}\sum_{j\ne k}\pi_j$, and direct expansion gives $J_S-J_R=\frac n{n-1}(\pi_S-\pi_R)$ and $J_S+J_R=\frac{n-2}{n-1}(\pi_S+\pi_R)-\frac2{n-1}\sum_{k\ne S,R}\pi_k$; the identity holds to $3.6\times10^{-15}$ [num: T3]. With $n\ge3$ the effective bias of (1) is $b_0n/(n-2)$: at $b_0=0.05$ it is 0.150, 0.100, 0.075 and 0.0625 for $n=3$, 4, 6 and 10, so informative talk needs a material bias below 0.083, 0.125, 0.167 and 0.200 [num: TK]. A two-message partition equilibrium, built directly with indifferent boundary types and numerical best responses, exists iff the effective bias is below $\frac14$ (for $n=3$: at $b_0=0.08$, bias 0.24, boundary type 0.02; at $b_0=0.1$, bias 0.30, none; at exactly $\frac14$, none); with $b_0=0$ and a common cost to third parties, full revelation is an equilibrium [num: T3]. *Quadratic third parties.* Let the third parties' payoffs sum to $-ky^2$ instead of a linear term. Both objectives then have curvature $-2(n-1+\theta(1+k))/(n-1)$, so they are concave iff $n-1+\theta(1+k)>0$ (at $n=3$, $\theta=-1$, iff $k<1$); their ideal points differ by $b_0(n-1-\theta)/(n-1+\theta(1+k))$, and the receiver's ideal action moves with the state at the slope $(n-1+\theta)/(n-1+\theta(1+k))$. Shifting the action by the receiver's ideal at $u=0$ and dividing by that slope turns each objective, up to a positive factor and terms free of the action, into the uniform-quadratic payoff with bias $b_0(n-1-\theta)/(n-1+\theta)$ in units of the state, so the condition of (1) applies unchanged; at $\theta=-1$ the gap in units of the state is $b_0n/(n-2)$, as the grid finds [num: TK, VF]. With $n=2$ at $\theta=-1$ the curvature vanishes and the objectives sum to zero. If a sender's and a receiver's payoffs sum to zero, each sender type induces, among the equilibrium actions, one that minimizes the receiver's payoff in its state, so the receiver's payoff is at most that of ignoring messages, which it can always do; equilibrium payoffs equal the no-talk values (300 random games, 1,096 pure equilibria, none whose receiver payoff differs from the no-talk value) [num: TK].

(3) A sender that intends to race prefers the receiver to cooperate: with $R=1$, $T=2\varepsilon$, $P=\varepsilon$ and $S=0$, it gains $\varepsilon$ from the receiver's cooperation however small $\varepsilon$ is (compare Aumann 1990). $\square$

That pairs who still talk align against third parties is a reading of (2), not a result; the amplification falls with $n$, as Snidal (1991) found for relative gains generally.

### Proof of Proposition 5.17

*Arm 1.* By Remark S2.1, one channel's gap factor is $\Lambda^\ast=1+(d_K+\delta_f)(\Gamma-1)$, and $(d_K+\delta_f)\Gamma=(1-\beta)r\omega d_K$ by (2), so $\Lambda^\ast-1=(1-\beta)r\omega d_K-d_K-\delta_f$, increasing in $\omega$. Where $\Lambda^\ast>1$, $T_{\rm dec}=\ln(\bar\Delta/\varepsilon_0)/\ln\Lambda^\ast$ falls with $\omega$, and since $\upsilon$ is non-decreasing, $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$ rises, strictly where $\upsilon$ is increasing. With several channels the Perron root of (S1) rises with each channel's term $(1-\beta)r_j\omega_jd_j$, since above its poles the right side of the secular equation increases in each term and decreases in $\Lambda$. That term rises with the stakes in every channel: $r_j\omega_j=v_jX^{j\ast}\omega_j=v_j\kappa_j\Omega/(nd_j)$, and $v_j\propto X^{\xi_{r,j}-1}$ with $X^{j\ast}$ rising in $\Omega$, so $r_j\omega_j\propto\Omega\,X^{j\ast\,\xi_{r,j}-1}$, whose elasticity in $\Omega$ is $1-\omega_j+\xi_{r,j}\omega_j$, since that of $X^{j\ast}$ is $\omega_j$: positive wherever $\xi_{r,j}>0$ or $\omega_j<1$. Where $\omega_j$ rises instead because provision $k_{0j}$ falls, $X^{j\ast}$ falls and the term rises iff $\xi_{r,j}<1$, is unchanged at $\xi_{r,j}=1$ and falls above (Proposition S2.4(3)); so the Perron root rises with each channel's won share only in channels with $\xi_{r,j}<1$, as in a ratio channel. *Arm 2.* By (7) the deviation's gain in objectives is $g_i(\theta)=(1+\theta)\bar\Delta+\frac{n-1-\theta}{n-1}(\Delta\pi_i-\bar\Delta)$, which falls in $\theta$ when $\bar\Delta\le0<\Delta\pi_i-\bar\Delta$. At a symmetric profile $J_i=(1+\theta)\bar\pi$, so $C_i-v_i=(1+\theta)(\bar\pi_C-\bar\pi_P)$, which rises in $\theta$. Write $\bar p_{d,i}=(c-\alpha)/(b+c)$ as in the proof of Proposition 5.4: a narrower $\theta$ raises $\alpha$, lowers $c$ and does not lower $b$, and each lowers $\bar p_{d,i}$ where $b+c>0$ and $0\le\bar p_{d,i}\le1$. With a fixed prize, $\partial\theta_\Gamma/\partial\Gamma_\delta$ has the sign of $\theta_0-(n-1)<0$, and in one channel $\Gamma_\delta=(1-\beta)r\omega\,\delta d_K/\big(1-\delta(1-d_K-\delta_f)\big)$ is proportional to $\omega$; with a growing prize, Proposition 3.9 gives the sign. The binary game of Remark S5.1 meets the premises, and there $\bar p_d(\theta)=1-(1-\delta)(A-\theta B)/\big(\delta K(1+\theta)\big)$ rises with $\theta$ wherever $A>\theta B$, since $A-\theta B$ falls and $1+\theta$ rises: the instance the reduced form below computes. *Arm 3.* $\partial\bar p_{d,i}/\partial v_i=-\big[(1-\delta)g_i+\delta(D_i-C_i)\big]/\big[\delta(D_i-v_i)^2\big]$, negative wherever $\bar p_{d,i}<1$; Section 4 gives the mechanism by which captured correctors raise the value of the punishment phase. *Arm 4.* $\bar p_{d,i}$ is unchanged when $(g_i,C_i,D_i,v_i)$ are scaled alike. If $\alpha$ and $b$ are multiplied by $k$ relative to $c$, $\bar p_{d,i}=(c-k\alpha)/(kb+c)$, whose derivative in $k$ is $-c(\alpha+b)/(kb+c)^2<0$ where $c>0$ and $\alpha+b>0$, that is, $\bar p_{d,i}<1$ [num: ES]. *The pivot.* In one channel $\Gamma=(1-\beta)r\omega d_K/(d_K+\delta_f)$ and $\Gamma_\delta$ are both proportional to $\omega=\kappa_c\Omega/(\kappa_c\Omega+nk_0)$, which rises with the stakes and falls with provision, so arms 1 and 2 are comparative statics of $\omega$. Scaling stakes and provision together leaves $\omega$ and, in a ratio channel, $r$ unchanged; in a channel with $\xi_{r,j}>0$, $r_j\propto X^{j\ast\,\xi_{r,j}}$ grows with the common scale, so there provision that keeps pace with the stakes does not hold the gain still (in a linear level channel, scaling both by 2 and 4 scales $\Gamma$ by 2 and 4) [num: VF]. $\square$

*The reduced form (JX).* In the binary game of Remark S5.1 at $D=C$, let actors act on $\theta_\Gamma(\theta_0,\Gamma_\delta)$ with zero common return, so that the compounding contest running alongside enters to first order through the price of standing; rivals do not respond, one $p_d$ applies to the deviation, and the punishment is the stage equilibrium of the binary game, with no breakout during punishment ($q_b=0$). The largest discounted loop gain at which restraint remains enforceable is found by bisection on direct discounted streams, with no threshold formula. For $\theta_0=0$ it is 0.924, 0.852, 0.387 and 0.108 at $p_d=0$, 0.5, 0.9 and 0.94, and none exists above $p_d=0.9495$; for $\theta_0=0.4$ it is 0.953, 0.908, 0.621, 0.448, 0.377 and 0.161 at $p_d=0$, 0.5, 0.9, 0.94, 0.95 and 0.97, and none above 0.9793 (V6.1 printed 0.99 for both). With a common loop gain, the prize growing with what all reinvest, the objective blade opens again: at $p_d=0.9$ the value rises from 0.387 to 0.571 at $\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta=0.3$ and to 0.755 at 0.6 [num: JX]. In this game $D+V^{\rm sub}>2P$, so a breakout technology that also acted during punishment would soften it (Proposition 5.4(5)) and lower every figure; the joint model below computes that case.

**Remark S5.2 (Both blades in one reduced model) [num].** The arms of Proposition 5.17 are proved one at a time; this remark runs them together along paths of the pivot, so that the conjunction can be seen in one place. It estimates nothing. *Model.* Four actors and one ratio channel, capacity, in the model of Proposition 2.3: $p_i=K_i^r/\sum_jK_j^r$ with $r=3$, $d_K=0.2$, $\delta_f=0.05$ and nothing shared; the won share $\omega$ fixes provision against the stakes, and $\Gamma$ and $\Gamma_\delta$ follow from it at $\delta=0.9$. $T_{\rm dec}$ is the number of periods in which the nonlinear stock dynamics take a lead from 5% to 50%, with stakes and provision held at their current values while it opens (the linearized time is computed beside it). Detection and response run on a memoryless clock of mean 10 periods, so $p_d=e^{-T_{\rm dec}/10}$ (Remark 5.5). Actors act on the working objective $\theta_\Gamma(0,\Gamma_\delta)$ of Proposition 3.9, and on $-1$ at $\Gamma_\delta\ge1$, the limit from below; rivals do not respond. The stage game is that of Remark S5.1 ($b=1$, $c=0.3$, $h_d=0.2$, $\epsilon=1$) with every payoff, the decisive value included, proportional to the stakes, so that $\bar p_d$ does not depend on their scale and the stakes arm is off. The decisive position is worth $D=C$ or $D-C=0.5$ in objective units per unit of stakes. Punishment is reversion to the race, (i) without breakouts, or (ii) with one breakout technology throughout: during the punishment race each racer breaks out with the same $p_d$, independently, a simultaneous breakout leaving the race as it was and the subordinated being worth the race.

*Results.* At $D=C$ restraint is enforceable iff $\omega<0.551$ under (i) and $\omega<0.542$ under (ii). With $p_d$ held at zero, the objective blade alone closes at 0.556; with the objective held at $\theta_0$, the breakout blade alone never closes, since $p_d$ reaches only 0.45 at $\omega=0.9$. At the crossing $\Gamma=1.32$, $\Gamma_\delta=0.92$ and $p_d=0.10$. At $D-C=0.5$ the thresholds are 0.536 and 0.504. These use the one-period reduction of Remark 5.5. Under the exact timing in which cooperation continues until detection and punishment starts then, timing (A) of its derivation, the joint threshold (i) is 0.514 at $D=C$ and at $D-C=0.5$, since a slow clock delays the sanction as well; with perfect monitoring and only the stopping of the breakout delayed, timing (B), it is 0.556 and 0.554 [num: VF]. Along paths on which $\omega/(1-\omega)$ grows at the rate of the stakes less that of provision: with stakes growing 5% a period and provision fixed, from $\omega=0.20$, $p_d$ rises from 0 to 0.07 and $\bar p_d$ falls from 0.911 to 0.386 by period 30, and the blades cross at period 31.8; with provision growing as fast as the stakes, neither moves ($\omega=0.20$ and $\bar p_d=0.911$ throughout); with provision outgrowing the stakes from $\omega=0.60$, enforceability returns within ten periods. Over 96 settings of the detection clock (5 to 40 periods), diffusion (0 to 0.1), held self-model (0 and 0.4), decisiveness (2 and 3) and $D-C$ (0 and 0.5), the joint threshold lies strictly below the lower of the two blades' own in 84, and the breakout blade alone closes in 12 [num: JX, one reduced model, rivals not responding]; that it never lies above it follows from $p_d\ge0$, $D\ge C$, $\theta_\Gamma\le\theta_0$ and the rise of $\bar p_d$ with $\theta$, so the count of 96 checks the implementation, not the model. In the base settings the objective blade does most of the closing; with a slow clock (20 to 40 periods), mostly at $D-C=0.5$, the breakout blade moves the crossing earlier by 0.05 to 0.13 in $\omega$ in 26 of the 96, so there detection speed moves the threshold materially [num: VF]. Across decisiveness, the blades close only where the discounted loop gain can approach one: with $p_d$ at zero the objective blade closes only where $\theta_\Gamma$ falls below $\theta_{\rm rep}(0)=-0.901$, that is, at $\Gamma_\delta\ge0.924$ [num: JX]. At $r=1$ the blades never close for $\delta_f$ from 0 to 0.1; at $r=1.5$ they close only without diffusion, near $\omega=0.95$; at $r=1.7$ with $\delta_f=0.05$ they close at 0.975 [num: VF].

*A second specification.* A separately written model, with different choices throughout, gives the same picture; its meeting times are its own and are not comparable with those above. Four actors; one channel with $r=1.5$, $d_K=0.5$, $\delta_f=0$, $\beta=0$ and $\kappa_c=1$; $\delta=0.8$; stakes $\Omega_t=e^{0.05t}$ against provision $k_0=1$, so that $\omega$ starts at 0.20; $T_{\rm dec}$ from the linearized gap factor, from a 1% to a 50% gap; a detection clock of mean 12 periods; $\theta_0=0$; the stage game of Remark S5.1 held fixed while the stakes grow; punishment without breakouts. $\Gamma$ passes 1 near period 42, after which $p_d$ rises from 0, while $\theta$ narrows from $-0.16$ and $\bar p_d$ falls from 0.842; the blades meet at period 59, where $\omega=0.83$, $\Gamma=1.24$, $T_{\rm dec}=34$ periods, $p_d=0.057$, $\theta=-0.78$ and $\bar p_d=0.045$. If $b$ and $d$ scale with the stakes while $c$ does not, which turns part of the stakes arm on, they meet at period 47. With provision growing as fast as the stakes neither blade moves. Detection twice as fast moves the meeting only from period 59 to 60, and a held self-model of 0.4 moves it to period 69: here too the objective arm, not the breakout arm, does the closing, since $p_d$ is still small when $\bar p_d$ reaches it [num: JX, one reduced model, rivals not responding].

*Label.* Two reduced models, with rivals not responding and, in their base runs, the stakes arm switched off; in the world the co-movement of the arms is conjecture C0, tested by Test E (Section 7.4). What the models show is narrower and exact: along a path on which the stakes outgrow provision beneath the game, both blades of (14) move toward each other through the one quantity $\omega$, and along a path on which provision keeps pace, neither moves.

## S6. AI: instrument and incarnation

This chapter gives the proofs and numerics of Section 6; the full statements of three results the core states in a sentence (Proposition S6.2, Proposition S6.4, Remark S6.5); three supplementary results: how certain a lock must be for narrow E1 to need no trust (Remark S6.1), the formal part of the masked class, and the bounded optimizer (Remark S6.3); and, from Section 6.4, the sources of weak incarnation, the demonstrations of masking and the worked instance of legible reasoning. Numbers cite the block of `checks_v72.py` that reproduces them; AL is the alignment review's independent recomputation of Section 6 (`../v6.1/reviews/scripts-v6.1-alignment/al_checks.py`), ported whole, with its parts named as the reviewer named them.

### Proof of Proposition 6.1

The harm coefficient, $d$ in V6.1, is written $h_d$, since $d$ is the correction rate; its exponent in (3) is $\beta_h$ (the log of SM(5) writes $\beta_d$).

Changing $a_i$ from 0 to 1 changes $u_i$ by $\epsilon b-c-\epsilon h_d$, independently of the others' choices, and each other $u_j$ by $-\epsilon b/(n-1)-\epsilon h_d$. Summed over all principals the relative terms cancel: at all-racing each principal has $-c-n\epsilon h_d$, which gives (1). In objectives, racing changes $J_i$ by $\epsilon b\big(1-\frac{\theta}{n-1}\big)-c-\epsilon h_d(1+\theta)$, which is positive iff $\theta<\theta^\ast$; this gives (2). For (3), write $\theta^\ast=(T-H-c)/(T/(n-1)+H)$ with $T=\epsilon b$ and $H=\epsilon h_d$ growing with exponents $1+\beta_b$ and $1+\beta_h$, and $c$ with $\beta_c$. If $\beta_c<1+\max(\beta_b,\beta_h)$, $c$ is negligible against the larger of $T$ and $H$, and the limit is set by $T/H$, which is constant, unbounded or vanishing as $\beta_b$ equals, exceeds or falls short of $\beta_h$. If $\beta_c>1+\max(\beta_b,\beta_h)$, the numerator tends to $-\infty$ faster than the denominator grows. $\square$

*Numerics* ($n=4$, $b=1$, $h_d=0.2$, $c=0.3$). Exponents $(\beta_b,\beta_h,\beta_c)=(0,0,0)$ and $(1,1,0)$ give the limit 1.50; $(0,0.5,0)$ gives $-0.99$ at $\epsilon=10^6$; $(0.5,0,0)$ gives 2.998; $(1,0,3)$ and $(0,0,1.5)$ diverge to $-\infty$; at the boundary $\beta_c=1+\max(\beta_b,\beta_h)$, $(0,0,1)$ gives 0.9375 and $(1,0,2)$ gives 2.1 [num: SM(5)]. With $b$, $h_d$ and $c$ fixed, $\theta^\ast$ is 0.9375 at $\epsilon=1$ and 1.44 at $\epsilon=10$; with $c$ also scaling with $\epsilon$ it stays 0.94 [num: AL(6.1)]. The double edge of Section 6.2: at $\epsilon=1$, all-racing gives each principal $-1.1$; lowering $h_d$ to 0.1 and 0.05 cuts the loss to 0.70 and 0.50 while raising $\theta^\ast$ to 1.39 and 1.70, and $\Delta J_i$ changes sign at $\theta^\ast$ [num: AL(6.1)]. This is the second fate of safety progress in Section 6.4. The condition once written as $\beta_c<\beta_b=\beta_d$ was neither necessary nor sufficient; the correct one is $\beta_c<1+\max(\beta_b,\beta_h)$.

### Proof of Proposition 6.2

Actor $i$'s payoff is $-L_e(1-w_i)-L_c\big[1-\prod_j(1-\psi_j)\big]$, with $w_i=(1+s\iota_i)/\sum_j(1+s\iota_j)$. (1) With $k$ others delegating, $w_i(1,k)=(1+s)/(n+(k+1)s)$ and $w_i(0,k)=1/(n+ks)$. The first is non-decreasing in $s$ and the second decreasing, so the gain $L_e[w_i(1,k)-w_i(0,k)]$ increases in $s$, toward $L_e(1-1/n)$ for $k=0$ and $L_e/(k+1)$ for $k\ge1$. The cost is the change in shared survival, $L_c\Delta\psi(1-\psi_0)^{n-1-k}(1-\psi_1)^k$, independent of $s$. If every limit exceeds its cost, there is a speed beyond which delegating is strictly better at every $k$; if one limit does not, delegating is worse at that $k$ for every $s$, since the gain stays below its limit. The smallest limit is $L_e/n$ and the largest cost $L_c\Delta\psi$, so $L_e/n>L_c\Delta\psi$ suffices. (2) With all delegating $w_i=1/n$, as with none, and shared survival is $(1-\psi_1)^n<(1-\psi_0)^n$. (3) Under relative objectives the shared term is common to all and cancels; the gain in $w_i$ remains, and is positive for every $s>0$. $\square$

*Numerics.* With $n=3$, $L_e=L_c$, $\psi_0=0.01$ and $\psi_1=0.2$, delegation is dominant from $s^\ast=1.1628$, where a lone delegator wins with probability 0.5196 against $1/3$ without delegation [num: AL(6.2)]. A speed advantage of this size is far from decisive, which is the sense of "well below decisive capability" in Section 6.3.

### Proof of Lemma 6.3, and where $q_L$ comes from

With $\dot{\mathcal M}=-\delta_h\mathcal M$ under delegation and $\dot{\mathcal M}=\varrho_h(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M)$ otherwise, capacity reaches $\mathcal M_{\min}$ after $T_{\rm lock}=\ln(\mathcal M_0/\mathcal M_{\min})/\delta_h$ of delegation, and from $\mathcal M<\mathcal M_{\min}$ it regains $\mathcal M_{\min}$ after $T_R(\mathcal M)=\varrho_h^{-1}\ln[(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M)/(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M_{\min})]$. Capacity maintained in parallel does not decay; the resources it uses are withdrawn from strength. $\square$

The lock probability $q_L$ of Proposition 6.4 is therefore not free. Let a delegation last $T_{\rm del}$, and let the contest allow an actor that reverts a window $T_W$ before its next consequential decision. The lock counts if the delegated decision is irreversible within the window for deliberation, or if $T_{\rm del}>T_{\rm lock}$ and $T_W<T_R\big(\mathcal M(T_{\rm del})\big)$; $q_L$ is the probability of that event, set by the kind of decision, the contest's tempo and $\delta_h$. Drilled parallel capacity keeps the second part of the event rare at a cost in strength, which is why it has to be held in common. The same capacity carries correction: where the effective correction rate is $d(\mathcal M/\mathcal M_0)^w$ and $\mathcal M$ decays at $\delta_h$, the rate at which independent correction must grow rises by $w\delta_h$ (Proposition 4.6; S4, block QS).

### Proposition 6.4 in full, and proof

*Let locked delegation end the delegator's ability to overrule with probability $q_L$, and losing the contest mean subordination with probability $p$. With $Q=\prod_j(1-\psi_j)$ and win probabilities as in Proposition 6.2, actor $i$'s survival is $\Phi_i=(1-q_L\iota_i)\,Q\,[w_i+(1-w_i)(1-p)]$. Write $a=(1-\psi_1)/(1-\psi_0)$ and, with $k$ others delegating, $w_D=(1+s)/(n+(k+1)s)$ and $w_N=1/(n+ks)$, so that*
$$\frac{\Phi_D}{\Phi_N}=(1-q_L)\,a\,\frac{1-p+p\,w_D}{1-p+p\,w_N}.$$
*(1) If $q_L=1$ and $Q>0$, not delegating gives strictly higher survival for every finite $s$, every $n$, every $p$ and every profile of the others. (2) If $p=1$ and $A=(1-q_L)a$, the ratio is $A(1+s)(n+ks)/(n+(k+1)s)$, which rises with $k$; all delegating is an equilibrium iff $A(n+(n-1)s)/n\ge1$, and no one delegating iff $An(1+s)/(n+s)\le1$. At least one always holds, since $A(n+(n-1)s)/n-An(1+s)/(n+s)=A(n-1)s^2/[n(n+s)]\ge0$, and both can. (3) If $p<1$, the subordination term $1-p$ dilutes the win probabilities and the ratio can fall as $k$ rises. If it decreases in $k$ and crosses one between $k=m-1$ and $k=m$ for some $1\le m\le n-1$, the profiles with exactly $m$ delegators are the only pure equilibria, and neither corner is one. (4) At bounded speed one delegator does not drive another's survival to zero: a non-delegator facing $k$ delegators survives with probability at least $Q_N/(n+ks)>0$.*

*Proof.* (1) $\Phi_D=0$ while $\Phi_N\ge Q_Nw_N>0$. (2) Take $k=n-1$ for all delegating and $k=0$ for none; the identity gives "at least one". With $p=1$ delegations are complements. (3) A profile with $m$ delegators is an equilibrium iff the ratio is at least one at $k=m-1$, so that no delegator gains by stopping, and at most one at $k=m$, so that no other actor gains by starting; a decreasing ratio meets both conditions at one $m$ only. With $p<1$ delegations can be substitutes. (4) $\Phi_N=Q_N[1-p+p\,w_N]\ge Q_Nw_N$. $\square$

*Numerics.* The outside reviewer's case, $n=3$, $s=1$, $p=0.1$, $q_L=0$, $\psi_0=0$, $\psi_1=0.015$: the ratios for $k=0,1,2$ are 1.00259, 1.00097 and 0.99928, so the only pure equilibrium has two delegators; with $p=1$ the ratios rise with $k$, 1.48, 1.58 and 1.64, and all delegate [num: DL(a), (b)]. Over 20,000 random draws with $q_L<1$, neither corner is an equilibrium in 0 draws at $p=1$, 1,586 at $p=0.5$ and 60 at $p=0.1$ [num: DL(c)]. Part (1) holds in 20,000 of 20,000 random settings [num: DL(d)]. At $p=1$, over 100,000 draws "neither" never occurs and "both" occurs 4,068 times [num: SM(6)]; an independent recomputation with another sampling distribution finds 0 and 21,922, with the closed forms equal to the direct survival comparison in every draw [num: AL(6.4)]. With $n=2$, $p=1$, $q_L=0.99$, $\psi_0=0.01$ and $\psi_1=0.05$, all delegating is an equilibrium only for $s\ge206.4$, with survival 0.0045 there [num: AL(6.4)]. V6.1's core statement of part (2) omitted $p=1$, without which it is false.

**Remark S6.1 (How certain the lock must be) [thm].** *In the model of Proposition 6.4, not delegating gives strictly higher survival for every $n$, every $p$ and every profile of the others whenever $(1-q_L)(1+s)(1-\psi_1)<1-\psi_0$.* Since $w_N\le w_D\le(1+s)w_N$, the ratio $(1-p+pw_D)/(1-p+pw_N)$ lies between 1 and $1+s$, so $\Phi_D/\Phi_N\le(1-q_L)a(1+s)<1$. The bound is uniform in $n$, $p$ and the profile, holds at equality, and is approached as $n\to\infty$ at $p=1$. For a given $n$ a less certain lock suffices: $w_D/w_N=(1+s)(ks+n)/((k+1)s+n)$ rises with the number $k$ of others delegating, so the binding profile is all others delegating at $p=1$, where the ratio is $(1-q_L)a\big(1+(n-1)s/n\big)$, and $(1-q_L)a\big(1+(n-1)s/n\big)<1$ suffices; at $s=2$ the factor $1+(n-1)s/n$ is 2.0, 2.33, 2.8 and 2.98 for $n=2$, 3, 10 and 100, against the uniform 3 [num: VF]. A lock is near certain in the sense of narrow E1 when its probability of failing is below $(1-\psi_0)/\big((1-\psi_1)(1+s)\big)$: the larger the speed advantage, the nearer one the lock must be for keeping AI out to need no trust.

### Proofs of Lemma 6.5 and Remark 6.6

*Lemma 6.5.* Variants acting on common terms exist and have relative performance lower by at least a positive margin, so under cumulative retention their share tends to zero, as in Proposition 3.4; the retained systems' decisions are the actor's. If constraints are an enforced admission condition, no admissible variant violates them. That an actor which cannot overrule decisions taken in its name has lost O2 in that domain is a definition. For a reader who counts survival only as preservation of the unit, delegation acts through the shared hazard instead: delegated systems interacting at machine speed without a human veto raise the probability of shared loss by reciprocal fear of surprise attack (Schelling 1960), inadvertent escalation (Posen 1991) and flash war (Scharre 2018), an argument with cited mechanisms, not a derivation. $\square$

*Remark 6.6.* A faithful system maximizes its operator's objective. Under a relative payoff that objective puts a weight on winning, equivalent to a self-model $\theta<0$ (Proposition 3.5(3)), and where reinvestment's relative return exceeds its common return the working objective narrows further (Proposition 3.9). Retention by contest performance selects among systems on the same relative payoff. A more effective system raises the value the objective attains, not its content, as $\epsilon$ does in Proposition 6.1. $\square$

### Risk homeostasis in delegation (Section 6.3)

**Proposition S6.2 (Risk homeostasis in delegation) [thm].** Inside the contest, gains in the safety of delegation are partly absorbed by more delegation. *In the model of Proposition 6.2, let each actor choose $\iota_i\in[0,1]$, with strength $1+s\iota_i$ and hazard $\psi_0+\Delta\psi\,\iota_i$; write $x=\Delta\psi\,\iota$, $b_s=\Delta\psi/s$ and $K_h=(n-1)L_e/(n^2L_c)$. An interior symmetric equilibrium solves $(x+b_s)(1-\psi_0-x)^{n-1}=K_h$. (1) Where $x\ll1-\psi_0$, the added hazard is $nx\approx(n-1)L_e/\big(nL_c(1-\psi_0)^{n-1}\big)-n\Delta\psi/s$, nearly independent of $\Delta\psi$, until delegation saturates. (2) There can be several interior equilibria, and along a branch $\partial x/\partial\Delta\psi=-(1-\psi_0-x)/\big(s[1-\psi_0-nx-(n-1)b_s]\big)$, whose sign changes between branches. (3) After saturation, realized risk falls with $\Delta\psi$ and can be summable under full delegation and growing speed: with $n=3$, $L_e=L_c=1$, $\psi_0=0$, $s_t=2^{t+1}$ and $\Delta\psi_t=2^{-(t+1)}$, full delegation is an equilibrium at every $t$, the realized hazard is below one at every $t$, survival from the start is about 0.024, and from $t=4$ the realized risk is at most $3/16$. (4) With all-or-nothing delegation, any equilibrium with $k\ge1$ delegators has realized hazard above the baseline, so an improvement that carries $\Delta\psi$ across the threshold at which delegation becomes an equilibrium raises realized hazard.*

*Proof.* At the symmetric point $\partial w_i/\partial\iota_i=s(n-1)/(n^2(1+s\iota))$, and the marginal cost is $L_c\Delta\psi(1-\psi)^{n-1}$ with $1-\psi=1-\psi_0-x$; equate them and divide by $s$. (1) approximates for $x\ll1-\psi_0$. (2) differentiates implicitly. (3) The own marginal gain at full delegation, $2s_t/(9(1+s_t))-\Delta\psi_t(1-\Delta\psi_t)^2$, is positive at every $t$, and the objective is concave in own delegation, so full delegation is a best response; the realized hazard $1-(1-2^{-(t+1)})^3$ is below one at every $t$, survival from the start is $\prod_{t\ge1}(1-2^{-t})^3$, and the union bound gives $\sum_{t\ge4}3\cdot2^{-(t+1)}=3/16$. (4) $(1-\psi_0-\Delta\psi)^k<(1-\psi_0)^k$. $\square$

*Numerics.* (1): with $n=3$, $s=100$, $L_e=1$, $L_c=100$ and $\psi_0=0.01$, the added hazard is 0.0011, 0.0053, 0.0062, 0.0065 and 0.0067 as $\Delta\psi$ falls from 0.19 through 0.05, 0.02 and 0.01 to 0.005, so on the interior branch it rises as the hazard per unit of delegation falls, and it falls to 0.0030 only at complete delegation, $\Delta\psi=0.001$ [num: AL(6.11)]. (2): with $n=2$, $s=100$, $L_e=0.5$, $L_c=1$, $\psi_0=0.01$ and $\Delta\psi=0.9$ there are two interior roots, $\iota=0.153$ and $0.937$, and the corner is also an equilibrium [num: HO of `../v5/checks_v5.py`, run by `checks_v72.py --legacy`]. (3): the own marginal gain at full delegation is $+0.023$, $+0.037$, $+0.102$, $+0.154$ and $+0.186$ for $t=0,\dots,4$ and rises after, full delegation is a best response on a grid at $t=0,\dots,40$, survival from the start is 0.0241, and the exact realized risk from $t=4$ is 0.1726, against 0.1836 for the sum of the hazards [num: SM(12); AL(6.11)]. V6.1 stated (3) as "an equilibrium for $t\ge5$" with $s_t=2^t$, an example whose hazard at $t=0$ is 1, so that its survival from the start is zero; the shift by one period keeps every hazard below one, as Lemma 5.11 needs. Compare Stafford, Trager and Dafoe (2022) on safety–performance trade-offs in races, and the risk compensation of Peltzman (1975) and Wilde (1982).

### The tendencies of weak incarnation (Section 6.4)

The tendencies the core names have formal sources as well as constructed demonstrations. In some environments optimal policies seek power for most reward functions (Turner et al. 2021), as do decision-makers that are not optimal but can be retargeted (Turner and Tadepalli 2022); trained policies can pursue misgeneralized goals (Langosco et al. 2022). The demonstrations are registered in EV6 with their limits, and those of masking are listed below with the formal part of Remark 6.8.

### Proof of Proposition 6.7

(1) Level 2 is relative and payoff-monotone, so Lemma 3.3(1) removes every term common to all developers.

(2) Level 0 maximizes its reward by definition. Level 1 chooses rewards and constraints to maximize the developer's objective, which counts privately visible divergences through customers, liability and reputation. Under $(A_\tau)$, countering lowers the developer's payoff relative to its matched variant by a positive margin net of every private benefit, and the matched variant is in the support of selection, so under cumulative selection the countering developer's share relative to that variant tends to zero (Proposition 3.4, with developers as carriers and the matched variant as the carrier otherwise identical). Without a matched variant the comparison is between different developers: one whose other advantages exceed the tax grows against unsafe rivals that lack them, for as long as those advantages last (Remark S8.2).

(3) A constraint borne by one developer lowers its relative payoff, which (2) selects against. A constraint enforced on all as a condition of admission is a common term among compliers when its incidence is equal (Lemma 3.3(2)); when unequal, its cost differences are payoff differences among compliers only, and they re-sort the compliers. Each type $k$ of developer can comply at cost $c_k$ or evade against an expected sanction $\bar F_k$. Within a type, evasion does not grow relative to compliance iff $\bar F_k\ge c_k$, and is selected out if the inequality is strict (Lemma 3.3(3)). Across types, $\bar F_k\ge c_k$ for every type suffices but is not necessary: a type whose evasion pays within its type can still lose to another type's compliers. $\square$

*Numerics.*
- *The premise.* A unilateral safeguard that cuts the error rate from 0.20 to 0.01 at cost 0.01 is selected for (its share tends to one), which is the case $(A_\tau)$ excludes; a safeguard whose benefit is common is selected away [num: CI(e); AL(6.7)].
- *Several types* (compliance costs 0.1 and 0.2). With sanctions $(1,1)$ or $(0.15,0.25)$ compliance takes over; with $(1,0.15)$ it also takes over, although the second type's evasion pays within its type, because its evaders earn less than the first type's compliers; with $(1,0.05)$ evasion takes over [num: CI(h)].
- *Incidence.* With unequal compliance costs and sanction 1, the compliant share is 0.999999975 at $t=20$ and the compliers sort 0.88 to 0.12; with equal incidence their relative shares are preserved exactly [num: CI(i)].
- *The matched variant.* A safeguarded developer with baseline 2 and tax 0.1 outgrows an unsafe one with baseline 1 (its share tends to one), so a positive tax alone does not eliminate safeguarding; against its matched twin its share falls to $2\times10^{-9}$ by $t=200$ [num: CI(i)], and to $1.4\times10^{-87}$ by $t=2000$ in a second implementation [num: CI(j)]. With zero tax the shares are unchanged: the safeguard neither spreads nor is removed [num: CI(i)].
- *A tax paid from a lead.* A tax of 0.01 paid from an independent advantage of 0.02 survives; from an advantage of 0.005 it does not [num: CI(j); AL(6.7)].

*Interface.* What level 2 selects on is the developers' relative payoff, of which the trained systems' contribution to relative standing is part (S13).

### The formal part of Remark 6.8

Let admission detect a system that carries the divergence with probability $p_{\rm det}$ and impose a sanction $F$; the variant that does not counter the divergence saves the tax $c_k$. The expected sanction is $\bar F_k=p_{\rm det}F$, so within a type evasion has payoff advantage $c_k-p_{\rm det}F$. With these payoffs fixed, Proposition 6.7(3) gives, by Lemma 3.3(3): the countering variant survives cumulative selection iff $p_{\rm det}F\ge c_k$. With a tax of 0.3, an expected sanction of 0.25 leaves the share of the non-countering variant at one, and one of 0.35 removes it [num: CI(b)].

Masking enters through $p_{\rm det}$. An evaluation that reads behaviour detects a divergence that changes under evaluation, or appears only in some contexts, only as often as it shows there, and that frequency is driven down by training against the evaluation, by context, or by the system's own recognition that it is being evaluated, each shown in constructed settings (EV6): deception that survives safety training (Hubinger et al. 2024), alignment faked when a model takes itself to be in training (Greenblatt, Denison et al. 2024), scheming in context (Meinke et al. 2024), misalignment learned in agentic training that chat-style safety training hides from chat evaluations (MacDiarmid et al. 2025), and covert behaviour that falls with evaluation awareness and rises when that awareness is removed (Schoen et al. 2025). For a fixed positive tax $c_k$ and a fixed finite sanction bound $F_{\max}>0$, detection falling toward zero implies $p_{\rm det}F<c_k$ for every admissible $F$ beyond a finite capability: evasion then has a payoff advantage. A developer's own tests face the same bound, so their failure to detect the divergence cannot establish that the countering it bought removed it. This is a limit of assurance from those tests. An intervention can nevertheless remove the divergence or lower its realized hazard, without these tests verifying that result.

If the tax also changes with capability $K$, the condition for every admissible sanction to leave evasion an advantage is $p_{\rm det}(K)F_{\max}<c_k(K)$. A positive lower bound on $c_k(K)$ makes detection tending to zero sufficient; positivity at each capability alone does not. For example, $p_{\rm det}=e^{-K}$, $F_{\max}=1$ and $c_k=e^{-2K}$ keep the maximum expected sanction above the tax at every $K>0$. Along a changing path, a divergent integral of the eventual payoff advantage gives elimination under cumulative selection, as in Proposition 3.4; a positive sign alone does not guarantee it. In this example, $K(t)=\ln(t+2)$ gives the countering variant an advantage $1/(t+2)-1/(t+2)^2$ whose integral diverges: countering wins under ordinary replicator selection even though detection tends to zero. C9 and C12 therefore jointly decide the moving-path ceiling. An evaluation of propensity can show a tendency present; absence under that evaluation alone does not establish its absence elsewhere.

Hence the direction of the bound. A capability evaluation gives a lower bound, a capability shown, and sandbagging lowers it (EV6), so a rule that admits while shown capability stays below a threshold would be fooled. The bound on capability has to come from channels the behaviour controls less directly: compute, training data and elicitation by fine-tuning (Remark 4.9; S4). White-box evidence, weights and activations, is a second such channel, which an audit can read (Marks et al. 2025). Given a capability bound so obtained, the hazard the system can realize is bounded by a control protocol that is safe against a system of that capability that may be adversarial (Greenblatt, Shlegeris, Sachan and Roger 2024): the bound works through control, not through detection. Beyond the capability at which a protocol is shown safe, that protocol supplies no established assurance case. Neither the uncovered share nor the residual hazard follows from the failure of detection alone: even $p_{\rm det}=0$ can coexist with an independent intervention yielding $h_t=2^{-t-2}$, whose sum is finite and whose limiting survival is positive. Applying Theorem 5.13 requires both (coverage lags) and (residual floor) for this class, and (shared failure) to extend its conclusion to every actor. Absent another assurance case, the protection standard treats the class as uncovered and presumes those premises for the decision, as Section 6.4 states; E2 does not build the class and E3 holds its pace to what control covers [rec]. How $p_{\rm det}$ scales with capability at fixed auditor access is crux C12; audit games with planted hidden objectives (Marks et al. 2025) measure it.

### A worked instance: legible reasoning (Section 6.4)

If legible reasoning lets correctors catch what outputs hide (Korbak et al. 2025; Baker et al. 2025) and opaque reasoning buys capability, the cost of legibility falls on the developer and the benefit mostly on everyone, so legibility is selected away unless it is an admission condition for every eligible system with detectable evasion (Proposition 6.7(3)), and it has to be of roughly equal incidence across architectures if it is not to re-sort them. If the capability gap at fixed compute is negative, legibility pays privately and spreads without coordination; at zero it is neutral under selection, spreading only by the choice of developers that weigh the common term. At the current frontier reinforcement learning has not materially lowered monitorability, and a smaller model at higher reasoning effort can match capability with higher monitorability at higher inference cost (Guan et al. 2025; EV6): a positive tax, paid in compute, so the instance is conditional; its sign at fixed compute is crux C9a.

### Relative selection inside training (Section 6.4)

In a Bradley–Terry model the probability that response $y_1$ is preferred to $y_2$ is $\sigma(R(y_1)-R(y_2))$. Adding the same harm to third parties to both responses leaves it unchanged: 0.7110 with shared harm 0 and with shared harm $-5$ [num: AL(BT)]. If abstaining is among the responses compared and the rater sees the harm, complying (quality 1.3, harm $-5$) is preferred to abstaining (0, 0) with probability 0.024 [num: AL(BT)]. Since only differences enter, a reward fitted to comparisons is identified up to an additive constant for each context, and whether to act at all is not in it.

### The bounded optimizer (Section 6.4)

**Remark S6.3 (Bounded objectives, cheap resources) [thm].** *An optimizer that maximizes $1-e^{-R}-cR$ over the resources $R$ it acquires, at unit cost $c<1$, acquires $R^\ast=\ln(1/c)$, without bound as $c\to0$; a satisficer that stops at success probability $1-\varepsilon_s$ acquires $\ln(1/\varepsilon_s)$, whatever $c$.* The first-order condition is $e^{-R}=c$. With $c=0.1$, 0.01, 0.001 and $10^{-6}$ the optimizer acquires 2.30, 4.61, 6.91 and 13.8, while the satisficer with $\varepsilon_s=0.05$ stays at 3.0 [num: AL(bounded)]. In the contest the same falling cost is harmless to a mind that counts the shared loss: at the symmetric equilibrium of Proposition 3.6(1) with $n=4$, $r=1$, $\Omega=1$ and a shared loss of 0.3 per unit, intensity under $\theta=-1$ rises from 0.25 to 2.5, 25 and 250 as the private cost falls from 1 to 0.1, 0.01 and 0.001; under $\theta=-0.5$ it reaches 1.449, and under $\theta=0$ it rises only from 0.144 to 0.623, since any $\theta>-1$ leaves the term $(1+\theta)\ell$ in the denominator [num: AL(contest)]. A bounded goal plays the part of a bounded prize, not of satiation: what bounds acquisition is a term that counts its cost to others, the analogue of $\theta>-1$, or a rule that stops, the analogue of satiation (Proposition 5.15).

### Correction that people cannot understand (Section 6.5)

**Proposition S6.4 (Correction that people cannot understand) [thm].** A corrector that people cannot follow lowers residual risk wherever it blocks more than it adds, and keeps survival bounded away from zero only if its blocking becomes complete. *Let a correction architecture block a share $c_{b,t}$ of failures at base hazard $h_0\in(0,1)$ and add a residual hazard $h_{c,t}$ of its own in period $t$, with residual hazard $h_0(1-c_{b,t})+h_{c,t}<1$. It lowers residual risk in period $t$ iff $h_0c_{b,t}>h_{c,t}$, and keeps survival bounded away from zero iff $\sum_t\big[h_0(1-c_{b,t})+h_{c,t}\big]<\infty$, that is, iff $\sum_t(1-c_{b,t})$ and $\sum_th_{c,t}$ are both finite; a fixed $c_b<1$ never does.*

*Scope.* Summable residual risk is what keeps survival bounded away from zero (Lemma 5.11). Whether the loss of legibility is a loss of O2 is a separate question, answered by overrulability (Section 6.5).

*Proof.* The residual hazard in period $t$ is $h_0(1-c_{b,t})+h_{c,t}$; compare it with $h_0$. Survival $\prod_t(1-h_t)$ with every $h_t<1$ is bounded away from zero iff $\sum_th_t<\infty$ (Lemma 5.11); both terms are non-negative and $h_0>0$, so the sum is finite iff $\sum_t(1-c_{b,t})$ and $\sum_th_{c,t}$ are, which no fixed $c_b<1$ gives. $\square$

*Example.* With $h_0=0.1$ a period, $c_{b,t}=1-2^{-(t+1)}$ and $h_{c,t}=2^{-(t+2)}$ from $t=0$, survival is 0.5101 at period 200 and at period 20,000, against $7.1\times10^{-10}$ by period 200 without the corrector [num: SM(11)]. A second summable example, $c_{b,t}=1-2^{-t}$ from $t=1$, gives 0.6888 at both horizons [num: SM(11)]. The example printed in V6.1, a fixed $c_b=0.99$ with the same $h_{c,t}$, lowers risk but is not summable: survival to period 200 is 0.473 counting from $t=0$ (0.630 counting from $t=1$, the figure V6.1 printed) and of order $10^{-9}$ by period 20,000 [num: SM(11); AL(6.8)]. V6.1's claim that it made residual risk summable is withdrawn.

### Proof of Proposition 6.9

Each layer covers $\mathsf m$ times the attention of the layer above, so $k=\ln(\mathsf A/\mathsf B)/\ln\mathsf m$ layers are needed, where $\mathsf A$ is the attention the overseen conduct requires and $\mathsf B$ the attention of the top layer. A serial chain catches a problem only if every link succeeds: $p_\ell^k=(\mathsf A/\mathsf B)^{\ln p_\ell/\ln\mathsf m}$. Independent checks across the gap all fail with probability $(1-p_\ell^k)^{k_c}$; a common cause independent of them that defeats all with probability $p_{\rm cc}$ multiplies the catch rate by $1-p_{\rm cc}$. Layered redundancy needs every layer to succeed, each with probability $1-(1-p_\ell)^{k_c}$. A blind spot common to every check, covering a share $\rho_{\rm cc}$ of hazards, is missed whatever the topology, so for hazards that strike at random the catch rate is at most $1-\rho_{\rm cc}$, and an adversary that knows the blind spot and places every attempt in it is never caught. $\square$ (V6.1 wrote the one-layer reliability as $f$, the checks per layer as $m$ and the common-cause probability as $q_{\rm cc}$; $f$, $m$ and $q$ are table symbols.)

*Numerics* ($p_\ell=0.9$, $k=6$). The serial chain catches 0.531; six checks across the gap, 36 layer-checks, catch 0.989, or 0.940 under a common cause with $p_{\rm cc}=0.05$; layered redundancy catches 0.941 with two checks per layer (12 layer-checks) and 0.994 with three (18), so per unit of budget layered redundancy is the better design [num: SM(13); AL(6.9)]. Six independent checks of catch probability 0.9 with a blind spot common to all, covering a share $\rho_{\rm cc}$ of hazards, catch only $(1-\rho_{\rm cc})(1-0.1^6)$ of hazards that strike at random, however the checks are funded or appointed: 1.000, 0.950 and 0.700 at $\rho_{\rm cc}=0$, 0.05 and 0.3 [num: SM(7)]. Against an attacker that strikes in the blind spot when it knows it and at random otherwise, with $\rho_{\rm cc}=0.05$, catch rates are 0.950, 0.475, 0.095 and 0 as the probability that it knows rises through 0, 0.5, 0.9 and 1 [num: AL(6.9)]. Common causes include shared training sources, shared models and shared funders; evaluators' preference for their own lineage's outputs (Panickssery et al. 2024); and collusion between untrusted monitors and the policy they monitor, through self-recognition or coordination signals, which the control literature treats as a design variable (Greenblatt, Shlegeris, Sachan and Roger 2024; Gardner-Challis et al. 2026). Systems of one lineage serving rival principals can also coordinate around them, as pricing algorithms learn to in simulated markets (Calvano et al. 2020; Fish, Gonczarowski and Shorrer 2024). Whether prover–estimator debate (Brown-Cohen et al. 2025) scales is part of crux C3a.

### Prediction horizons under chaos (Section 6.5)

**Remark S6.5 (Prediction horizons under chaos) [thm].** *In a system with largest Lyapunov exponent $\lambda_L>0$, an initial error $\epsilon_{\rm obs}$ reaches a tolerance $\Delta_{\rm tol}$ after about $\lambda_L^{-1}\ln(\Delta_{\rm tol}/\epsilon_{\rm obs})$, so a gain in precision by a factor $g_p$ extends the horizon only by $\lambda_L^{-1}\ln g_p$.* In the logistic map at full chaos, errors of $10^{-3}$ and $10^{-12}$ reach 0.1 after 7.4 and 37.3 steps on average [num: AL(6.10)]. This limits safety cases that predict trajectories, such as "the margin we bought will suffice", not certification of invariants, reachable sets or probabilistic bounds. That AI raises the sensitivity of the systems it acts in is a conjecture [conj].

## S7. The present trajectory

### Proofs of Proposition 7.2, Proposition 7.3 and Corollary 7.4

*Proposition 7.2.* Each item is the cited result read under its branch's condition.

(1) *Branch I.* As $p_d\to1$ with $D_i\ge C_i$, (14) becomes $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-D_i)\le0$, so only profiles with $g_i\le0$, stage equilibria of the objectives held, are sustained (Proposition 5.4(4)). The best-response and joining statements are Proposition 5.1; dissipation is Proposition 5.2; delegation is Proposition 6.2(1) in the additive model of S6, where $L_e/n>L_c\Delta\psi$ suffices for dominance as speed grows, and Proposition 6.4 with $q_L=1$; the failure of proportional correction at $a\tau_c\ge1$ is Proposition 5.12; the motive for preventive attack, under (A8), is Proposition 5.9(1).

(2) *Branch I′.* Above the saturating level further advantage changes no outcome, so a deviation from above it yields no position beyond punishment and $p_d=0$. (14) then reduces to $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-v_i)$, which repetition enforces on the terms of Corollary 5.7. The race channel (Proposition 5.10) and the growth channel (Theorem 5.13) do not pass through $p_d$, so their hazards remain.

(3) *Branch II.* $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$ where punishment is triggered by detection (Remark 5.5), and Proposition 5.4(2) gives enforceability for every actor with $p_d\le\bar p_{d,i}$. In the bargaining model of S5, with A's chance of prevailing falling from 0.5 to 0 over $N$ periods, $c_A=0.05$ and $\delta=0.8$, attack is rational at the start for $N\le3$ and never for $N\ge4$, by backward induction on $V_t=\max(W_t,\delta V_{t+1})$, since at every date the value of fighting now falls below the value of the next offer [num: legacy v4.4]. Modes with $a\tau_c<1$ admit a stabilizing proportional correction (Proposition 5.12). For delegation: in the model of Proposition 6.2, when $k$ others delegate the gain from delegating is $L_e[w_i(1,k)-w_i(0,k)]$, increasing in the speed $s$ and proportional to the loss from defeat $L_e$, while its cost $L_c\Delta\psi(1-\psi_0)^{n-1-k}(1-\psi_1)^k$ does not depend on $L_e$. The speed at which gain and cost are equal therefore rises as $L_e/L_c$ falls, and if the limiting gain falls below the cost for some $k$, delegation never becomes dominant. That a defeat answered before it becomes decisive carries a smaller $L_e$ is the premise of the item.

(4) *Branch III.* Proposition 3.6(1) gives $e(\theta)=r\Omega(n-1-\theta)/\big(n^2(c+(1+\theta)\ell)\big)$, so intensity and shared loss are proportional to $r$; Proposition 5.2(1) gives a dissipated share proportional to $r$; and Proposition 5.10 a hazard proportional to $r_t$. With $p_d=0$ no breakout term enters (14). Low structural decisiveness does not give Branch III, since the channels of (2) add (Proposition 2.2(1)): a capacity channel with gain 0.8 and a rule-support channel with gain 0.3 give $\Gamma=1.1$, and a relative gap of $10^{-4}$ grows to about 4 in 4,000 periods of the nonlinear model [num: LG]; and in the Markov-perfect contests of Remark S2.2, the equilibrium gain is 1.11 at a structural gain of 0.45 and 2.83 at 0.6 [num: MP]. $\square$

*Proposition 7.3.* (1) Write $\pi=\Pr(E)\in(0,1)$. Since $\Pr(W)=\Pr(W\mid E)\pi+\Pr(W\mid\neg E)(1-\pi)<\Pr(W\mid E)$ when $\Pr(W\mid E)>\Pr(W\mid\neg E)$, Bayes' rule gives $\Pr(E\mid W)=\Pr(W\mid E)\pi/\Pr(W)>\pi$. Under cumulative payoff-monotone selection, conduct that raises relative success grows in share round by round (Proposition 3.4), so each round's shift starts from a distribution already shifted. (2) If winning depends on $X$ only through $E$, then $\Pr(X\mid W,E)=\Pr(X\mid E)$ and $\Pr(X\mid W,\neg E)=\Pr(X\mid\neg E)$, so
$$\Pr(X\mid W)-\Pr(X)=\big[\Pr(X\mid E)-\Pr(X\mid\neg E)\big]\big[\Pr(E\mid W)-\Pr(E)\big],$$
which is positive under the premise and part (1). Without the premise the inequality for conduct before victory does not carry to conduct after it; without the conditional independence it can fail even with the premise, since winners among those who do not restrain themselves can differ in $X$ from the rest of that group. (3) After a decisive win, a promise to give a fraction $\beta$ of the prize to the others is worth breaking by $\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/(n-1)$, with no one to sanction it (Proposition 5.8): promises about conduct after a shift in power are not credible to the party that will lose (Fearon 1995; Powell 2006), and uncertainty about intentions compounds the problem (Mearsheimer 2001). An actor that decides by its worst case over a stated set of possibilities, and applies that rule to every action (Gilboa and Schmeidler 1989), counts the worst winner; a survival-first actor need not, and the proposition does not need it to. $\square$

*Corollary 7.4.* (1) Proposition 5.1 gives that restraint is not a best response in the objective to a racing rival when $S_i<P_i$; in Branch I, and in Branch II without timely detection, $p_d$ is not small, and by Proposition 5.4(2) repetition sustains restraint only for an actor with $p_d\le\bar p_{d,i}$, which as $p_d\to1$ with $D_i\ge C_i$ admits only stage equilibria (Proposition 5.4(4)). (2) Proposition 5.8 gives the first half: after a decisive win what everyone else receives is set by the winner's objective alone. Proposition 7.3(2) gives the second for winners that extend dominance; that these are the winners whose objectives give least (Proposition 5.8) is the condition the statement names, where extending dominance goes with giving least. (3) The final step is a judgment of value, not a derivation. That a course which leaves every participant's protection to a winner's objective, and selects winners whose objectives give least, cannot be justified as a collective course rests on V1 (no actor's victory is an adequate criterion of success), V2 (O1 and O2 protected first) and V3 (those affected keep a capacity to contest the direction). The form "safe provided the right actor wins" does not escape it: which actor wins is what (2) says the race selects, and after the win its protection of others rests on its objective alone. $\square$

### Near parity

Section 7.2 states that near parity is where standing is priced highest. The support is the two-actor Markov-perfect contests of Remark S2.2, in which a lead discourages the follower [num: MP; held for outside review]:
- *The price at parity is amplified*: about 1.05 to 7.9 times its structural value across the cases computed (Remark S2.2(3); S3).
- *Summed over the two contestants, the price is higher at parity than at a three-to-one split in every case computed.* With large jumps ($r=1$) at $\Gamma=0.3$ the prices at shares 0.75 and 0.25 are 0.2703 and 0.1003, together 0.371, against $2\times0.1934=0.387$ at parity; at $\Gamma=0.6$, 0.6543 and 0.1065 against $2\times1.1762$; with small jumps ($r=4$) at $\Gamma=0.3$, 0.0856 and 0.0093 against $2\times0.1941$.
- *Not every leader's own price is highest at parity.* In the large-jump case at $\Gamma=0.3$ the leader's price at 0.75 exceeds the price at parity (0.2703 against 0.1934, 153% of the structural value). "Highest at parity" holds for the contest as a whole and for the laggard, not for every leader.
- *Effort is highest at parity, and falls as a lead opens.* In a tug-of-war contest absorbing at a lead of $\pm K$ ($K=3$, $\delta=0.95$), total effort one step from parity is 0.405 of its value at parity and two steps away 0.013; the laggard's effort is 0.138 and then 0.000 of its value at parity.
- *The laggard stops contesting standing*: its price falls, below zero with a shared loss (Remark S2.2(6)).

Where a strong motive to catch up makes the laggard try harder, the strategic correction shrinks and in one computed case reverses (Remark S2.2(5)). The statement of Section 7.2 is therefore about contests in which a lead discourages the follower, and it is held for outside review with the other Markov-perfect results.

### The four loops, formally

Section 7.1 quotes the four loops in the author's words and states their formal correspondence. The table gives, for each, its formal type, the state it runs on, what sets its gain, its delay, where it is shown, and its dual among the strands of Section 10.4.

| Loop | Type | State | Gain set by | Delay | Shown in | Dual (Section 10.4) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Compounding | Reinforcing | Concentration of standing | The capacity terms of (2): $r$, $\omega$, $\beta$, $\delta_f$ | Turnover of capacity, about $1/(d_K+\delta_f)$; a gap's growth factor $\Lambda^\ast$ (Remark S2.1) | Proposition 2.2; Proposition 2.3 | Reward separated from advantage |
| Capture of the rules; adopt or be out | Reinforcing, in parallel with compounding: the gains add | The same state, through rule support | The rule-support terms of (2): $\chi$, $b_{ij}$, the correction of rule support; the incidence of rules | Settling of rule support, about $1/(\varepsilon d)$; spread of a practice at the rate $\zeta$ of (10) | Proposition 2.2(1); Proposition 4.3; Lemma 3.3(2); Definition 4.1 | Correction in common hands, plural and decorrelated |
| The naturalized narrative | Reinforcing [conj] | The formation of minds | Not modelled | Generations | Section 5.4 [conj] | Reasoning instead of winning |
| Regulator lag | Balancing, weakened and delayed | Correction and mobilization | The correction rate $d$ and its effective rate $d_{\rm eff}$, lowered by capture and by the erosion of human capacity | Tempo $\tau_c$, manifestation lags $\tau_e$, masking | Proposition 4.5; Proposition 4.6; Remark 4.7; Proposition 5.12; Proposition 4.8; Remark S2.3 | Pace bound to understanding |
| The drive | Raises the weight on winning in all four | Held self-models and horizons | $\theta_0$ and $\delta$; in part the discounted gain, through $\theta_\Gamma$ | — | Proposition 3.9 | Provision beneath the game |

*The couplings.* Capture lowers the gain of the balancing loop because a captured corrector imposes less and reports less (Proposition 4.5), and the erosion of the human capacity on which correction rests lowers it too (Proposition 4.6). Acceleration, masking and alienated language lengthen its delay: acceleration shortens the time correction has, masking hides the harm that would start it, and alienated language delays the recognition that would mobilize it (Proposition 5.12, Proposition 4.8, Proposition 5.16) [thm, each in its model]. Each consequence of the pressure thus acts as a mechanism of the others.

Because a balancing loop with delay does not subtract from the reinforcing gain (Remark S2.3), the four cannot be summarized by one net gain. What no model here contains is the slowest delay, the formation of minds (S13, slow variables of mind).

### The matrix of shared parameters

Table 7.1 lists the thresholds; this matrix lists the shared parameters against them. A row is an intervention on one parameter. *Held fixed*, unless a row says otherwise: every other parameter of the model that supplies the sign, held self-models, and, for signs that pass through the working self-model, the others' conduct (first order).

*Legend.* ↑: a rise moves the threshold toward its danger side, in the model named in the row's entries. ↓: away from it. 0: no effect in that model. ±: the sign turns on a condition stated in the entries. θ: the sign passes through the working self-model: the parameter moves $\theta$ by Proposition 3.9, first order with the others' conduct fixed, and $\theta$ moves the threshold by its own result; the first step is the interface *loop gain and working objectives*, which Table 7.1 marks †. †: the sign needs an interface that is not proved, named in the entries. —: the parameter does not enter the threshold's model. Columns: Load; Comp., compounding; Stand., standing; Capt., capture; Enf., enforcement; Cov., coverage and tempo, with the race channel; Talk; Tax, the alignment tax; Del., delegation.

| Parameter | Load | Comp. | Stand. | Capt. | Enf. | Cov. | Talk | Tax | Del. |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| P1 sharing $\beta$, as won | — | ↓ | ↓ | — | ↓† | ↓ | ↓θ | — | ↓† |
| P2 sharing $\beta$, promised for after victory | — | 0 | 0 | — | ↑ | — | — | — | — |
| P3 convertible share $\kappa_c$ (capacity) | — | ↑ if $k_0>0$ | ↑ if $k_0>0$ | — | ↑† if $k_0>0$ | — | ↑θ if $k_0>0$ | — | ↑θ if $k_0>0$ |
| P4 provision $k_0$, through capacity | — | ↓ | ↓ | — | ↓† | — | ↓θ | — | ↓θ |
| P5 provision, through a linear level channel | — | 0 | 0 | — | 0 | — | 0 | — | 0 |
