*The entries, row by row.* Each row gives the intervention, any exception to what is held fixed, the model that supplies each sign, and what is not proved.

- **P1, sharing as won.** *Intervention:* a fraction $\beta$ of each period's prize divided equally as it arises. *Signs:* Comp. Proposition S2.4(1); Stand. Proposition 3.9 ($\Gamma_\delta$ scales with $1-\beta$; with a proportional prize the condition is $(1-\beta)r>1$); Enf. Proposition 5.4(2), since the gain from deviating falls by the shared part of what a deviation wins in the period; Cov. Proposition 5.10, which scales the contested stakes by $1-\beta$; Talk through $\theta$ and Proposition 5.16; Del. Proposition 6.2, since the loss from defeat $L_e$ scales by $1-\beta$. *Not proved:* that the gain from deviating and the loss from defeat contain the contested prize in proportion is the interface *stocks and stage payoffs* (below).
- **P2, sharing promised for after victory.** *Intervention:* a promise, made before victory, that the winner will give a fraction $\beta$ of the prize to the others. *Signs:* Enf. Proposition 5.8 with Proposition 5.4(2): after a decisive win the promise is worth breaking by $\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/(n-1)$, so a decisive position is worth that much more against cooperation, $D-C$ rises and $\bar p_d$ falls; Comp. and Stand. 0, since nothing is divided before it is paid. Sharing paid as it arises, or held in common custody as it arises, leaves nothing for a decisive winner to renege on and is neutral for $D-C$. *Not proved:* nothing beyond the model; the result is that a promise without a sanction does not bind.
- **P3, convertible share.** *Intervention:* the share $\kappa_c$ of winnings convertible into capacity. *Signs:* Comp. and Stand. Proposition S2.4(4): in a ratio channel the won share rises with $\kappa_c$ iff $k_0>0$, and without provision $\kappa_c$ sets only the level of capacity (Proposition 2.3); Enf. through the time to decisiveness (Remark S2.1, Remark 5.5); Talk and Del. through $\theta$. *Not proved:* Enf. passes through the interface *loop gain and breakout probability*, and the gap at which a lead becomes decisive is a parameter (C5), not derived. V6.1's entry "↑ for conversion into control" under the capture certificate belongs to conversion into rule support, P9.
- **P4, provision through capacity.** *Intervention:* an inflow $k_0$ to every carrier's capacity, not won in the contest. *Signs:* Comp. Proposition S2.4(3), with a logarithmic channel's gain falling from 1.2857 to 1.0000 as $k_0$ rises from 0.1 to 0.2 [num: CH]; Stand. Proposition 3.9, through the won share, with $1+\theta_\Gamma$ rising from 0.58 to 0.81 as the won share falls from one to a half [num: SP]; Enf. Proposition 5.17(1), the breakout arm, and its pivot $\omega$; Talk and Del. through $\theta$. Provision does not act on absolute growth, on liability or on the correction rate: provision of correction is formula funding, a term of $d$ (P10). *Not proved:* Enf. passes through *loop gain and breakout probability*; that laggards who keep provision also keep the means to sanction, so that $v$ falls, is not shown [conj].
- **P5, provision through a level channel.** *Intervention:* rule support, or any stock whose decisiveness is linear in its level, provided equally to all. *Signs:* Proposition S2.4(3): in a linear level channel provision adds the same to every carrier and changes nothing (a gain of 1.125, unchanged [num: CH]); where decisiveness rises faster than the level ($\xi_r>1$) provision raises the gain (1.75 to 2.25 for a square channel [num: CH]). *Not proved:* nothing. This row is why provision is the lever for capacity and not for the reflexive channel.
- **P6, decisiveness.** *Intervention:* the decisiveness $r$ of the success function. *Signs:* Comp. Proposition 2.2; Stand. Proposition 3.9(1) through $\Gamma_\delta$ and (2) through $(1-\beta)r$; Enf. $p_d$ through $\Gamma$ and $\Lambda^\ast$ (Remark S2.1, Remark 5.5); Cov. Proposition 5.10, $h_t\propto r_t$; Talk and Del. through $\theta$. *Not proved:* Enf. passes through *loop gain and breakout probability*, and that the gain from deviating and the decisive value rise with $r$ through *stocks and stage payoffs*. Capt.: a higher $r$ concentrates shares, and influence is selected with the square of share (Proposition 4.3), but the link from the contest's concentration to the field model's shares is the interface *reflexive gain off parity* (S13): one form, not one theorem.
- **P7, diffusion of what is not decisive.** *Intervention:* the rate $\delta_f$ at which knowledge, methods and services diffuse toward the mean. *Signs:* Comp. and Stand. Proposition S2.4(1); with a proportional prize, the narrowing threshold rises to $(1-\beta)r>1+\delta\delta_f/(1-\delta(1-d_K))$, below the threshold of instability (Proposition S3.2); Enf. through $\Lambda^\ast$ (Remark S2.1), and with diffusion a gap can settle short of decisive; Talk and Del. through $\theta$. A measure that blocks diffusion to keep a lead raises the gain it means to exploit (Section 2.2). *Not proved:* Enf. passes through *loop gain and breakout probability*.
- **P8, diffusion of decisive-class capability or of weights.** *Intervention:* diffusion of capability of the decisive class, or release of weights. *Signs:* Comp. and Stand. as P7, since it restores laggards; Enf. ±: leads decay faster, which lowers each actor's $p_d$, but (14) must then hold for every actor that holds the capability, and the race begins if any one of them prefers it (Proposition 5.1); Tax ↑, since admission ends at release and released weights escape it (Table 9.1, R8a). *Not proved:* nothing; where the boundary between the two kinds of diffusion lies is a question of design (Section 10).
- **P9, appropriability and conversion.** *Intervention:* the appropriable fraction $\chi$ of rule support and the conversions $b_{ij}$ of success into it. *Signs:* Capt. Proposition 4.3, Proposition 4.5(1); Comp. and Stand. Proposition 2.2(1) and Proposition S2.4(4), the level channel, whose term adds to the gain. *Not proved:* Load: Lemma 3.3(3) gives tipping where the expected sanction falls as evaders spread, and Section 4.1 writes captured rule-making as $\tau_{\rm eff}=\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})$, but the join of the field model to the load model is an interface. Enf.: captured correctors raise $v$, and $\bar p_d$ falls in $v$ (Proposition 5.4(2); Proposition 5.17(3)); that they do is the interface, with no joint model [conj]. Cov.: captured assessment is not legible, overrulable, exercised and resourced, so it does not count toward $U_t$, but the production relation between correction and coverage is the open interface *correction rate and coverage*. Talk: captured indicators raise masking (Proposition 4.8), through *masking and alarms*. Tax: the sanction on evasion of an admission constraint is itself subject to capture (Proposition 6.7(3), Lemma 3.3(3)), and masking rises with the share of evaluation done by the evaluated lineage, which capture raises; Proposition 4.8 takes masking as given (*the outer training loop*). Del.: captured evaluation that reports delegation safe makes capture hysteretic (Proposition 4.6(2)), one simulation [num: QS]. Comp. off parity: the reflexive channel's gain is higher at the present concentration than at parity (*reflexive gain off parity*).
- **P10, correction of rule support.** *Intervention:* the correction rate $d$ of the field model (10), the level channel of (2). *Signs:* Capt. Proposition 4.5(1): certified iff $d>d^\ast$, with $G_{ij}\propto1/d_i$; Comp. and Stand. Proposition S2.4(2), with a linear channel's gain falling from 1.125 to 0.750 as $d$ rises from 0.2 to 0.3 [num: CH]; Talk through $\theta$, and captured indicators corrected. *Not proved:* Load, Enf., Cov., Tax and Del. as in P9, with the opposite sign.
- **P11, turnover of capacity.** *Intervention:* the depreciation $d_K$ of capacity in (1), a ratio channel. *Signs:* Comp. Proposition S2.4(2): unchanged without diffusion, rising with it (0.857 to 0.964 as $d_K$ rises from 0.2 to 0.3 at $\delta_f=0.1$ [num: CH]); Stand. Proposition S2.4(2): $\Gamma_\delta$ always rises (0.853, 1.157 and 1.409 at $d_K=0.1$, 0.2 and 0.4 with $\Gamma$ unchanged [num: CH]); Enf. ±: by Remark S2.1 the gap multiplier $1+(d_K+\delta_f)(\Gamma-1)$ rises with $d_K$ when $\Gamma>1$, so leads open faster, and falls when $\Gamma<1$; Talk and Del. through $\theta$. Capture, talk's indicators and coverage are not moved: a shared symbol is not a shared mechanism, and this $d$ is not the field model's (*correction across models*). *Not proved:* Enf. passes through *loop gain and breakout probability*. V6.1 merged this row with the correction of rule support and gave it the wrong sign.
- **P12, a falling cost of control.** *Intervention:* the rate $\alpha_{\rm ctl}$ at which AI cheapens control capacity, under $[H_\gamma]$. *Signs:* Capt. Proposition 4.5(2), the rate law. *Not proved:* every other column passes through capture as in P9, and Talk also through the masking by the evaluated model, $m_{\rm model}$ (EV6) [obs]; Cov. is ± because AI also cheapens independent correction, which keeps pace where it grows at least $1/(1+\eta)$ as fast and is independent of the evaluated lineage (Section 4.3), a question symmetric with (coverage lags) (C3); and the interface *capacity and the field* is quasi-static (Proposition S4.2).
- **P13, a per-unit cost floor.** *Intervention:* a per-unit cost $f$ of capacity that AI does not lower. *Signs:* Capt. Proposition 4.5(3): capacity is cultivated only while $d<d_f$, so the requirement levels off at $\max\{A/c_{\rm ev},d_f\}$. *Not proved:* nothing.
- **P14, erosion of human corrective capacity.** *Intervention:* the rate $\delta_h$ at which delegation erodes the human capacity on which correction and decisions rest. *Signs:* Capt. Proposition 4.6(1), the threshold of the rate law raised by $w\delta_h$ [thm], and (2) [num: QS]; Del. Lemma 6.3: after $\ln(\mathcal M_0/\mathcal M_{\min})/\delta_h$ of delegation, reverting means operating below $\mathcal M_{\min}$. *Not proved:* Capt. holds for the form of $d_{\rm eff}$ that Proposition 4.6 assumes, a modelling choice inside the interface *human capacity and correction*; Cov.: coverage counts only decisions that are resourced and exercised (Section 5.3), which human capacity supplies, but the production relation between correction and coverage is open.
- **P15, stakes with provision fixed.** *Intervention:* the stakes $\Omega$ rise while $k_0$ stays, so the won share $\omega$ rises. *Signs:* Comp. and Stand. through $\omega$ (Proposition 2.3, Proposition 2.2, Proposition 3.9); Enf. ↑ on $p_d$ through $\omega$, the breakout arm of Proposition 5.17, and on $\bar p_d$ the danger rises iff the gain from deviating and the decisive value grow faster than $C-v$, since $\bar p_d$ is homogeneous of degree zero in $(g,C,D,v)$, the stakes arm; Cov. Proposition 5.10, $h_t\propto\Omega_t$; Talk through $\theta$, and contested stakes raise the material bias $b_0$ (Proposition 5.16); Del. Proposition 6.2, as the loss from defeat grows against the shared loss of control. *Not proved:* Enf. passes through *loop gain and breakout probability* and *stocks and stage payoffs*; Cov. on the growth channel needs the premise (variety grows); Capt.: the return to influence scales with the value of rule support ((11)), which rises with the stakes only if that value does [prem].
- **P16, stakes growing with capacity.** *Intervention:* a prize proportional to total capacity, $\Omega=gS$. *Signs:* Stand. Proposition 3.9(2): ↑ if $(1-\beta)r>1$, ↓ if $(1-\beta)r<1$, without provision or diffusion; Comp. Proposition 2.3: equal shares are unstable iff $\kappa_cg\big((1-\beta)r-1\big)>\delta_f$; Enf., Talk and Del. through $\theta$, with the sign of Stand. *Not proved:* away from the balanced path the two returns vary over time and are not closed (S3).
- **P17, breakout probability and detection lag.** *Signs:* Proposition 5.4(1), (2); Remark 5.5, where a longer lag lowers $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$. *Not proved:* nothing.
- **P18, the decisive value against cooperation.** *Signs:* Proposition 5.4(2): $\bar p_d$ falls with $D$. *Not proved:* nothing.
- **P19, patience.** *Signs:* Comp. 0, since $\Gamma$ does not depend on $\delta$ (Proposition 2.2); Stand. ↑ with a fixed prize, as $\Gamma_\delta$ rises toward $\Gamma$, and ± with a common return, as $\Gamma_\delta$ against $\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta$ (Proposition 3.9); Enf. ±: the sanction $\delta(1-p_d)(C_i-v_i)$ grows with $\delta$ in (14) while, with a fixed prize, the working objective narrows; Talk and Del. through $\theta$, with a fixed prize. *Not proved:* the narrowing side of Enf. passes through $\theta$.
- **P20, a wider held self-model.** *Signs:* Stand. Proposition 3.9: a wider held model gives a wider working one at every $\Gamma_\delta$; Enf. Proposition 5.4(2) through the objective payoffs, with restraint a stage equilibrium at $\theta\ge A/B$ in Remark S5.1 (Test G); Talk Proposition 5.16(1), the effective bias falls; Del. Proposition 6.2, through the weight on the shared loss; Cov. on the race channel, through $n-1-\theta$ (Proposition 5.10); Load Proposition 3.7, for strategic actors, and a point of enough in accounting at $\theta>-1$ (Proposition 3.5(5)); selection on conduct (Proposition 3.4) is unaffected. *Not proved:* nothing.
- **P21, variety, coverage and residual floor.** *Signs:* Theorem 5.13, Proposition 5.15, Proposition 5.14, Proposition 5.12. *Not proved:* the premises (variety grows), (coverage lags) and (residual floor) are the central crux (Section 12), and their join to survival is the interface *coverage and residual risk*.
- **P22, the speed gain of delegation.** *Signs:* Del. Proposition 6.2. *Not proved:* Enf.: escalation at machine speed shortens the window in which detection and response can act, so $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$ falls; there is no model, only cited mechanisms (S6) [conj].
- **P23, the alignment tax.** *Signs:* Tax Proposition 6.7 under $(A_\tau)$, class by class (C9); where the tax is negative the safeguard spreads without coordination, and where it is zero it is neutral under selection, spreading only by the choice at level 1 of developers that weigh the common term. *Not proved:* level 2 selects on relative payoff (*the outer training loop*).

*Reading the matrix.* Every sign is proved in the model named; where the entries name no interface, the parameter and the threshold live in one model and the arrow is proved inside it. Where they name one, the arrow is proved inside its model and the join is a hypothesis, which is why Table 7.1 marks those cells †. Three joins carry most of the weight. *Loop gain and breakout probability*, through which compounding reaches the enforcement condition, with $T_{\rm dec}$ set by $\Lambda^\ast$ and $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$; the reduced joint model of S5 exercises it [num: JX]. *Loop gain and working objectives*, through which compounding reaches $\bar p_d$, talk and delegation, first order with the others' conduct fixed, and amplified where rivals respond (Remark S2.2). *Stocks and stage payoffs*, new in this matrix: the assumption that a change in the stakes or the sharing of the stock model (1) moves the stage payoffs $g_i$, $D_i$, $C_i$ of (14) and the loss from defeat of Proposition 6.2 in the same direction. It is stated as an assumption because no model in this paper derives the stage games of Sections 5.2 and 6.3 from the stock dynamics of Section 2.2. That the parameters move together in the world, so that growth moves several thresholds at once, is C0, which Tests Γ and E address.

### The mechanism index

The loops of Section 7.1 act through mechanisms that overlap. Each is stated separately because each has its own formal anchor, measure, counter-mechanism and analogue in other substrates. They concern three objects that reinforce one another without being interchangeable: what actors value (accounting and boundary), what actors believe (observation, attribution and language), and what institutions reward, permit and protect (compounding, selecting the selectors, decisiveness and tempo).

Some overlaps can be named. Masking is selecting the selectors applied to observation. Decisiveness is reached faster where the loop gain is larger and the stocks turn over faster (Remark S2.1: $\Gamma$ decides the sign of a gap's growth, $\Lambda^\ast$ its speed), while whether a position beyond sanction exists at all is a separate condition that no gain decides. The alienation of language is accounting applied to talk, together with selecting the selectors applied to narratives. The drive is partly the price of standing felt from inside, which provision lowers (Proposition 3.9), and partly held, where whether provision lowers it is C8. Tempo decides whether any counter-mechanism arrives in time, and the drive how hard every mechanism is pushed.

## S8. Comparing paths

### Proof of Lemma 8.2

Couple the two paths by a common uniform draw $\mathsf u_t$ each period: the event occurs on a path at $t$ when $\mathsf u_t$ falls below that path's conditional hazard given the shared world history. On every history on which both have survived to $t$, the two conditions give $A$'s hazard at most $C$'s, so $\{A\text{ fails at }t\}\subseteq\{C\text{ fails at }t\}$. By induction the event "$C$ survives to $H$" is contained in "$A$ survives to $H$", path by path, whatever the rule by which $A$ dissolves, so $\Phi^A(H)\ge\Phi^C(H)$. $\square$

*A step that fails.* Comparing after conditioning on the dissolution date, $\Phi^A(H\mid\tau)\ge\Phi^C(H)$ for every $\tau$, is not valid when dissolution depends on the state. In a two-state Markov world in which bad states make dissolution more likely, the conditional comparison fails at $H=30$ for dissolution at $t=1$ (0.291 against 0.324), while the coupled comparison holds at horizons 10, 30 and 100 [num: SM(4)]; an exact dynamic programme over the world state, the arrangement's status and both survivals, with a common uniform, gives $\Phi^A=0.884$, 0.551 and 0.077 against $\Phi^C=0.813$, 0.461 and 0.062 at those horizons [num: SM(14)]. If $C$'s hazard is a deterministic schedule, the two conditions need only be stated on $A$'s histories. *Example.* Let continuation's hazard be $h_c(t)=0.05(0.2+0.01t)+0.001$, the arrangement's own hazard 0.0015 a period, dissolution 2% a period, and the fallback a restart of continuation ten periods back, with hazard $h_c(\max(t-10,0))$ after dissolution. The arrangement survives with probability 0.976, 0.864 and 0.211 at horizons 10, 30 and 100, against 0.875, 0.575 and 0.026 for continuation [num: FD].

### The discounting comparison

With utility linear in consumption, discount rate $\rho$, a constant race hazard $h$ and growth rates $g_r$ racing and $g_p$ paced (pacing taken as hazard-free), the race is worth $c_0/(\rho+h-g_r)$ and pacing $c_0/(\rho-g_p)$, each finite where its denominator is positive, so the race is preferred iff $h<g_r-g_p$, though it ends in catastrophe with certainty; a rising hazard reverses the preference only if it comes to exceed the growth differential [thm]. This is our derivation. It is the special case of Jones's (2024) trade-off, which runs a growth-raising technology until $v(c^\ast)=g/h$, with $h$ the hazard (his $\delta$) and $v(c)=u(c)/(u'(c)c)$ the value of a year of life relative to consumption, in which the value of a year of life does not rise with consumption. With curvature $\gamma\ge1$ it does, and Jones's planner stops: at a 1% annual hazard and 10% growth, with logarithmic utility after about 40 years, at incomes about 55 times higher and about a one-in-three cumulative risk; with $\gamma=2$, after about 4.5 years at about 4%; at a 2% hazard, with logarithmic utility, at once. Jones also finds that mortality improvements from AI raise the acceptable risk, to about 25% for $\gamma=2$ or 3 in his richer model with a one-time risk, which belongs to crux C10, and that without them a lower social discount rate lowers the acceptable risk toward zero [obs: EV11]. The protection standard does not discount the protected losses at all, by choice (N1).

### Survival at a common horizon

**Proposition S8.1 (Assurance in survival terms at a common horizon) [thm].** Comparing by survival classifies the game between restraint and racing by two signs, and the classification can change with the horizon. *Survival is computed with per-period conditional probabilities over a common horizon $H$. Let $h_R$ be the hazard under restraint, $q_{\rm I}$ the loss of the ability to overrule by delegation, $q_{\rm II}$ the shared hazard (with superscripts for the branch on which it is evaluated), $q_W$ the hazard of preventive attack, $p_{\rm dec}$ and $p^S_{\rm dec}$ the probability that a racer's advantage becomes decisive when all race and when the actor stays out, and $p_{\rm ext}$ the actor's belief that a decisive winner would extend its dominance over it. With independence, $\Phi^R=1-h_R$, $\Phi^T=(1-q_{\rm I})(1-q^T_{\rm II})(1-q_W)$, $\Phi^P=(1-q_{\rm I})(1-q^P_{\rm II})(1-q_W)\big(1-p_{\rm dec}\frac{n-1}n\big)$ and $\Phi^S=(1-q^S_{\rm II})(1-p^S_{\rm dec}p_{\rm ext})$.*
1. *The advantage of restraint is $\varpi(\Phi^R-\Phi^T)+(1-\varpi)(\Phi^S-\Phi^P)$: dominance if both differences are non-negative; a stag hunt with threshold $(\Phi^P-\Phi^S)/\big((\Phi^R-\Phi^T)+(\Phi^P-\Phi^S)\big)$ if only the first is positive; chicken if only the second is; a prisoner's dilemma if neither.*
2. *Where $\Phi^S\ge\Phi^P$ and $\Phi^R\ge\Phi^T$, an actor that holds the prediction restrains whatever it believes about the others.*
3. *Mutual racing is a strict equilibrium at every horizon $H$ with $\Phi^P_H>\Phi^S_H$.*
4. *For constant per-period hazards $q_B$ on a branch and $q_R$ under the arrangement, $\Phi^B(H)/\Phi^R(H)=\big((1-q_B)/(1-q_R)\big)^H$.*
5. *If an actor gives the premises of Section 5.3 probability $p_{\rm prem}$, and the branches of the race have survivals $T_\neg$, $P_\neg$, $S_\neg$ when those premises fail, the classification at horizon $H$ uses the mixtures, and when the arrangement's own survival also decays the signs of (1) can fail at long horizons.*

*Proof:* the advantage is linear in $\varpi$; (2)–(5) by substitution. $\square$

*Numbers* ($n=2$, $p_{\rm ext}=1$) [num: legacy v4.4, SV, recomputed from the closed forms for V6.2; (5) legacy v5, SR].
- With $q_{\rm I}=0.6$, $q^T_{\rm II}=q^S_{\rm II}=0.3$, $q^P_{\rm II}=0.4$, $q_W=0.2$, $p_{\rm dec}=p^S_{\rm dec}=0.6$ and $h_R=0.1$: $\Phi^R=0.90$, $\Phi^T=0.22$, $\Phi^P=0.13$ and $\Phi^S=0.28$, and restraint is dominant.
- With low delegation ($q_{\rm I}=0.05$, every shared hazard 0.1, $q_W=0.1$, $p_{\rm dec}=p^S_{\rm dec}=0.3$, $h_R=0.1$), the game is a stag hunt with threshold 0.16.
- For a leader that wins with probability 0.8 if it races, so that when both race the rival's decisive win has probability $0.2\,p_{\rm dec}$, and whose exit hands decisive advantage to the rival ($q_{\rm I}=0.2$, $q_W=0.2$, $p_{\rm dec}=0.9$, $p^S_{\rm dec}=0.95$, $h_R=0.1$): a stag hunt with threshold 0.42 when every shared hazard is 0.3, and 0.11 when it is 0.8. For a leader whose exit would hand a rival a decisive win, the shared hazard is what carries the case for restraint.
- With $p_{\rm prem}=0.99$, the arrangement's survival $e^{-0.005H}$, race branches decaying at rate 0.05 when the premises hold and $(T_\neg,P_\neg,S_\neg)=(0.9,0.4,0.1)$ when they fail, the arrangement's survival exceeds the mixed survival of racing against restrainers at $H=10$, 100 and 600, by 0.34, 0.59 and 0.04; at $H=1{,}000$ it does not, and racing becomes dominant.

### The alternatives: results stated briefly in the core

**Remark S8.2 (What a lead buys).** (1) With diffusion at rate $\delta_f$, a lead $\Delta_0$ held by a leader that pauses obeys $\dot\Delta=-\delta_f\Delta-1$ and is exhausted after $\delta_f^{-1}\ln(1+\delta_f\Delta_0)<\Delta_0$: a six-month lead diffusing at 0.1 a month lasts 4.7 months, a one-year lead 7.9 months, a two-year lead 12.2 months [num: legacy v4.5, LD]. (2) The margin is time, but what it must buy is coverage, which rises by about $\dot U\Delta$ while uncovered variety grows; for hazard classes with an evidence lag above $\Delta$ it buys nothing (Proposition 5.12). (3) Whether it suffices is judged by the leader's own evaluators, under time pressure. (4) In a static contest the follower raises effort when the leader slows (Dixit 1987); in dynamic races a large lead discourages the follower (Harris and Vickers 1987; Konrad and Kovenock 2009), and a leader seeing its window close has a motive to strike first (Van Evera 1999). A developer able to commit first to a slightly slower speed gains, because the others then slow too, a local and Pareto-improving result that needs no lead (Bueno de Mesquita, Dziuda and Polborn 2026). On their own median estimates, the planners of one detailed scenario judge that spending part of a lead buys about a third (0.32) of the reduction in takeover risk that a coordinated slowdown buys [obs: EV10]. *Proof of (1):* $\dot\Delta=-\delta_f\Delta-1$ with $\Delta(0)=\Delta_0$ reaches zero at $\delta_f^{-1}\ln(1+\delta_f\Delta_0)$, which is below $\Delta_0$ since $\ln(1+x)<x$. $\square$

**Remark S8.3 (The finite sprint, by mechanism).** A finite sprint has two continuations. In *relinquishment*, the sprinter freezes or rolls back its own capability and accepts losing superiority as rivals catch up. Its exposure after freezing is zero in Proposition 5.14 only if the hazardous ratio built is at most one; freezing one actor does not bound other actors' hazards under shared failure. The sprint still faces exposure during construction, preventive responses from rivals uncertain of its intention, credible performance of the promised relinquishment, and correction independent of its holder. Those burdens do not make it a prohibition imposed on rivals. Excluding it as a deviation from an arrangement requires its net deviation payoff to be negative (Proposition S10.3), and rejecting it as a policy requires a comparison of its actual hazards and fallback. In *permanent monopoly*, the holder additionally stops rivals' growth to preserve its edge. This adds the unequal incidence of an imposed freeze: by Lemma 3.3(2), (3), constrained actors evade wherever evasion pays, so enforceable sanctions must cover their gains; removing their effective authority violates O2. In either branch the large holder is a conversion target, since influence returns scale with the square of share (Proposition 4.3) and cultivated conversion with a high moment of shares (Proposition 4.5(4)); independent correction must be demonstrated rather than ruled impossible by definition. E2 and common custody propose an equal stop on the decisive class, including custodians' own capability, and separately held correction. Whether their constraints, correctors and pre- or post-construction fallback work better than credible relinquishment is open. X8 compares the branches on D4 and D5.

**Proposition 8.5 in full.** *Facing a rival it believes certain to race, comparing by its own survival: (1) a single strike that escalates to catastrophe with probability $\varepsilon_P$, and otherwise succeeds with probability $s_P$ in returning the field to something like the arrangement and leaves the race as it was if it fails, gives $(1-\varepsilon_P)\big[s_P\Phi^R+(1-s_P)\Phi^P\big]$, and beats both staying out and joining iff $\varepsilon_P<1-\max(\Phi^S,\Phi^P)/\big(s_P\Phi^R+(1-s_P)\Phi^P\big)$; (2) sustained prevention against a target that rebuilds with probability at least $b_P$ a period, each strike escalating with probability at least $\varepsilon_{\min}$, has per-period hazard at least $b_P\varepsilon_{\min}$; (3) at common horizons, sustained prevention with hazard 0.01 beats racing with hazard 0.05: 0.90 against 0.60 at $H=10$, 0.60 against 0.08 at $H=50$, 0.13 against 0.00 at $H=200$.* *Proof:* (1) compare and solve; as $\Phi^S,\Phi^P\to0$ with $s_P\Phi^R>0$ the bound tends to one. When $\Phi^S$ and $\Phi^P$ fall in proportion, by a common factor $\lambda$, $\max(\Phi^S,\Phi^P)/(s_P\Phi^R+(1-s_P)\Phi^P)$ is increasing in $\lambda$, so the bound rises; it need not rise when one branch falls alone: with $\Phi^S>\Phi^P$, lowering $\Phi^P$ lowers it ($\Phi^S=0.5$, $\Phi^R=0.9$, $s_P=0.5$: 0.231 at $\Phi^P=0.4$, 0.167 at 0.3) [num: VF]. (2) Lemma 5.11. (3) $0.99^H>0.95^H$. $\square$ With $\Phi^R=1$, $s_P=0.7$, $\varepsilon_P=0.2$ and the race branches near zero it gives 0.56 [num: legacy v5, FS]. Sustained prevention is compared with racing by its hazard, not by the event: both end in catastrophe over an unbounded horizon, and at a common finite horizon the lower hazard survives better. Shifts that cannot be observed raise the chance of mistaken prevention (Debs and Monteiro 2014); verification that supports deterrence can also supply targets (Coe and Vaynman 2020). An actor that manipulates shared risk as an instrument (Schelling 1966) plays chicken in survival terms, with mixed equilibria and wars of attrition. When the probability that a capable rival holds an unbounded relative objective is low, a survival-first actor's best course is to propose verifiable limits and take narrow E1 verifiably in its own loops, the costly signal of Proposition 11.1.

**Remark S8.4 (Why insuring against falling behind is not an exit).** Bounding the penalty for falling behind with autonomous, good-enough provision and defence does not leave the contest, for three reasons. The penalty is set by the winner, not the laggard, so the bound is only as reliable as the belief that a winner would not extend its dominance. A winner with a relative objective has a motive to destroy what the laggard secured, since its standing rises by the laggard's loss ((7)). And insurance accepts the frame of the contest: it hedges against losing instead of declining the game, which is the reply that the ruling of Section 3.8 rejects, since against a winner of that logic falling behind cannot be made survivable. What would leave the contest is different in kind. Defence that makes decisive advantage unattainable for everyone lowers $r$ for all and belongs to E2, provided it stays under human oversight. Provision held by no one and supplied beneath the game lowers the won share for every actor (G5), and belongs to a world that has left the contest; the same provision held by one actor against its rivals is a position in it.

### Proofs for the verified agreement

**Proposition 8.6 in full.** *Payoffs $R,T,P,S$; a defection detected in time is reversed, the defector receiving $P-F$ and the cooperator $P$; undetected defection yields $T$ and $S$. With $T_\upsilon=\upsilon(P-F)+(1-\upsilon)T$, $g_C=R-T_\upsilon$ and $g_D=(1-\upsilon)(P-S)$: (1) the advantage of cooperating is $\varpi g_C-(1-\varpi)g_D$, with the three cases of the core and, if both are negative, chicken; (2) where $T-P+F>0$, $g_C>0$ iff $\upsilon>(T-R)/(T-P+F)$, and where $T<R$, $g_C>0$ at $\upsilon=0$; (3) cooperation is risk-dominant iff $g_C>g_D$, a property of payoffs selected under global games and adaptive play (Carlsson and van Damme 1993; Kandori, Mailath and Rob 1993; Young 1993); (4) with $n$ players and independent belief $\varpi_1$ about each rival, $\varpi_1^{n-1}\ge\varpi^\ast=g_D/(g_C+g_D)$ suffices only if a cooperator gets at least $S$ when some but not all others defect and the advantage of defecting in any such state is at most $g_D$.* *Proof:* (1) cooperating yields $\varpi R+(1-\varpi)\big(\upsilon P+(1-\upsilon)S\big)$, defecting $\varpi T_\upsilon+(1-\varpi)P$; their difference is $\varpi g_C-(1-\varpi)g_D$. (2) $g_C=(R-T)+\upsilon(T-P+F)$; rearrange. (4) Bound the intermediate states. Without the second bound the condition fails: with advantages $(1,-10,-1)$ by number of defecting others in a three-player game, each rival cooperating with probability $\sqrt{0.5}$ gives $-3.73$ although all others cooperate with probability 0.5 [num: legacy v5, N13]. $\square$ The threshold in (2) is the deterrence condition of the inspection game (Avenhaus, von Stengel and Zamir 2002); what this paper adds is its identity with the enforcement condition and the comparative statics of both under growth. Read with its payoffs as continuation values, cooperation $R=C_i$, a deviation that becomes decisive $T=(1-\delta)(C_i+g_i)+\delta D_i$ and one detected and answered $P-F=(1-\delta)(C_i+g_i)+\delta v_i$, it equals $1-\bar p_{d,i}$ of the enforcement condition, as S5 shows for Remark 5.5 [identity: JX]; the two differ in what they take as given, the sanction $F$ and trust here, a credible continuation computed with breakouts possible there.

**Proposition S8.5 (Commitment under a growing overhang) [thm].** An agreement that holds growing capability in check needs a sanction that keeps pace with the net gain from exit, and preserving relative positions alone does not remove their contribution to the gain from exit. *Let $\mathcal O(t)$ be the capability an agreement holds in check, $V^{\rm exit}$ and $V^{\rm stay}$ the values of exiting first and of staying, exit occur at hazard $\mathcal H$, a fraction $\mu_x$ of the overhang be destroyed on exit, and the expected loss from exit be $\mathcal L=\mathcal H(1-\mu_x)\Lambda_x(\mathcal O)$ with $\Lambda_x$ increasing. (1) Deterring an irreversible, public exit requires $\upsilon F\ge V^{\rm exit}-V^{\rm stay}$; deterring a reversible covert preparation requires $\upsilon F\ge(1-\upsilon)(V^{\rm exit}-V^{\rm stay})$; the net gain from exit grows with the overhang when leaving with a decisive advantage gains faster than staying with one's share. (2) $\dot{\mathcal L}/\mathcal L=\dot{\mathcal H}/\mathcal H-\dot\mu_x/(1-\mu_x)+\varepsilon_\Lambda\dot{\mathcal O}/\mathcal O$, with $\varepsilon_\Lambda$ the elasticity of $\Lambda_x$, so growth of the overhang faster than the decline of the exit hazard is not sufficient for the loss to rise. (3) An agreement that preserves relative positions preserves the relative term of every payoff. That term alone does not establish a positive net gain from exit or defeat an effective sanction.* *Proof:* (1) expected values. (2) Differentiate $\ln\mathcal L$; counter-case: $\mathcal O=e^{2t}$, $\mathcal H=e^{-1.5t}$, $\Lambda_x=\sqrt{\mathcal O}$ and $\mu_x=0$ give $\mathcal L=e^{-0.5t}$. (3) Proportional caps leave each share $w_i$ unchanged. $\square$

*Theorem 8.7, by part.* (1) By (2) of Proposition 8.6, the required $\upsilon$ is $(T-R)/(T-P+F)$, which tends to one as $T\to\infty$ with $R$, $P$ and $F$ fixed. (2) Timely detection here means detection followed by an effective response before decisiveness: $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$ (Remark 5.5), $\upsilon$ is non-decreasing in the time allowed, and $T_{\rm dec}$ falls as the loop gain rises (Remark S2.1). (3) Bounding the largest undetected defection bounds $T$, since a defection that cannot become decisive cannot earn a decisive payoff. The bound needs the coalition's effective compute to stay above the decisive ratio, an identity of the definition; effective compute is algorithmic efficiency times compute, so a coalition that caps compute keeps its bound only while its compute advantage grows as fast as diffusion erodes its algorithmic one. Nothing here derives that it does: the clause is the premise (advantage keeps pace), and the rate of erosion is an observation (EV9). Revealing types is the information role of Proposition S10.3. The Scope collects Proposition 5.16 (unverifiable information), Proposition S8.5 (future commitments) and Section 4 (the reliability of the verifiers, who are correctors that success can buy). $\square$

*A conditional endpoint.* Keeping private objectives and the decisive prize unchanged does not by itself violate (14). With $\delta=0.9$, $g=0.1$, $C=1$, $D=100$, $v=0$ and maintained $p_d=0.001$, its left side is $0.0991$ and its right side $0.8991$; the breakout bound is $0.89/90\approx0.00989$. This is substitution into the condition, not an estimate of feasibility. If the bounds hold for every relevant deviation and the arrangement also maintains independent correction, coverage and the other applicable requirements, verified containment can be a durable endpoint. Whether any real arrangement can maintain those bounds is C4/C7; neither this example nor Theorem 8.7 proves that it can.

*An instance.* One detailed plan among leading powers to slow development and delay superintelligence to about 2040 combines compute declarations, supply-chain accounting, inference-only retrofits and recomputation of workloads, research transparency, mutually assured destruction of compute if the deal declines, and hardened reserves of about two per cent of pre-deal compute; it bounds covert projects rather than requiring near-perfect detection, the route of Theorem 8.7(3). One author's scenario judgment puts decline in years five to ten at about 4% a year, half dissolution and half impairment, and cumulative decline over ten years at about 48% [obs: EV10]. X8 gives its durability and failure-mode rows beside every other path's, by the same standard.

### An illustration of the two cruxes

Let the per-period hazard on a path be 0.05 times its uncovered share plus a background 0.001, and on the cooperative path add a dissolution hazard times the loss its fallback carries. Survival at horizons 10, 30 and 100: continuation with the uncovered share rising from 0.2 by 0.01 a period, 0.871, 0.567, 0.028; continuation with a summable uncovered share, 0.966, 0.941, 0.873; cooperation with a summable share and dissolution 2% a period with fallback loss 0.5, 0.873, 0.696, 0.319; cooperation with dissolution 0.2%, 0.956, 0.913, 0.789 [num: legacy v5, CMP]. The numbers are illustrative. They show that a well-corrected continuation can beat a fragile arrangement, and that a rising uncovered share makes any modestly durable arrangement better past the near term.

### The failure-mode table: method

The table in X8 on which Section 12.1 compares is built in four steps, the same for every path, the paper's own design included. First, the failure modes are enumerated from two sources: the path's own documents, read for what they say ends or suspends it, and the rule for $\Theta$, which admits every mode that a named mechanism of Part I produces and the register does not contradict. Second, each mode is classified as reversible or not. A mode is reversible when a reversal exists that is named, stops short of O1 and O2, and is executed by someone other than the party whose failure it is; "the arrangement is renegotiated" is a reversal only if the documents say who can compel it, and otherwise it is recorded as a reversal by the parties. A branch is irreversible when correction cannot arrive before a protected loss: a decisive position that removes independent overrule (Proposition 5.8), a crossing of the protected floor (Theorem 10.4(3)), an irreversible delegated decision or a contest that denies the recovery interval of Lemma 6.3, or catastrophic escalation under Proposition 8.5. Capture alone need not be irreversible while an independent corrector survives, and loss of capacity alone need not be irreversible while recovery is possible. The same test applies to every path, including the proposed design: recertification, rotation and standby name possible remedies, and only their timely execution prevents a protected loss. Loss of guaranteed correctability marks exit from the kernel of Definition 8.1; actual protected loss may follow later. Third, each mode is given the measurement that would show it under way, drawn from the signatures and cruxes of Sections 7.3 and 12.3, so that the table and the trigger of Section 12.1 use one set of indicators. Fourth, every cell names the kind of support behind it, with one standard for every row: the path's own documents, a scenario judgment by its authors, an observation in the register, a result of this paper under its premises, or none. A cell with none says so, and the paper's own design is marked by the same rule: its documents are X10, so its reversals are provided by design and not yet shown to work, and its hazard of breakdown has no estimate and no base rate in the register. The asymmetry of reversibility on which Section 12.1 rests is read off the reversibility column, mode by mode, not from the labels of the paths.

## S9. Requirements

### Table 9.1, row by row

Each row is its cited result read as a condition on arrangements, in that result's model and over its horizon. The derivations, and the examples that fix each row's scope, follow; numbers come from the blocks of S15 named in brackets. The last column of the table holds two kinds of entry, kept apart here: conditions under which a row is not needed, and conditions under which it is met without design, which the table marks as met, not excused.

- **R1, consequences return.** *The two forms, under constant-speed replicator selection.* With other advantages equal and a race carrier present, the exact identity $\ln[p_r(t)/p_k(t)]=\ln[p_r(0)/p_k(0)]+s(\bar e-e_k)B(t)$ holds at either sign of the payoff gap. Hence a cumulative advantage $B(t)=\int_0^t(\nu-\tau_{\rm eff})\,ds$ that tends to infinity sends the total race share to one and the load to $\bar e$, so $\limsup_tV\le(1-h_V\bar e/\rho)^+$, which (reach) puts below the floor. Viability at every horizon therefore needs $B(t)\not\to\infty$. That implies the form of Theorem 10.4(3), whose proof in S10 takes the same step: if $B(t_k)\le M$ along some $t_k\to\infty$, then $\frac1{t_k}\int_0^{t_k}\tau_{\rm eff}\,ds\ge\nu-M/t_k$, so $\limsup_t\frac1t\int_0^t\tau_{\rm eff}\,ds\ge\nu$. *The average form is necessary and not sufficient:* with $\tau_{\rm eff}=\nu-1/(2\sqrt{t+1})$ the time average of liability tends to $\nu$ while $B(t)=\sqrt{t+1}-1\to\infty$, and on the logistic host with $\rho=0.05$, $h_V=0.08$ and floor 0.3, from a race share of 0.001, the host crosses its floor at $t=89$; with $\tau_{\rm eff}=\nu$ it never does [num: HO]. *Not at a finite horizon:* a trajectory with zero liability need not cross the floor before $H$. With the host starting at full capacity, floor 0.2, $\rho=0.01$, $h_V=0.02$ and zero liability, even full load from the start keeps $V(50)\ge0.435$, and the host crosses the floor only at $t=\ln3/\rho=109.9$, although (reach) holds [num: HO]. At a finite horizon the necessary condition is weaker: liability must keep the cumulative advantage below that of the path that just reaches the floor at $H$, by monotone comparison. *Not needed* either where other advantages correlated with restraint offset the selection of load (the scope of Proposition 3.4).
- **R2, coverage of every actor whose share can grow.** Let a carrier of the race type stand outside the common constraints while the covered carriers bear effective liability $\tau_{\rm eff}$. Its payoff exceeds that of a covered carrier with load $e_l$ by $\nu\bar e-(\nu-\tau_{\rm eff})e_l$, positive wherever the constraint costs the covered anything, so its cumulative advantage over every covered carrier grows without bound; where nothing bounds its share, the share tends to one and Proposition 3.4 applies, whatever the constraints on the others. Coverage is needed on a second ground: the race begins if any one capable actor prefers racing to restraint, and where those events are independent its probability tends to one as capable actors multiply (Proposition 5.1), so coverage must reach the most race-inclined. *Not needed* where harms stay local; where the uncovered carriers' combined share is bounded at $\bar p_{\rm unc}$ with $h_V[\bar p_{\rm unc}\bar e+(1-\bar p_{\rm unc})\bar e_{\rm cov}]<\rho(1-V_{\min})$, $\bar e_{\rm cov}$ the load the covered carriers still carry (positive, for instance, under the liability margin of Theorem 10.4), so that the load of all cannot carry the host below its floor; or within a horizon shorter than the crossing time.
- **R3a, the rate law.** Proposition 4.5(2) gives both halves in the field model with a seeded evaluation domain, $[H_\gamma]$, a positive cultivation channel and no per-unit floor, and Proposition S4.2 gives them off the selected level. *Sufficient at any speed:* with $\alpha_{\rm cor}\ge\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$ and $d_0>d^\ast(\gamma_0)$ the certificate holds at every date, for every adjustment of capacity that starts at or below its selected level and does not raise capacity while above it, since such capacity stays below its selected level and evaluation's row sum below its quasi-static value. *Necessary where capacity tracks:* below the rate law, with $\liminf_tq/q^\ast>0$, the cultivated term keeps a fixed fraction of a quasi-static value that grows without bound, and the certificate fails beyond a finite time. Carriers that optimize track; so does adjustment at a constant rate, which only delays failure [num: QS]; gradual selection tracks only where the variance of capacity rises faster than capacity itself. A requirement wants the sufficient half, the safe design rule; the necessary half says when a slower rate is a real failure and not a delayed one. *Human capacity:* where correction acts at $d\,(\mathcal M/\mathcal M_0)^w$ and delegation erodes $\mathcal M$ at $\delta_h$, both halves hold with the threshold raised by $w\delta_h$; where the erosion stops at a floor $\mathcal M_{\min}$, the rate returns to $\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$ and the required level rises by the factor $(\mathcal M_0/\mathcal M_{\min})^w$ (Proposition 4.6). *Not needed:* with $\chi=0$, or the conversion channels removed, a unit of capacity returns nothing, and the requirement is $A/c_{\rm ev}$, its level without second-order selection (Proposition 4.5(1)); with a per-unit cost floor that AI does not lower, cultivation stops once $d\ge d_f$ and the requirement levels off at $\max\{A/c_{\rm ev},d_f\}$, a level, not a rate; with a floor that falls at $\alpha_f<\alpha_{\rm cor}$, cultivation ends at a finite date and the certificate holds from some date on, though not necessarily before it: with the rate law at 0.1, $f_0=0.1$, $\alpha_f=0.05$ and $\alpha_{\rm cor}=0.051$, it fails at $t=38.4$, while cultivation ends only at $t=588$ [num: FF]; with capacity selected gradually and its variance not rising faster than capacity, capacity stays bounded and correction growing at any positive rate certifies in the end, after a possible transient failure (Proposition S4.2) [num: QS]; with nothing captured; and within a horizon shorter than the failure time. A lump-sum cost of capacity does not belong on this list: it delays cultivation and leaves the requirement unbounded as control cheapens (Proposition 4.5(3)) [num: FF].
- **R3b, no core.** Proposition 4.10(4): excluding every locally stable captured configuration needs an empty core of mutually sustaining capture, under the nonunit pruning-gain conditions of Proposition 4.10(4). The exact boundary $x_i=1$ is invariant, so a claim of recovery from literally every initial state would be false. *Not needed* if only the states actually reached must be excluded: with a row sum of two, the low initial state $(0.1,0.1)$ stays in the invariant rectangle $[0,0.1]\times[0,0.2]$ and converges to no capture, with $x=3.2\times10^{-10}$ at $t=20$ [num: FA]. A full row sum below one is the stronger certificate, sufficient for eventual recovery from $x_i(0)<1$ and finite initial support (Proposition 4.10(2)), without by itself bounding transient capture, and not necessary.
- **R4, breakouts within the bound.** Proposition 5.4(2): a deviation with $D_i(a)>v_i$ is deterred iff $p_d(a)\le\bar p_{d,i}(a)$, so restraint enforced by repetition needs the inequality for every actor and every such deviation; where punishment is triggered by detection and acts before decisiveness, $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$, and the inequality reads $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})\ge1-\bar p_{d,i}$ (Remark 5.5). Corollary 5.7 classifies the means. Where $D_i\le v_i$, a breakout does not raise the temptation net of the sanction: (14) is hardest to meet at $p_d=0$, and the ordinary condition of repetition, $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-v_i)$, decides. *The exception.* (14) is affine in $p_d$, so restraint survives every $p_d\in[0,1]$ iff it holds at $p_d=0$ and at $p_d=1$; at $p_d=1$ it reads $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-D_i)$, which where $D_i\ge v_i$ implies the condition at $p_d=0$. Directly: under this inequality the left side of (14) is $(1-\delta)g_i-\delta p_d(C_i-D_i)\le\delta(1-p_d)(C_i-D_i)\le\delta(1-p_d)(C_i-v_i)$ for every $p_d$, so $\bar p_{d,i}\ge1$. A decisive position worth less than cooperation does not give this by itself: with $C_i=1$, $D_i=0.9$, $v_i=0$, $g_i=2$ and $\delta=0.9$, $\bar p_{d,i}=0.864$, and (14) fails at $p_d=0.87$ and $0.9$; with $g_i=0.5$, so that $(1-\delta)g_i=0.05\le0.09=\delta(C_i-D_i)$, it holds at $p_d=0$, $0.5$, $0.9$ and $1$ [num: ES]. *Not needed* where no deviation reaches a position beyond punishment, so that $p_d=0$: in Branch III, and in Branch I′ above its saturating level (Proposition 7.2), where the terms of Corollary 5.7 decide. *Met, not excused,* in Branch II, where detection and response come within the time a breakout needs, by the detection form above.
- **R5, a summable uncovered share.** *Necessary under (residual floor) alone.* If on every surviving history the uncovered share is at least $\underline u_t$, (residual floor) makes the conditional probability of the irreversible event at $t$ at least $h\underline u_t$, and Lemma 5.11 gives $\Pr(\Phi_H)\le\prod_{t<H}(1-h\underline u_t)\le\exp\big(-h\sum_{t<H}\underline u_t\big)$, which tends to zero when $\sum_t\underline u_t=\infty$. Survival bounded away from zero therefore needs a summable uncovered share. *Sufficient* under a residual bound below one: if the residual probability is at most $\bar hu_t$ with $\bar h<1$ and $\sum_tu_t<\infty$, survival stays bounded away from zero (Theorem 5.13). Under (shared failure) both statements are every actor's, whoever is racing. Neither (coverage lags) nor (variety grows) enters the necessity: (coverage lags) says when R5 fails, and (variety grows) is what makes (coverage lags) expected on a continuation branch, so its failure removes a reason to expect R5 to fail, not the requirement. *Not needed* where (residual floor) fails, as where a correction architecture whose blocking becomes complete makes residual risk summable without coverage (Proposition S6.4); at a finite horizon what counts is the exposure up to it, which a freeze after a sprint keeps finite and a rollback below coverage ends (Proposition 5.14). *Met, not excused,* where hazardous variety stops growing at a level set by need while coverage keeps growing at least linearly, so that the uncovered share reaches zero in finite time (Proposition 5.15).
- **R6, independent observation.** Remark 4.9: where an upper bound on masking comes from a channel the field does not control, an alarm on reports set correspondingly higher fires no later than the true condition reaches its target; for masking by the evaluated model, the bound must come from evidence its behaviour controls less directly. Proposition 4.8 shows what fails without it: captured evaluation lowers the level at which a fixed alarm fires, and can silence it through the crossing of the floor. Proposition 5.16: informative cheap talk needs an effective bias below $\frac14$, which relative accounting raises, and between two rivals at $\theta=-1$ no talk informs, so trust without good will needs stakes on the topic of talk that are not contested, or signals that are costly or verifiable. Each is sufficient, not necessary. The detection on which R4's detection form rests is observation of this kind, and masking hides both $T_{\rm dec}$ and $\upsilon$ (Section 5.2). *Not needed* where channels that observe actions directly, such as verifiable deployments, compute and the performance of contracts, suffice for the purpose at hand, since they do not pass through the masking of (13).
- **R7, standing.** V3. Proposition 3.12(1) shows that inclusion stopping at the edge of a club over-produces harm to those outside relative to the system optimum whenever $m_o\ell_o>0$; it does not show that O1 or O2 is reached, so the requirement is normative, declared and not derived.
- **R8a, admission constraints that hold.** Proposition 6.7(3), with Lemma 3.3(3) type by type: under relative, cumulative selection among developers and $(A_\tau)$, with a matched variant admissible, a divergence whose costs fall on common terms persists unless a constraint enforced on every developer as a condition of admission covers it; the constraint keeps the divergence from spreading if, for every type that can evade it, the expected sanction is at least that type's gain from evasion, removes it under cumulative selection if every such inequality is strict, and within a type only if. Across types the condition is sufficient, not necessary: in one field with equal baselines, compliance takes the field iff the best-paid variant complies, so a type whose evasion pays still loses its evaders to another type's compliers when those earn more. With compliance costs 0.1 and 0.2 and expected sanctions 1.0 and 0.15, the second type's evasion pays and still vanishes, while with 0.05 in place of 0.15 evasion takes the field [num: CI]. The matched variant is load-bearing, since Proposition 6.7(2) is about variants, not about every pair of developers: a safeguarded developer with baseline 2 and tax 0.1 outgrows an unsafe one with baseline 1, and falls to a share of $2\times10^{-9}$ by $t=200$ against its matched twin; a tax of 0.01 is paid out of a lead of 0.02 for as long as the lead lasts, and not out of a lead of 0.005 [num: CI]. *Not needed* where developers are selected by something other than relative payoff, such as a public mandate, though a national laboratory that only moves relative selection to the level of states leaves it in force there; where the tax is zero or negative for the class, so that the safeguard is neutral or spreads without coordination; and where decisions are not retained by contest performance (Lemma 6.5). These exceptions remove this selection mechanism, not the independent duties of persistence, coverage and assurance. At zero tax the variants are neutral: a protective variant can persist if present, but it need not spread or remove a hazardous one. A positive residual hazard can remain even with zero tax [num: RP]. *The liability route.* A liability on the developer for the harm of a divergence enters its payoff as a private cost of not countering it. Where the harm can be attributed and the liability collected, and it exceeds the tax, $(A_\tau)$ fails and the safeguard spreads without coordination (the scope of Proposition 6.7). It reaches only what can be attributed: delayed and masked divergences are where attribution fails, harms beyond the developer's assets call for regulation in advance (Section 10.3), and the liability is itself imposed by correctors (R3). *Where admission does not reach.* Admission reaches only what it admits, and three things lie beyond it. None makes R8a unnecessary, which is why Section 9 states them as limits and not in the table's last column; a design meets each by other means. Systems that automate a developer's own research are used first inside it, where a condition imposed at release cannot see them (Section 6.4); R8a is therefore stated for consequential deployment, internal deployment included, and the means is the row's own scope. Openly released weights can be fine-tuned around any constraint (Section 6.4), so for an openly released class the decision to release is the admission decision for every later use, and what remains afterwards is a liability on use where harm can be attributed, the liability route above, within its limits of attribution. A masked divergence shows under evaluation only as far as the system's behaviour lets it, so admission conditioned on evaluated behaviour keeps it out only while the detection it allows, times the sanction, exceeds the tax (Remark 6.8); the developer's own detection-conditioned sanctions face the same incentive comparison. With a fixed positive tax, bounded sanctions and detection tending to zero at fixed auditor access (C12), coordination alone cannot restore deterrence at all capabilities. If the tax also falls, compare expected sanction with the changing tax (C9 and C12 jointly), including the cumulative advantage needed for an asymptotic claim. None of this establishes the residual floor required by R5. Under the protection standard the recommendation is admission up to the capability at which independent control can establish safety, with development bound to the pace of control (E3) on every branch, broad E1 included. Stopping the decisive class (E2) follows its separate governance trigger; the removal of concentrating contests does not by itself supply coverage or an assurance case. Whether admission can bound masking at a given capability remains C12 (Section 12.3).
- **R8b, equal incidence.** R8 is split because its clauses do different work (Section 3.2): the sanction of R8a keeps a constraint, and the equal incidence of R8b keeps it from becoming a weapon against challengers. Lemma 3.3(2): a constraint that costs every complier the same additive amount is a common term among them and leaves their relative growth unchanged, and one whose cost differs re-sorts them toward those it costs least. Equal incidence is therefore necessary and sufficient for neutrality among compliers, and not needed to keep a divergence out: with compliance costs 0.1 and 0.2 and a sanction of 1, the compliers re-sort to shares 0.88 and 0.12 by $t=20$ while the compliant share is 0.999999975, and with equal costs their relative shares stay at 1.000000 [num: CI]. What it protects against is the second direction of capture, a rule whose cost falls on challengers (Definition 4.1); that this protection is required rests on V5, since a rule that bars challengers gives the successful more advantage in the same game [val].
- **R9, human decision authority kept.** R9 is the row for O2. Lemma 6.3: with the capacity to take delegated decisions back decaying at $\delta_h$ under delegation and recovering at $\varrho_h$ without it, an actor that has delegated for longer than $T_{\rm lock}=\ln(\mathcal M_0/\mathcal M_{\min})/\delta_h$ and reverts must operate below the floor of safe operation for a recovery time. Where the contest denies that time, the delegator can no longer overrule decisions taken in its name, and O2 is lost in that domain (Section 6.3); where a decision is irreversible within the time available for deliberation, the lock is immediate. Keeping the capacity in practice removes the lock, and where the contest is decided by strength it costs strength, so under relative selection it is not kept alone (Lemma 3.3(2)). Where delegation is dominant at the speed reached (Proposition 6.2(1)), or the lock is uncertain, so that delegation by all can be an equilibrium (Proposition 6.4(2)), the capacity survives only under a commitment binding every actor [thm∣prem]. *Not needed* where delegation leaves the capacity intact, or where automation is bounded and reversible within the time available, which the test of O2 in Section 6.6 decides. *Met without an arrangement* where every actor prices in a lock it knows to be certain: not delegating is then the unique equilibrium, strict and in need of no trust (Proposition 6.4(1)), the narrow E1 of Section 11.3.

**Sufficient routes.** Four routes are sufficient and not necessary. Full inbound row sums below one certify eventual recovery of each corrector domain from $x_i(0)<1$ and finite support, against every state of the others (Proposition 4.10(2)); they say nothing about whether the correctors' errors are independent (Proposition 6.9). The safeguards (I1)–(I4) of Section 10.3 give viability once in force, and over the transient when the liability margin is at least $\Delta_{\min}$, a threshold set by the host, the floor, the initial composition and the speed of selection: on the logistic host with the race strategy at 99% of activity, $\Delta_{\min}$ is 0.108, 0.165 and 0.355 for floors 0.2, 0.3 and 0.5; on a host that degrades by itself below a threshold, the margin must also keep the transient above that threshold, 0.26 to 0.51 in the cases computed, and a shortfall there is permanent (Theorem 10.4) [num: HO]. Not building the decisive class, holding it in common custody, and detecting and answering faster than breakout lower $p_d$, and so meet R4, by changing the premise of Proposition 5.4 rather than its terms (Corollary 5.7(a)). Verification with an expected sanction $\upsilon F$ at least the net gain from exit deters irreversible exit from an agreement whose overhang grows (Proposition S8.5).

### Proof of Proposition 9.2

(1) With other advantages equal, a carrier that loads less than another earns $\nu-\tau_{\rm eff}$ less per unit of the difference, so while $\tau_{\rm eff}<\nu$ its share falls relative to the other's (Proposition 3.4). Correction keyed to no one, and the cost of verification, are borne by the carrier that funds them and benefit every carrier, so they are selected against among otherwise identical carriers (Proposition 4.4). Each contribution is a cost borne by one carrier, which relative selection removes (Lemma 3.3(2)).

(2) A liability imposed by one rule-maker leaves the carriers it does not reach uncovered. By hypothesis their combined load satisfies (reach), and the race carriers among them earn the full advantage $\nu$ of load, so their cumulative advantage over every carrier that bears the liability grows without bound and Proposition 3.4 applies: the host crosses its floor whatever that rule-maker imposes on its own carriers, and it meets neither R1 nor R2 alone. Imposing the liability lowers the payoffs of the carriers it reaches against those it does not, by (1), and so lowers the rule-maker's standing among the $n_s$ rule-makers, while the benefit, a host kept above its floor, is common to all. Unilateral imposition is therefore restraint in the commons of Proposition 3.7, with $\Upsilon_s$ the value to the field of a unit of it over its private cost, and no rule-maker imposing is the equilibrium once $\lambda_s>(\Upsilon_s-n_s)/(n_s-1)$. Whether an agreement among the rule-makers is an equilibrium is governed by Proposition 8.6.

(3) Where captured rule-making leaves $\tau_{\rm eff}=\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})$ (Section 4.1), the private payoff of a unit of load is $\nu-\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})$, negative iff $\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})>\nu$, that is iff $x_{\rm rule}<1-\nu/\tau$, and only then does the selection of Proposition 3.4 reverse. That captured rule-making acts on liability in this form is the join of the field model to the load model, an interface of S13. $\square$

### Why R1 needs the signed-gap identity

General cumulative selection does not make R1 necessary when liability crosses the private advantage. Let $x$ be the share of a race carrier of load 1 against a carrier of load 0, with $\nu=1$ and equal background payoffs, and write $g=1-\tau_{\rm eff}$. The rule $\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{logit}x=g+g^3$ is relative, payoff-monotone and cumulative with $\varkappa=1$: its response to every positive gap is at least that gap, in either carrier order. Repeat a cycle of $\tau_{\rm eff}=0$ for one time unit and $\tau_{\rm eff}=3$ for 0.4 units. Each cycle adds 0.2 to $B$, so $B\to\infty$ and average liability tends to $6/7<\nu$; but it adds $2-4=-2$ to the race logit, so the race share tends to zero. From $x(0)=0.001$, $x(t)\le\operatorname{logistic}(\operatorname{logit}(0.001)+2)=0.00734215$ for all time. On the host with $\rho=0.05$, $h_V=0.08$, $V(0)=1$ and floor 0.3, (reach) holds, yet comparison gives $V(t)\ge1-(0.08/0.05)\,0.00734215=0.98825256>0.3$ for all time. Strong intermittent correction preserves viability although both forms of R1 fail. Smooth short transitions between the liability levels preserve these strict inequalities. Under constant-speed replicator selection on the same liability path, the race logit instead rises by $0.2s$ a cycle and the host eventually crosses its floor. The analytical bounds and independent integrations are in `verification/readiness_checks.py` [num: RP].

The difference is the sign change: on a negative-gap interval the cumulative condition, applied with the carrier order reversed, is an upper bound on the race carrier's relative growth. It cannot be integrated as the positive lower bound used by Proposition 3.4, whose premise keeps the gap positive. R1 uses constant-speed replicator dynamics to avoid that unsupported extension.

### Which rows need more than one party

Section 9 gives the reasons for R1, R2, R3 and R6. R4, R5, R8a and R9 need more than one party under their own premises. Of the means of R4, custody needs more than one party, not building binds only the one that refrains, and detection faster than breakout needs access to the conduct of others. Under (shared failure) each actor's survival turns on the uncovered share of all, so a pace kept by one leaves R5 unmet for every actor (Theorem 5.13). Under $(A_\tau)$ an admission constraint adopted by one developer is selected against (Proposition 6.7(3)). And where the contest is decided by strength, capacity kept in practice costs strength, so R9 kept by one is kept at a loss (Lemma 6.3).

### Coverage can unravel

**Remark S9.1 (The participation threshold) [thm∣prem].** *Let $x$ be the share of capable activity outside an arrangement, weighted by share; let a participant of type $k$ bear a cost $c_k$ of its common constraints; and let the expected cost of standing outside, the sanction, the access forgone and the provision lost, be $s_k(x)$, continuous and strictly decreasing in $x$, because sanctions are funded by participants, shared provision is worth less with fewer inside, or rule-making is captured by those outside. Within a type, under cumulative payoff-monotone selection between staying and leaving, this is Lemma 3.3(3) with evasion read as exit: if $s_k(0)>c_k>s_k(1)$, staying and leaving are both stable, separated by the tipping share $x_k^\dagger$ at which $s_k(x_k^\dagger)=c_k$; if $s_k(1)\ge c_k$, staying is globally stable; and if $s_k(0)\le c_k$, leaving is, and the arrangement cannot hold the type on these terms. Participants who choose by best response, staying iff $s_k(x)\ge c_k$, have the same stable states and the same tipping share. Across types, where $s_k(0)>c_k$ for every type, every state whose outside share is below $\min_kx_k^\dagger$, the minimum taken over the types that tip, returns to full participation, with the type masses fixed and some participation inside each type. Past it, return is no longer guaranteed, but neither is unravelling: types still below their own tipping shares can return faster than the first type leaves, so the basin is a region in the vector of type shares, and a cascade follows only where the first type's leaving keeps the outside share above the next type's tipping share. The arrangement's participation threshold, the coverage $1-\min_kx_k^\dagger$ set by the type for which leaving first pays, is therefore a conservative sufficient coverage, not the tipping boundary.*

*Proof.* Within type $k$ the leaving variant grows relative to the staying one iff $c_k>s_k(x)$, so the type's outside share moves as $\dot x_k\propto x_k(1-x_k)\big(c_k-s_k(x)\big)$, and the claims for one type are those of Lemma 3.3(3). Across types, while $x<\min_kx_k^\dagger$ every bracket is negative, so every $x_k$, and with them $x$, falls. $\square$

An exit moves the outside share at once by the leaver's own share, so one large participant can carry it past a type's tipping share, and the exits of that type then follow without a further shock. With a cost of staying of 0.3 and a cost of standing outside of $0.48(1-x)$, the tipping share is 0.375; with $0.6-0.4x$ it is 0.75; with $0.5+0.5(1-x)$, which never falls below 0.5, staying is globally stable; with $0.24(1-x)$, leaving is [num: CI, evasion read as exit]. Conditions of access act on $s_k$: trade and access tied to participation, provision available only inside, and sanctions financed by formula rather than by the participants keep the cost of standing outside from falling as others leave, and once $s_k(1)\ge c_k$ for every type nothing can unravel. What raises the cost of staying moves the threshold the other way. A pause whose end is judged on several conjunctive indicators measured with noise errs toward "not ready" (Section 11.3) [thm], so an arrangement that holds participants longer than its evidence warrants raises the cost of staying and spends its coverage [thm∣prem].

### The crosswalk

The paper states what an adequate arrangement must do in several places, each for its own purpose: the thresholds of Table 7.1, the generative marks G1–G7 (Section 8.2), the safeguards (I1)–(I4) (Section 10.3), the durability tests D1–D8 (Definition 8.4) and the readiness dimensions Q1–Q7 (Definition 11.2). The crosswalk maps each row of Table 9.1 onto the others, by label; each list is stated only in its home. A dash means that no entry of that list asks for the row; the readings after the table say why.

**Table S9.2 (Each requirement against the paper's other lists) [def].**

| Row of Table 9.1 | Threshold it guards (Table 7.1) | Mark | Safeguard | Durability test | Readiness |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| R1 Consequences return | Load | G1 | (I1); (I2) in rule-making; (I4) | D5 | — |
| R2 Coverage of every actor whose share can grow | Load; Enforcement, since the race is set by the most race-inclined actor | every mark, held for every capable actor | the reach of (I1)–(I3) | D5; D8 | — |
| R3a Correction at the rate | Capture | G3 | (I2); (I4) | D4 | Q4 |
| R3b No core of capture | Capture | G3 | (I2) | D4; D7 | Q4 |
| R4 Breakouts within the bound | Enforcement; Compounding, through $T_{\rm dec}$; Standing, through $\bar p_d$ (Proposition 5.17) | G4; G2 and G5, through the won share | — | D5 | Q3; Q7 |
| R5 A summable uncovered share | Coverage and tempo | G7 | — | D7 | Q1; Q5 |
| R6 Independent observation | Talk; Capture, through masking; Enforcement, through detection | G6 | (I3) | D7 | Q1; Q7 |
| R7 Standing for those affected | — | — | — | D3; D8 | — |
| R8a Admission constraints that hold | Alignment tax; persistence and assurance remain when the tax vanishes | G3; G1, by the liability route; G7, for masked classes | (I1), by the liability route | D4; D7 | Q1; Q5 |
| R8b Equal incidence | Load; Capture, in its second direction | G2; G3 | — | D8 | — |
| R9 Human decision authority kept | Delegation | G7, applied to decisions | — | D3 | Q6 |
| *Sufficient:* row sums below one | Capture | G3 | (I2) | D4 | Q4 |
| *Sufficient:* (I1)–(I4) with margin | Load; Capture | G1; G3 | (I1)–(I4) | — | Q4 |
| *Sufficient:* not building, custody, detection faster than breakout | Enforcement | G4 | — | D5 | Q7 |
| *Sufficient:* verification against exit | Enforcement, under a growing overhang | G4; G6 | — | D5 | Q7 |

*Read by threshold.* Every threshold is guarded by some row. Compounding and Standing are guarded only through R4, by the time a lead needs to become decisive and by the bound the working objective sets; what reverses them directly is not a requirement but a lever, because the models give sharing, provision and non-convertible reward as alternatives, any one of which can carry the margin (Proposition 2.3). What sets the levers apart from the rows is that each meets several rows at once: provision that grows with the stakes holds the won share still, and with it both sides of R4 (Proposition 5.17); reward that does not convert into control, $\chi=0$, brings R3a's requirement down to a level without second-order selection (Proposition 4.5(1)) [thm]. Two of the generative marks of Section 8.2, G2 and G5, name them, and the reading by mark says which rows each meets.

*Read by mark.* G1, G3, G4, G6 and G7 each answer rows directly, G7 both the pace of R5 and, applied to decisions, the delegation of R9. G2 and G5 meet several rows at once rather than one: through the won share they move both sides of R4, and G2 is also what equal incidence protects (R8b), which is why Section 10 argues for them. No mark answers R7: the marks describe a change in what is selected, and standing (V3) is what any selection must leave in place.

*Read by safeguard.* (I1)–(I4) implement R1, R3 and R6 on the host of Section 3, reach R2 through their coverage and R8a through the liability route, and do not touch R4, R5, R8b or R9, which are not mechanisms of load on the host.

*Read by test.* D4, D5, D7 and D8 test rows directly, and D3 tests R7 and R9 after delegation. D1, D2 and D6 test no requirement: they test how an arrangement fails and what it keeps running, and their counterparts are the fallback (Lemma 8.2), the participation threshold (Remark S9.1) and the continuity of essential services, O3, which no row carries because the rows concern what the contest selects.

*Read by readiness.* Q1 and Q3–Q7 each ask a row. Q7 asks for common custody or equally effective durable containment, including a verifiable disposition of capability already built; its connection to R4 is the maintained deviation bound, not a unique institutional form. R5 and independent assurance still apply on every branch, including broad E1 and zero alignment tax. Q2 asks none: it measures the Standing threshold and G5 directly, through governing purposes, which requirements do not constrain, since selection acts whatever carriers value (Proposition 3.4). R1, R2, R7 and R8b have no readiness dimension. Readiness is asked of the frontier during a pause and presupposes them; a pause that ends with R1 or R2 unmet resumes a field in which load is selected.

*Other lists.* The four properties of provision in Section 10.1 are rows applied to provision itself: distributed is R3, many providers below the size at which influence over correctors pays (Proposition 4.3); good enough is R4 and R5, a point of enough that keeps provision within correction and confers no decisive advantage (Proposition 5.15); replaceable is R9, human capacity kept above its lock-in floor (Lemma 6.3); and for everyone is R2 and R7. The outer level of Section 6.6 is R8a, R3 and R6 for AI, and its inner level, correctability secured from outside, is R8a's capability threshold for masked classes together with R9. The function table of Section 10.5 gives the rows their forms. The requirements of Table 11.3 are of the path to an arrangement, not of the arrangement; persistence, the last of them, asks for the verification safeguard, row sums below one and (I4), with each step held for its dwell time.

## S10. The generative alternative

This chapter states in full the three results that Section 10 carries in a sentence each, gives Proposition 10.1 in full with its variations, proves Proposition 10.2 under both sharing conventions, and computes the margin of Theorem 10.4 on both hosts.

### A common frontier

**Proposition S10.1 (A common value: too little speed, not too much) [thm].** *If the value of capability is common, $\pi_i=\Omega(\mathcal E)/n-ce_i-\ell\mathcal E$ with $\Omega$ increasing and strictly concave in total intensity $\mathcal E$, private incentives give $\Omega'(\mathcal E)=n(c+\ell)$ against the joint optimum's $\Omega'(\mathcal E)=c+n\ell$, so total intensity is at or below the joint optimum: the residual failure is too little speed, not too much, and the joint optimum must also respect the host's floor (Definition 3.1).*

*Proof.* Each actor's first-order condition is $\Omega'(\mathcal E)/n=c+\ell$; the sum of payoffs, $\Omega(\mathcal E)-c\mathcal E-n\ell\mathcal E$, has the first-order condition $\Omega'(\mathcal E)=c+n\ell$. Since $n(c+\ell)-(c+n\ell)=(n-1)c\ge0$, with equality iff $n=1$ or $c=0$, and $\Omega'$ is decreasing, private total intensity is at most the joint one (interior solutions). $\square$

**Proposition 10.1 in full.** *Of $n$ actors, cooperators hold effort $e_c$ and racers choose theirs; each pays $c$ per unit of its own effort and bears $\ell$ per unit of total intensity (Section 3.4); whoever wins the decisive contest gains $\Omega$, and every loser bears $L_b$. Trust in a rival is the probability, independent across rivals, that it cooperates. Under separate frontiers every effort enters the contest separately; under a common frontier the $n_c$ cooperators pool effort into one entity of strength $(n_ce_c)^r$, which yields a common value $\Omega_c$ split equally and confers no dominance among them. With $n=4$, $\Omega=\Omega_c=L_b=c=1$, $\ell=0.3$, $e_c=0.05$, $r=1.3$: (1) under separate frontiers the unique pure equilibrium is all racing; (2) under a common frontier all cooperating ($R=0.140>T=0.002$) and all racing are both strict equilibria, cooperating being a best response iff trust in each rival exceeds about 0.61, or 0.82 if a deviator best-responds to fixed intensities; (3) treating the others as a coalition maximizing their summed payoff, the coalition's cooperation is dominant; (4) a smaller effort per cooperator ($e_c=0.03$) leaves only all racing, and with $n=2$ all cooperating is an equilibrium for $r$ up to about 1.2; (5) holding $\Omega_c$ and $L_b$ fixed, the effort each cooperator must contribute to the pool, $e_c$, to keep $T\le R$ is 0.0054, 0.035, 0.141 and 0.49 for $\Omega=0.5,1,2,4$; scaling $\Omega_c$ and $L_b$ with $\Omega$, it is 0.0175, 0.035, 0.070 and 0.140.* Racers' intensities are the symmetric fixed point of best responses, each verified as a global best response; cases without a symmetric pure equilibrium are excluded (`../v4/checks_v45.py`, N22, run in the legacy suite of `checks_v72.py`) [num: legacy v4.5, N22]. Variations in the same block keep both strict equilibria with $n=3$, with $L_b=0.5$, with $\ell=0$, with $\Omega_c=2$ and with $n=6$ at $r\in\{0.8,1\}$, the aggregate trust threshold ranging from 0.14 to 0.90; at $n=6$, $r=1.3$ some racer subgames have no symmetric pure equilibrium, and results there are not equilibria.

A common frontier turns a dilemma into a coordination problem while the value of winning is moderate; when it is large, keeping ahead of defectors is the race moved up a level. *Within the pool.* The model fixes $e_c$. Each cooperator's effort raises the pool's strength, and with it the chance that the pool and not a racer wins; the gain, $\Omega_c$ split equally and the loss $L_b$ avoided, goes to every cooperator alike, while the cost falls on the contributor. Contributions chosen one by one are therefore a good common to the cooperators, which selection among them does not favour (Lemma 3.11), and parts (4) and (5) put a floor under the pooled effort that rises steeply with $\Omega$: a pool whose contributions drift below the floor loses its cooperative equilibrium. That is a free-rider result, and it is why Discussion X10 collects frontier contributions by formula from the same base as the levy rather than by subscription. Their size in an equilibrium with chosen contributions is not computed here.

### Proof of Proposition 10.2

(1) Pooled payoffs $\pi'_j=(1-\beta)\pi_j+\beta\bar\pi$ give $J_i=\big[(1-\beta)+\beta(1+\theta_0)/n\big]\pi_i+\big[\theta_0(1-\beta)+\beta(1+\theta_0)(n-1)/n\big]\bar\pi_{-i}$; dividing by the first coefficient gives $\theta_{\rm eff}(\beta)$, strictly increasing in $\beta$ for $-1<\theta_0<n-1$, with value $n-1$ at $\beta=1$.
