At $\theta_0=-1$, $J_i=(1-\beta)(\pi_i-\bar\pi_{-i})$, and under relative selection $\pi'_k-\pi'_l=(1-\beta)(\pi_k-\pi_l)$, so choices and orderings are unchanged for $\beta<1$. At $\beta=1$ the relative objective and every payoff difference vanish: selection becomes neutral and the relative objective is indifferent among all choices.

(2) Write $A_w=(1-\beta\varrho)\Omega_e+\Omega_s$ for what the winner keeps. *Shared among all $n$:* every actor receives $\varrho\beta\Omega_e/n$ whoever wins, so $\pi_i=w_iA_w+\varrho\beta\Omega_e/n-ce_i-\ell\mathcal E$; the shared term is common to all and drops out of every objective $J_i=\pi_i+\theta\bar\pi_{-i}$ and of relative selection, leaving the contest with prize $\Omega_{\rm eff}=A_w$. *Shared among the $n-1$ others:* actor $j$ receives $\varrho\beta\Omega_e/(n-1)$ whenever another wins, so $\pi_j=w_jA_w+\varrho\beta\Omega_e(1-w_j)/(n-1)-ce_j-\ell\mathcal E=w_j\Omega_{\rm eff}+\varrho\beta\Omega_e/(n-1)-ce_j-\ell\mathcal E$ with $\Omega_{\rm eff}=A_w-\varrho\beta\Omega_e/(n-1)=\big(1-\beta\varrho\,n/(n-1)\big)\Omega_e+\Omega_s$; since $\sum_{j\ne i}w_j=1-w_i$, $\bar\pi_{-i}=-w_i\Omega_{\rm eff}/(n-1)$ plus terms independent of $w_i$, and $J_i=w_i\Omega_{\rm eff}(n-1-\theta)/(n-1)-ce_i-(1+\theta)\ell\mathcal E$ plus a constant: again the contest with prize $\Omega_{\rm eff}$. This is the convention under which Proposition 3.5(4) and Proposition 5.8 count what a winner gives. The symmetric equilibrium $e(\theta)$ of Proposition 3.6(1), the dissipation of Proposition 5.2 and the hazard of Proposition 5.10 are each proportional to the prize. Under relative selection a mutant at $y$ among residents at $x$ has relative payoff $\Omega_{\rm eff}(y^r-x^r)/(y^r+(n-1)x^r)-c(y-x)$ under either convention, with zero slope at $x^\ast=r\Omega_{\rm eff}/(nc)$, concave for $r=1$, and no profitable mutant on a grid for $r<1$ [num: SH].

*Where the uninvadable intensity is positive.* Under the first convention $\Omega_{\rm eff}>0$ for every $\beta\varrho<1$, and with $\Omega_s>0$, $\Omega_{\rm eff}\ge\Omega_s>0$ for every $\beta\le1$, so the uninvadable intensity is at least $r\Omega_s/(nc)$ however much of the divisible prize is shared. Under the second, $\Omega_{\rm eff}\ge\Omega_s$ while $\beta\varrho\le(n-1)/n$; beyond it a winner that has given the $n-1$ others more than $(n-1)/n$ of the divisible prize gains less from it than a loser, and $\Omega_{\rm eff}$ stays positive for every $\beta$ iff $\Omega_s>(\varrho n/(n-1)-1)\Omega_e$. With a promise kept with certainty, $\varrho=1$, this is $\Omega_s>\Omega_e/(n-1)$, the value at which the positional prize outweighs the divisible loss of a winner who has given everything away. At $\Omega_{\rm eff}=0$ the relative payoff advantage of a mutant is $-c(y-x)$, so positive intensity is selected against; the interior formula is used only for $\Omega_{\rm eff}>0$. V6.1 stated "positive for every $\beta$ when $\Omega_s>0$" without the convention; it holds under the first.

(3) is Proposition 2.3(1) with Proposition S2.4(4): with $k_0=0$, $S^\ast=\kappa_c\Omega/d_K$, and the condition of Proposition 2.3(1) becomes $(1-\beta)r-1<\delta_f/d_K$, in which $\kappa_c$ does not appear, with or without diffusion. With $\chi=0$ the reflexive channels of Proposition 2.2 are removed; then the return to influence is zero (Proposition 4.3) and $d^\ast=A/c_{\rm ev}$ (Proposition 4.5(1)). $\square$

*Numerics* [num: SH]. With $n=4$, $r=1$, $c=1$, $\Omega_e=1$, the uninvadable intensity is 0.25 without a positional part and 0.125 with half the divisible prize shared among all; with $\Omega_s=1$, sharing all of $\Omega_e$ only halves it, from 0.5 to 0.25. In a common field of intensities $(0.2,0.05,0.1,0.1)$ the higher-intensity carrier out-earns the lower by 0.017 with half shared, and the difference turns negative only beyond 55%: sharing lowers the advantage of intensity at a given profile without moving the uninvadable intensity to zero. With $\Omega_e=1$ and $\Omega_s=0.5$, the uninvadable intensity is 0.300 (shared among all) and 0.275 (among the others) at $\beta=0.3$, and 0.225 and 0.175 at $\beta=0.6$, equal to $r\Omega_{\rm eff}/(nc)$ in each case with no mutant gaining on a grid. With $\Omega_s=0$, actors that value their absolute payoff and choose intensities as best responses follow the same two prizes: their Nash intensities, $r\Omega_{\rm eff}(n-1)/(n^2c)$, are 0.13125 and 0.11250 at $\beta=0.3$, and 0.07500 and 0.03750 at $\beta=0.6$. Earlier versions said that only changing what winning converts into reverses the direction of selection; sharing the divisible prize among all leaves a positive effective prize whenever $\Omega_s>0$, but complete payoff pooling is neutral and sufficiently large sharing among the others can erase the contest reward despite a positional prize.

**Remark 10.3 in full.** At the first order, more contenders make it likelier that the race begins (Proposition 5.1(4)), and in a model of developers racing under a shared existential hazard, fragmentation raises speed and conditional risk, while a developer able to commit first to a slightly slower speed gains because its rivals slow too (Bueno de Mesquita, Dziuda and Polborn 2026; EV11). At the second order, cultivated influence rises with $\sum_kp_k^{1+(2-\xi_v)/\eta}$ (Proposition 4.5(4)), so merging the frontier into one carrier maximizes the return to capturing its correctors, and the gain of the reflexive channel is higher at the present concentration of standing than at parity (S2). A merged frontier also raises connectivity and with it common-mode failure, so it keeps modules of independent lineage inside it (Proposition 6.9), and its correction outside it.

### Proof of Theorem 10.4, and the margin on both hosts

(1) (I2) with a margin in rule-making gives a full inbound row sum below one, so $x_{\rm rule}\to0$ (Proposition 4.10(2)); once (I1) is enacted, $\tau_{\rm eff}=\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})$ exceeds $\nu$ after a finite time, load has a negative private payoff, the load falls to $e_{\min}$, and $\liminf V\ge1-h_Ve_{\min}/\rho>V_{\min}$ on the host (3), the last inequality being the premise $h_Ve_{\min}<\rho(1-V_{\min})$, which V6.1 left implicit in "a carrier of low enough load". For unanticipated harms, (I2) in evaluation gives $x_{\rm ev}\to0$, so masking falls below its threshold, or the conservative rule of Remark 4.9 applies, and the alarm fires above the floor.

*The margin.* Fix the state, above the floor, at which the margin $\Delta=\tau_{\rm eff}-\nu>0$ takes force, and measure time from it. Relative fitness is then $-\Delta(e-E)$, so at selection speed $s$ the distribution of load at $t$ is the distribution at that state tilted by $e^{-s\Delta et}$ and normalized; its mean falls as the tilt $s\Delta t$ rises, its derivative in the tilt being minus the variance, so at every $t>0$ the load $E(t)$ is lower the larger $\Delta$. With two loads the race share solves $\dot x=-s\Delta(e_{\max}-e_{\min})\,x(1-x)$. Since $\dot V$ is decreasing in $E$ on the host (3) and on the threshold host below, the comparison theorem makes $V(t)$ nondecreasing in $\Delta$ at every $t$, so the minimum of $V$ along the path is nondecreasing in $\Delta$, and by continuity the margins that keep it at or above the floor form an interval $[\Delta_{\min},\infty)$. It is not empty on (3): as $\Delta\to\infty$ the load falls to $e_{\min}$ at once, and $V$ then moves monotonically toward $1-h_Ve_{\min}/\rho>V_{\min}$. On a host whose per-capita regeneration is lower at every level and negative below a threshold $V_u$, a path that falls below $V_u$ does not return; the interval is then not empty only if the state at which the margin takes force lies above $V_u$ at that load, and a normative floor below $V_u$ would make crossing the floor a loss that is not the first.

(2) Without (I1), Proposition 3.4 applies; without (I2) in rule-making, $x_{\rm rule}\to1$ and $\tau_{\rm eff}\to0$; without (I3), silent crossing follows (Proposition 4.13); with rule-making captured first, the floor can be crossed when rule-making adapts slowly.

(3) Under constant-speed replicator dynamics, for a race carrier $r$ and every lower-load carrier $k$, $\ln[p_r(t)/p_k(t)]=\ln[p_r(0)/p_k(0)]+s(\bar e-e_k)B(t)$, even when $\nu-\tau_{\rm eff}$ changes sign. Thus $B(t)\to+\infty$ sends the total race share to one and the load to $\bar e$; the host comparison of Proposition 3.4 then puts the host below its floor under (reach). Viability at every horizon therefore keeps $B$ from tending to $+\infty$. If the lim sup of the time average of $\tau_{\rm eff}$ were below $\nu$, there would be $\epsilon>0$ and $T_0$ with average at most $\nu-\epsilon$ beyond $T_0$, so $B(t)\ge\epsilon t-C\to\infty$. The converse fails, as the numerics show. This proof uses the exact signed-gap identity, not just the one-sided cumulative bound; S9 gives the counterexample outside that class. $\square$

*The margin, computed* [num: HO]. On the forgiving host ($\rho=0.05$, $h_V=0.08$, $V_0=1$, the race strategy holding 99% of activity, $e_{\max}=1$, selection at speed $s=1$): with $e_{\min}=0$, a margin of 0.08 from the start dips to 0.1397 before recovering to 1.000, and $\Delta_{\min}$ is 0.108, 0.165 and 0.355 for floors 0.2, 0.3 and 0.5; with $e_{\min}=0.1$ the long-run level is 0.840, a margin of 0.08 dips to 0.1136, and $\Delta_{\min}$ is 0.125, 0.192 and 0.414. Three independent integrations, a closed-form share with its own stopping rule, a replicator integrated jointly with the host, and the memo's own code, agree on these margins to 0.002. Below the margin this host recovers: with $\Delta=0.02$ it falls to 0.0025 and returns to 0.840, since on the logistic host any $\Delta>0$ recovers eventually. On the pointwise harsher threshold host $\rho_A(V/A-1)(1-V)$, with $\rho_A=\rho A/(1-A)$, the largest rate at which it is harsher than the forgiving host at every level (S3, *Hosts harsher than the forgiving one*), which degrades by itself below its threshold $V_u(e_{\min})$, with $e_{\min}=0.1$ and the same initial composition, the margin that keeps the transient above $V_u$ is 0.260, 0.306, 0.359, 0.423 and 0.505 for $A=0.05,0.10,0.15,0.20,0.25$, with $V_u$ equal to 0.254, 0.308, 0.364, 0.421 and 0.481; below it the collapse is permanent. For $A\ge0.10$, $V_u$ lies above a normative floor of 0.3, so the floor must be set no lower than the host's threshold for crossing it to be the first loss. *Part (3) is not sufficient.* With $\tau_{\rm eff}=\nu-1/(2\sqrt{t+1})$ the average tends to $\nu$, but $B(t)=\sqrt{t+1}-1$ grows without bound, and from a race share of 0.001 the host crosses its floor at $t=89$; with $\tau_{\rm eff}=\nu$ it does not. The averaged condition of R1 is necessary and not sufficient; the cumulative one is the condition the proof uses.

*What the computation is.* Earlier versions called the threshold-host margin "the first computable instance of the correctable kernel", and stated viability under a clause, "viable if, in addition, the host stays above its floor while the load falls", that assumed what was to be shown. Both are withdrawn: the clause was circular, and the margin condition replaces it on both hosts. The computation is an instance of the boundary of viable parameters for one fixed strategy of correction; the correctable kernel of Definition 8.1 takes a union over admissible strategies and an intersection over disturbances in the justified range, and is not computed here.

### Rival verification

**Proposition S10.2 (Rival verification) [thm].** *Under the premises of Proposition 4.10(2), a rival's verifier whose captured share starts below one and whose full inbound row sum stays below one by a fixed margin has capture tending to zero, counting conversion from the verified party, from its own side and from shared sources such as models, supply chains and professional paradigms. This certificate does not establish the independence of its present decisions. Rivalry keeps the verified party's contribution near zero; it does not by itself bound the rest of the row.*

*Proof.* Apply Proposition 4.10(2) to the verifier's full row, including its initial-state and transient conditions. Even a zero row permits a long captured transient: with $\zeta c=1$, $\varepsilon d=0.01$, initial normalized support 2 and $x_0=0.99$, the logit is $\ln99+200(1-e^{-0.01t})-t$; at $t=50$ the uncaptured share is about $3.5\times10^{-15}$, though capture eventually vanishes. A verifier taking 0.10 from the verified party and 0.95 from shared sources is instead captured once those sources are, whatever the rivalry. $\square$

*Scope.* Rival verifiers raise false alarms, can collude against third parties, and check compliance rather than judgment. A verifier biased toward finding violations is, for claims the other side cannot check, a sender with a material bias, and talk survives only while the effective bias is below $\frac14$ (Proposition 5.16); so its findings must rest on evidence the other side can check. An accusation under rivalry can trigger prevention (Proposition 8.5), so joint resolution precedes any response.

### Participation

**Proposition S10.3 (Participation) [thm].** Under complete information an arrangement is efficient for a pair of types iff its survival-weighted joint value exceeds the sum of their survival-weighted race payoffs, and an overwhelmingly strong actor takes part only for a share near its race payoff. Under private types an arrangement without verification must give each type an interim share independent of its report, and is feasible iff the joint value covers the sum of the types' best race payoffs; verification that reveals types removes the information rents, and the constraint relaxes as survival in the race falls below survival under the arrangement.

*Two actors have private types, strong or weak, with costs $c_s<c_w$, each strong with probability $p_s$ independently. Outside the arrangement types are revealed in a complete-information all-pay contest for $\Omega+L_b$: a strong actor against a weak one gets $U^{\rm race}=(\Omega+L_b)(1-c_s/c_w)-L_b$, and otherwise $-L_b$; survival in the race is $\Phi^{\rm race}$. The arrangement yields a common value net of costs $S_a$, divided in shares $\mathsf s_i$, with survival $\Phi^R$; an actor's utility is $\Phi_i(\mathsf v_i+\bar{\mathsf v})$, with $\mathsf v_i$ its payoff and $\bar{\mathsf v}$ a value it keeps either way. (1) Under complete information and transferable utility, a pair gains iff $\Phi^R(S_a+2\bar{\mathsf v})\ge\sum_i\Phi^{\rm race}_i(U^{\rm race}_i+\bar{\mathsf v})$. (2) With private independent types and participation decided before types are revealed, Bayesian incentive compatibility makes each type's interim expected share independent of its report, budget balance makes the interim shares constants summing to one, and the arrangement is feasible without verification iff $\Phi^R(S_a+2\bar{\mathsf v})\ge\sum_i\sup_{c}E\big[\Phi^{\rm race}(c,t_j)(U^{\rm race}_i+\bar{\mathsf v})\mid c\big]$; ex-post shares may still vary with reports. (3) Verification revealing types removes the information rents; it is distinct from the timely detection of defection. (4) The information condition relaxes as $\Phi^{\rm race}/\Phi^R$ falls. (5) Under complete information an overwhelmingly strong actor takes part only for a share near its race payoff; raising the joint value, lowering the value of winning, compressing survival or changing the asymmetry of costs can still make equal shares feasible.*

*Scope.* Rare strong types expect much from the race: mutual optimism, a failure of bargaining under private information (Fearon 1995). Which mechanism is second-best depends on the mechanisms allowed and on commitment, and is not solved here. Participation benefits, voting rights and protection against domination are three different quantities in this model: (1) and (2) price the first, say nothing about the second, and the third is a constraint fixed before the shares are set (Section 10.3).

*Proof.* (1) is the comparison of joint survival-weighted values with transfers. (2) Interim incentive compatibility with independent types equalizes each type's interim expected share across reports; budget balance then fixes the interim shares as constants summing to one, and participation of every type needs the joint value to cover each type's best expected race payoff. (3) Revealed types allow shares conditioned on types, removing the rents. (4) and (5) follow from the form of the inequalities. $\square$

*Numerics* [num: legacy v4.5, K7; legacy v5, K7]. With $c_s/c_w=0.1$, $\Omega=1$, $L_b=0$, $S_a=1$ and every $\Phi=1$, the sum of suprema is 0.36, 0.90, 1.44 and 1.71 at $p_s=0.8,0.5,0.2,0.05$: feasible without verification in the first two cases and not in the last two, although cooperation is efficient for every pair; with $L_b=0.5$ the sums are $-0.46$, 0.35, 1.16 and 1.56. With $\Phi^R=0.95$, $\bar{\mathsf v}=1$ and $p_s=0.2$, equal shares become feasible once $\Phi^{\rm race}/\Phi^R\le0.872$. Under complete information with $L_b=0.5$ and $S_a=1$, the strong type's minimum share is 0.925 at $c_s/c_w=0.05$ and 0.700 at 0.2 (`../v4/checks_v45.py`, K7). The interim shares of (2) are checked in `../v5/checks_v5.py`, K7: ex-post shares that differ by report, with an interim expected share of 0.500 for each type. V6.1 attributed all of these numbers to the v5 block; the feasibility sums, the survival compression and the minimum shares are in the v4.5 block. Both are legacy blocks of `checks_v72.py --legacy`.

## S11. The transition

### What relaxes the trust constraints

Section 11.1 states the three places where trust enters a transition and what relaxes each; this is the result in full.

**Corollary S11.1 (What relaxes the trust constraints) [thm].** The three trust constraints relax under the respective payoff changes below; a common intervention relaxes all three only where it makes all three changes. Verification relaxes the first only on what it observes; a sanction relaxes the first and the third only while it can still be imposed. *In the notation of Proposition 8.6, Proposition 5.16 and Proposition S8.5, with $g_C,g_D>0$:*
1. *With $P,S,F,\upsilon$ fixed, raising $R$ while holding $T$ fixed, or lowering $T$ while holding $R$ fixed, raises $g_C$ and lowers the belief threshold. Where $T-P+F>0$ and $R>P-F$, either change also lowers the timely detection that cooperation needs, $(T-R)/(T-P+F)$ (Theorem 8.7(1)). Holding the other parameters of talk fixed, a larger $\theta$ lowers the effective bias $b_0(n-1-\theta)/(n-1+\theta)$. Reducing a positive relative premium in $V^{\rm exit}-V^{\rm stay}$, with its absolute part fixed, lowers the net gain from exit.*
2. *Where $T-P+F>0$, a larger probability $\upsilon$ of timely detection raises $g_C$ and, where $P>S$, lowers $g_D$, so it lowers the belief threshold; it carries no information about what it does not observe.*
3. *A larger sanction $F$ raises $g_C$, and it deters an irreversible, public exit while $\upsilon F\ge V^{\rm exit}-V^{\rm stay}$. It cannot be imposed on a defector whose position has become decisive, so against such a deviation it acts only through the bound $\bar p_d$ on the chance of breakout (Proposition 5.4(2)).*
4. *Lowering the vulnerability of restraint, $P-S$, lowers $g_D$ and with it the belief threshold.*

*Proof.* Write, as in Proposition 8.6, $g_C=R-\upsilon(P-F)-(1-\upsilon)T$ and $g_D=(1-\upsilon)(P-S)$. Where both are positive, the belief threshold $\varpi^\ast=g_D/(g_C+g_D)$ falls as $g_C$ rises and rises with $g_D$.

*(1)* $\partial g_C/\partial R=1$ and $\partial g_C/\partial T=-(1-\upsilon)\le0$, so raising $R$ or lowering $T$ lowers $\varpi^\ast$. The timely detection that cooperation needs, $u^\ast=(T-R)/(T-P+F)$ from Proposition 8.6(2), has $\partial u^\ast/\partial R=-1/(T-P+F)<0$ and $\partial u^\ast/\partial T=(R-P+F)/(T-P+F)^2$, positive iff $R>P-F$. The effective bias of Proposition 5.16(1), $b_0(n-1-\theta)/(n-1+\theta)$, has derivative $-2(n-1)b_0/(n-1+\theta)^2<0$ in $\theta$. The net gain from exit is $V^{\rm exit}-V^{\rm stay}$ (Proposition S8.5(1)), and an agreement that preserves relative positions preserves its relative term (Proposition S8.5(3)), so reducing its positive contribution to the exit premium, with the absolute part fixed, lowers the gain. A fall in $T-R$ alone is insufficient for the belief comparison: at $\upsilon=0.5$, $P=F=0$, $S=-1$, moving $(R,T)$ from $(2,3)$ to $(1,1.2)$ reduces $T-R$ but raises the belief threshold from $0.5$ to $5/9$. With both payoffs moving, $g_C$ rises exactly when $\Delta R-(1-\upsilon)\Delta T>0$ under these fixed primitives.

*(2)* $\partial g_C/\partial\upsilon=T-P+F>0$ under the premise, and $\partial g_D/\partial\upsilon=-(P-S)<0$ where $P>S$. Verification reaches only what it observes (Theorem 8.7, Scope): messages about the rest remain cheap talk, credible only where the effective bias is below $\frac14$ (Proposition 5.16).

*(3)* $\partial g_C/\partial F=\upsilon>0$. An irreversible, public exit is deterred while $\upsilon F\ge V^{\rm exit}-V^{\rm stay}$, and a reversible covert preparation while $\upsilon F\ge(1-\upsilon)(V^{\rm exit}-V^{\rm stay})$ (Proposition S8.5(1)). A defector whose position has become decisive is beyond punishment, so against a deviation with $D_i>v_i$ the sanction acts only through $v_i$, and restraint holds only while $p_d\le\bar p_{d,i}$ (Proposition 5.4(2)).

*(4)* $\partial g_D/\partial(P-S)=1-\upsilon\ge0$. $\square$

*The link to the levers* [thm∣prem]. Sharing a fraction $\beta$ of the contested stakes scales them by $1-\beta$ (Proposition 5.10), which lowers what racing against a restrainer gains over mutual restraint; provision beneath the game lowers the won share of capacity and with it $\Gamma_\delta$ (Proposition 5.17; Proposition 2.3); and a lower $\Gamma_\delta$ lowers the price of standing and widens the working objective (Proposition 3.9). A common frontier raises what restraint earns against a defector, $S$, by pooling the restrainers' effort, and so lowers $P-S$; in the contest of Proposition 10.1 it turns all cooperating into a strict equilibrium beside the race [num]. The stage payoffs $T,R,P,S$ of Proposition 8.6 are reduced forms of those contests, so the link holds where they are read from them.

### Proof of Proposition 11.1

The receiver restrains with probability $\rho_m$ after message $m\in\{\mathsf E,\mathsf N\}$, where $\mathsf N$ is not taking the step: $\rho_m=1$ if its posterior that the sender is $\mathsf S$ exceeds $\varpi^\ast$, $\rho_m=0$ if it falls short, and any $\rho_m$ at equality; after a message no type sends, any belief, and so any $\rho_m$, is admissible. Type $\mathsf t$ receives $\rho_{\mathsf E}\Pi_{\mathsf t}-c_{\mathsf t}$ if it takes the step and $\rho_{\mathsf N}\Pi_{\mathsf t}$ if not. The step is open to inspection: that is what makes it verifiable and gives $c_{\mathsf R}$ its size.

*(1)* In the candidate the posteriors are 1 after the step and 0 without it, so $\rho_{\mathsf E}=1$ and $\rho_{\mathsf N}=0$, since $\varpi^\ast\in(0,1)$. $\mathsf S$ takes the step iff $\Pi_{\mathsf S}-c_{\mathsf S}\ge0$, and $\mathsf R$ refrains iff $\Pi_{\mathsf R}-c_{\mathsf R}\le0$.

*(2)* If $c_{\mathsf R}<\Pi_{\mathsf R}$, $\mathsf R$ gains $\Pi_{\mathsf R}-c_{\mathsf R}>0$ by imitating the candidate of (1), which is therefore not an equilibrium. Let $0\le c_{\mathsf R}<\Pi_{\mathsf R}$ and $1-\mu<\varpi^\ast$, and take any equilibrium. The posteriors on the path average to the prior $1-\mu<\varpi^\ast$, so at most one message on the path has a posterior of $\varpi^\ast$ or more. If $\rho_{\mathsf N}>0$, then $\mathsf N$ is either off the path, so that $\mathsf E$ is the only message on it, its posterior is $1-\mu$ and $\rho_{\mathsf E}=0$; or on it with a posterior of at least $\varpi^\ast$, so that the posterior after $\mathsf E$, if $\mathsf E$ is sent, is below it and again $\rho_{\mathsf E}=0$, while if $\mathsf E$ is not sent the posterior after $\mathsf N$ is $1-\mu$, contradicting $\rho_{\mathsf N}>0$. If $\rho_{\mathsf N}=0$ and $\rho_{\mathsf E}>c_{\mathsf R}/\Pi_{\mathsf R}$, $\mathsf R$ takes the step with certainty, and whatever $\mathsf S$ does the posterior after the step is $(1-\mu)\sigma_{\mathsf S}/\big((1-\mu)\sigma_{\mathsf S}+\mu\big)\le1-\mu<\varpi^\ast$, so $\rho_{\mathsf E}=0$, a contradiction. Hence $\rho_{\mathsf E}\le c_{\mathsf R}/\Pi_{\mathsf R}$ in every equilibrium, after the step whether or not it is on the path. The bound is attained in the equilibrium in which $\mathsf S$ takes the step, $\mathsf R$ takes it with probability $\sigma_{\mathsf R}=(1-\mu)(1-\varpi^\ast)/(\mu\varpi^\ast)\in(0,1)$, which holds the posterior after the step at $\varpi^\ast$, and the receiver restrains with probability $c_{\mathsf R}/\Pi_{\mathsf R}$ after the step and not otherwise; $\mathsf R$ is then indifferent, and $\mathsf S$ takes the step iff $c_{\mathsf S}\le\Pi_{\mathsf S}c_{\mathsf R}/\Pi_{\mathsf R}$.

*(3)* follows from (2) as $c_{\mathsf R}\to0$. $\square$

V6.1 stated (2) as "the receiver restrains after the step only if $1-\mu\ge\varpi^\ast$". That holds among pure-strategy equilibria, where $\mathsf R$ either imitates always or never; the equilibrium that attains the bound above has $\mathsf R$ imitating with a probability below one, and restraint after the step with positive probability even where prior trust falls short.

*Refinement.* Let $1-\mu<\varpi^\ast$, $c_{\mathsf S}<\Pi_{\mathsf S}$ and $c_{\mathsf R}>\Pi_{\mathsf R}$. The step is strictly dominated for $\mathsf R$, whose payoff from it is at most $\Pi_{\mathsf R}-c_{\mathsf R}<0$. In any equilibrium in which $\mathsf S$ does not take the step, the posterior without it is $1-\mu<\varpi^\ast$, so $\rho_{\mathsf N}=0$ and $\mathsf S$ receives 0, while the step could give it up to $\Pi_{\mathsf S}-c_{\mathsf S}>0$. The intuitive criterion (Cho and Kreps 1987) then puts all weight after the step on $\mathsf S$, the receiver restrains after it, and $\mathsf S$ deviates; and no partially separating equilibrium exists, since $\mathsf R$ never takes a dominated step and $\mathsf S$, once the step earns restraint, strictly prefers it. The separating equilibrium is the only one that survives. Where prior trust is already sufficient, $1-\mu\ge\varpi^\ast$, an equilibrium without the step and with restraint exists beside it, and the step is not needed.

*The signal and its imitation.* This is the costly signal of a security-seeking type (Kydd 2000). Where the lock is certain, $c_{\mathsf S}$ can be zero or negative (Proposition 6.4(1)); where $c_{\mathsf S}\le0$, the survival-first type takes the step whatever the receiver does, and the signal's work is done entirely by what the step costs a relative type. What the selection account adds is the reason the step is cheap to imitate unless it is inspected: a relative type that declares the removal and keeps the delegation it does not disclose pays nothing for the declaration, so $c_{\mathsf R}\to0$ and (3) applies. Inspection of doctrine and exercises, and durability that makes reversal costly, are what make $c_{\mathsf R}$ a cost at all; the difference between the two types' costs is the difference a signal needs.

*Numerics* [num: TR]. A search over strategy profiles, which does not use the conditions it tests, agrees with (1) in 300 random games, finds no equilibrium above the bound of (2) in 200, and finds the bounding equilibrium in every one of the 135 in which $c_{\mathsf S}\le\Pi_{\mathsf S}c_{\mathsf R}/\Pi_{\mathsf R}$. With $c_{\mathsf S}=-0.05$, $c_{\mathsf R}=0.30$, $\Pi_{\mathsf S}=0.40$ and $\Pi_{\mathsf R}=0.20$ the step separates. If it can be reversed cheaply, $c_{\mathsf R}=0.05$, it does not: with $\mu=0.5$ and $\varpi^\ast=0.6$, $\mathsf R$ imitates with probability 0.667, and the step buys restraint with probability 0.25 at most.

### A pause is valued by what changes during it

**Proposition S11.2 (A pause is valued by what changes during it) [thm].** A pause that changes nothing buys time, not survival. *Let $h_t$ be the residual hazard of the irreversible event in the $t$-th active round, and let a pause add no hazard of its own. (1) If the same process resumes after the pause, so that the $t$-th active round has the same $h_t$, survival over any number of active rounds is unchanged, and so is its limit, which is zero where the $h_t$ are not summable (Lemma 5.11). (2) A pause after which the residual sequence is summable, where before it was not, keeps survival bounded away from zero, provided survival to the pause is positive and every hazard after it is below one, uniformly over histories. (3) In the field model, a pause that freezes shares while ordinary rule support keeps accumulating completes capture from every frozen share above $1/G$ (Proposition 4.12).*

*Proof.* *(1)* Let $h_t$ be the conditional probability of the irreversible event in the $t$-th active round, given survival to it, on each surviving history. A pause that adds no hazard of its own changes the calendar date of each active round and nothing else, so survival over the first $N$ active rounds, $\prod_{t\le N}(1-h_t)$ on each history, is the same with or without it, and so is the limit. By Lemma 5.11, it is zero where the $h_t$ are at least a non-summable sequence. *(2)* Where after the pause $h_t\le\bar h_t<1$ with $\sum_t\bar h_t<\infty$, survival is at least the product of the rounds before the change and $\prod_t(1-\bar h_t)>0$. *(3)* is Proposition 4.12 with ordinary rule-making, $b_p=b$. $\square$

Resuming unchanged after a pause repeats the process that made the pause necessary; that is (1), and it is why Section 11.3 defines readiness by what has changed rather than by the time elapsed.

*Example* [num: TR]. With residual hazard 0.01 a round for 20 rounds and $0.01/t^{1.5}$ in the $t$-th round after a change, survival to round 10,000 is 0.80, and its limit is 0.80, since $\sum_t0.01\,t^{-1.5}=0.01\,\zeta(3/2)\approx0.026$; with the hazard left at 0.01 a round it is $2\times10^{-44}$.

### The seven dimensions of readiness

Each dimension of Definition 11.2 asks a question with observable indicators and rests on a slow variable, whose time constant is the least residence time over which the indicator must have stayed past its threshold. The questions are functional: broad E1 and changes in what winning confers remain the preferred direction, but unchanged private preferences or relative positions do not disqualify an arrangement that demonstrably meets every applicable requirement by durable enforcement. Q1, Q6, Q4 and Q7 rest on the slow variables of Tracks 1, 2, 3 and 4 of Section 11.4.

| Dimension | Question | Slow variable; the residence time is at least |
|---|---|---|
| Q1 Technical reliability | Has the residual hazard of the relevant classes fallen, by evidence independent of the developer and valid against evaluation awareness? | Residual risk per class; the class's manifestation window $\tau_e$ (Proposition 5.12) |
| Q2 Shared objectives | Do effective governing purposes and binding rules protect the shared conditions, whether through changed objectives or durable constraints on private ones? | Governing mandates, the self-models that positions select and the constraints that bind them; a turnover of those positions |
| Q3 Strategic incentives | Are all relevant deviations deterred under the applicable enforcement condition, by reduced gains, credible constraints or both? | The won share $\omega$, deviation gains, breakout probabilities and credible response values ((14)); a period of changing stakes long enough to test the claimed bounds. Lowering the value of winning is the preferred route, not the only one |
| Q4 Effective correction | Can evaluators obtain evidence, keep independent funding and execute findings? | Rule support in each corrector domain; its dwell (Proposition S11.4), not the return of shares to their former level |
| Q5 Deployment constraints | Is the tempo of capability and delegation bound to correction capacity? | Coverage against hazardous variety; a generation of capability, across which catch rates of planted defects at fixed human hours stay level |
| Q6 Retained human agency | Can people still take over delegated functions? | Human capacity $\mathcal M$; its recovery time after delegation (Lemma 6.3) |
| Q7 Custody and verifiability | Is decisive-class capability under effective common custody or equally effective verified containment, with a registered disposition and independent executor if readiness or participation fails? | The capability held in check, end-to-end detection and effective response against breakout (Remark 5.5), and the demonstrated pre- or post-construction disposition; a period of relevant capability change, plus transfer and resistance drills (X10). Knowledge and reconstruction overhang remain in C4 |

### The decision rule of readiness

**Remark S11.3 (Readiness under noise and conjunction) [thm].** A reading fixed in advance biases a conjunction of seven dimensions toward "not ready", more so as noise grows; a sequential test turns noise into delay. *(1) If each of seven dimensions, when met, is declared met with probability $1-\mathsf b_q$, independently, a ready state is declared ready with probability $\prod_q(1-\mathsf b_q)$, which is $0.95^7\approx0.70$ at $\mathsf b_q=0.05$. (2) Let each dimension be decided by a sequential probability-ratio test between a registered level below its threshold and one above it, on independent, identically distributed readings whose likelihood ratio is computed from their distribution and is not degenerate, with boundaries set for error rates $\mathsf a_q$ (declaring it met when it is not) and $\mathsf b_q$ (declaring it unmet when it is met). The test ends with probability one, and at the two registered levels its error rates are at most $\mathsf a_q/(1-\mathsf b_q)$ and $\mathsf b_q/(1-\mathsf a_q)$, at every level of noise that the test knows (Wald 1945); where the readings' family has a monotone likelihood ratio, as Gaussian readings of known variance do, the rate of declaring the dimension met is no larger at any level below the lower one, so registering the lower level at the threshold controls false release everywhere below it. Between the two levels lies an indifference zone in which neither rate is controlled: with registered levels on either side of the threshold, a level just below it is declared met about half the time. Readings that are independent but whose informativeness decays can leave the test undecided for ever (S14) [num: CX]. Over seven dimensions a ready state whose met dimensions stand at or above their upper registered levels is held back with probability at most $\sum_q\mathsf b_q/(1-\mathsf a_q)$.*

*Proof.* (1) is a product of independent events. (2) is Wald's termination theorem for identically distributed, non-degenerate readings, the monotonicity of the operating characteristic under a monotone likelihood ratio, and his bounds for the boundaries $\ln\big((1-\mathsf b_q)/\mathsf a_q\big)$ and $\ln\big(\mathsf b_q/(1-\mathsf a_q)\big)$ on the log-likelihood ratio, with a union bound over the seven. $\square$

*Numerics* [num: TR]. A fixed reading of 16 observations, designed for a 1% rate of false "met" and a 5% rate of missed "met" at unit noise, and holding its 1% rate at any noise, passes a met dimension with probability 0.95 at unit noise and 0.37 at twice it; seven met dimensions then pass together with probability 0.71 and 0.001. Sequential tests with $\mathsf a_q=0.01$ and $\mathsf b_q=0.05/7$, on Gaussian readings at levels 0 and 1, keep both observed error rates below their bounds at noise 1, 2 and 4, while the mean number of readings needed to pass a met dimension rises from 10.5 to 38.7 and 148.6, about as the variance. Over seven such dimensions a ready state is held back with probability at most $0.05/0.99\approx0.0505$. The bounds need the registered noise model and the levels: a met dimension whose true level is 0.75, past the threshold but inside the indifference zone, is held back with probability about 0.06 at unit noise against the bound of 0.0072, and a test that assumes unit noise when the noise is twice that declares an unmet dimension met with probability about 0.2 against the bound of 0.0101 [num: VF].

The residence condition of Definition 11.2 sets the window over which the readings are taken: no test may end before the residence time has passed, so a dimension cannot be declared met before its slow variable could have crossed its threshold. The rates are registered asymmetrically because the errors are: declaring a dimension met too early is the error the protection standard weighs, since resuming cannot be undone; holding it back too long is bounded, since a pause held past its evidence spends coverage (Remark S9.1). A dimension once met is re-opened by a test of the same kind, so that a single low reading neither re-opens it nor is ignored.

### Dwell, depth and a recruitable lineage

Take one domain of the field model (10) with its own conversion $b$ and conversion gain $G=ab/(cd)>1$, and write it in the logit of the share, $u=\ln\big(x/(1-x)\big)$, and rule support in units of break-even, $\tilde z=az/c$: then $\dot u=\zeta c(\tilde z-1)$ and $\dot{\tilde z}=\varepsilon d(Gx-\tilde z)$. Capture is the state $x\to1$, $\tilde z=G$; absence of capture, $(0,0)$, is locally stable, the interior saddle is $(1/G,1)$, and the two basins are divided by the saddle's stable manifold, which meets $\tilde z=0$ at the share $x_\sigma$ of Proposition 4.12. A reform that insulates rule-making sets the conversion to zero for a time $T$ and then restores it; the *dwell boundary* $T_{\min}$ is the infimum of holding times after which the released state converges to absence of capture. From the capture basin, a successful reform is held strictly beyond this boundary. "Rule-making ten times slower than adoption" is $\varepsilon d=0.1\,\zeta c$.

**Proposition S11.4 (Dwell, depth and a recruitable lineage) [thm; num: DW].** A reform that insulates rule-making has to be held past the return of shares to their former level, for longer the deeper the capture, and without bound as capture ages unless a lineage of the alternative persists that can be recruited when the rule changes. *Start an insulating reform from rule support at its captured level, $\tilde z=G$, with share $x_0$.*
1. *(Two phases.) Under insulation $\tilde z=Ge^{-\varepsilon dt}$; the share keeps rising for $\ln G/(\varepsilon d)$, and its logit is back at $u_0$ when $G(1-e^{-s})=s$, $s=\varepsilon dt$. After release the state converges to absence of capture iff it lies below the saddle's stable manifold. If $x_0>x_\sigma$, the dwell exceeds the time at which the share is back at its starting level* [thm].
2. *(Values.) From $x_0=0.99$, $\varepsilon dT_{\min}$ is 1.89, 5.32 and 10.45 for $G=2$, 5 and 10 when rule-making is ten times slower than adoption, and 5.86, 10.34 and 16.18 when the two are equally fast; the share is back at its starting level at $s=1.59$, 4.97 and 10.0* [num: DW].
3. *(Depth.) $T_{\min}$ is nondecreasing in the depth $u_0$ and at least $(u_0-\operatorname{logit}x_\sigma)/(\zeta c)$, so it has no bound as depth grows. At the captured state $\dot u=\zeta c(G-1)$, so the depth grows linearly with the age of capture* [thm]. *Each further unit of depth adds about $\varepsilon d/(\zeta c)$ to $\varepsilon dT_{\min}$* [num: DW].
4. *(A recruitable lineage.) If a lineage of the alternative persists inside selection at a share of at least $\phi_{\rm alt}$, so that the practice's share never exceeds $1-\phi_{\rm alt}$, and grows from it when the rule changes, the depth never exceeds $\ln\big((1-\phi_{\rm alt})/\phi_{\rm alt}\big)$, and $T_{\min}$ is at most its value from that depth, whatever the age of capture* [thm]. *With $G=10$ and rule-making ten times slower, a floor of 0.1% cuts $T_{\min}$ from 10.45 to 3.81; a share of the alternative protected outside selection, which does not recruit, leaves it at 10.44* [num: DW].

*Proof.* (1) Under insulation $\dot{\tilde z}=-\varepsilon d\tilde z$, so $\tilde z=Ge^{-\varepsilon dt}$ and $u(t)=u_0+\zeta c\big[G(1-e^{-\varepsilon dt})/(\varepsilon d)-t\big]$, which rises while $\tilde z>1$ and is back at $u_0$ when $G(1-e^{-s})=s$. After release the function of the proof of Proposition 4.12 is nondecreasing along solutions, so every solution converges to an equilibrium and the saddle's stable manifold separates the basins. The field is cooperative (Lemma S4.3), so the basin of absence is a down-set in $(u,\tilde z)$; it contains $(u,0)$ only for $u<\operatorname{logit}x_\sigma$, and so it contains no state with $u>\operatorname{logit}x_\sigma$. When the share is back at $x_0>x_\sigma$, and at every earlier time, the state is therefore outside the basin of absence. (3) The insulated equation for $u$ does not depend on $u$, and $\tilde z(t)$ does not depend on $u_0$, so a start deeper by $\Delta u$ stays deeper by $\Delta u$ at every $t$ with the same $\tilde z(t)$; since the basin of absence is a down-set, a $T$ that suffices from the deeper start suffices from the shallower one. The released state is in the basin only if $u(T)<\operatorname{logit}x_\sigma$, and $u(T)\ge u_0-\zeta cT$. At the captured state $\tilde z=G$, so $\dot u=\zeta c(G-1)$. (4) With the floor, $u\le\ln\big((1-\phi_{\rm alt})/\phi_{\rm alt}\big)$ throughout, and the insulated path from any start is bounded above, at every $t$, by the capped path from that depth; by the down-set property the bound on $T_{\min}$ follows. $\square$ The increments of (3) and the values of (2) and (4) are numerical: once rule support has decayed the logit falls at a rate approaching $\zeta c$, so each further unit of depth costs about $1/(\zeta c)$ of time.

*Numerics* (DW: forward integration in logit coordinates, and independently a backward-integrated separatrix with the analytic insulated path, agreeing to three decimals in six of six cases). Depth without a floor, with $G=2$ and equal speeds: from $x_0=1-10^{-1}$, $1-10^{-2}$, $1-10^{-3}$, $1-10^{-6}$ and $1-10^{-12}$ the dwell is 3.45, 5.86, 8.17, 15.08 and 28.90; with $G=10$ it runs from 13.78 to 39.22; with rule-making ten times slower the increments are a tenth as large (1.59 to 4.34 at $G=2$; 10.21 to 12.76 at $G=10$). A floor on the alternative, with $G=10$ and rule-making ten times slower: 3.52 at $\phi_{\rm alt}=0.01$, 3.81 at 0.001, 4.60 at $10^{-6}$ and 6.04 at $10^{-12}$, against 10.45 without; at $G=5$, 2.99 at 0.001; with equal speeds and $G=10$, 9.48 and 11.79 at the first two floors, against 16.18. Re-seeding the alternative at the rate $\phi_{\rm alt}\zeta(G-1)$ also bounds the dwell (3.28 at $G=10$, rule-making ten times slower, $\phi_{\rm alt}=0.001$; 4.12 at $10^{-6}$), but re-seeding is symmetric and re-seeds the practice wherever it has been removed. A share protected outside selection, $b\to b(1-\phi_{\rm alt})$ with $\phi_{\rm alt}=0.001$, leaves the dwell at 10.44. *Depth reached without a floor.* Under insulation the logit peaks at $u_0+(\zeta c/\varepsilon d)(G-1-\ln G)$: from $x_0=0.99$ with $G=10$ it is 71.6 with rule-making ten times slower, an alternative's share of $8\times10^{-32}$, and 11.3 with equal speeds, a share of $1.2\times10^{-5}$ [num: VF]. In any finite population an alternative at the first share is extinct, and the dwell without a floor is then unbounded: the values without a floor at slow rule-making (10.45, and the depth series) show the model's arithmetic, and the floored values are the meaningful magnitudes.

*Scope.* $T_{\min}$ is an infimum, not an attained minimum: a release exactly on the stable manifold approaches the saddle, so a reform is held strictly beyond it, with a margin; at $G=2$, equal speeds, $x_0=0.99$, the boundary is $\varepsilon dt=5.859$, and releases 0.01 either side of it fall into opposite basins [num: CX]. A start at $x=1$ is invariant, and this control cannot restore it. One domain, quasi-static conversion and a fixed $G$. The dwell rises with $G$, so capacity that capture has built and that goes on converting, a sunk stock that decays slowly, keeps the effective gain, and the dwell, up. The starting share of 0.99 is a convention, which is why (3) matters. The field is cooperative, so these are lock-in times, not cycle times. With several domains the reform must also take down the core that sustains the domain (Proposition 4.11). Rule support is not observed directly, and near full share the depth cannot be read from the level. For real institutions the magnitudes are a conjecture (TESTS, Test D); the comparison the model supports, that a reform judged on share levels is withdrawn too early and that a floor under a recruitable alternative bounds the hold while a frozen order's hold grows with its age, is the claim of Section 11.4. This is the second job of plurality that Section 10.4 names: lineages kept inside selection and able to grow make reform possible in bounded time, as well as decorrelating errors. The share-freezing pause has its own length, given with Proposition 4.12 in S4: a frozen share accumulates no lock-in.

### The ratchet

**Remark S11.5 (Graduated steps reach only as a ratchet) [thm].** *In the field model, a step that moves a captured domain's share, rule support or own conversion without bringing an inbound gain below one from the domains that remain captured is re-absorbed once the domain is re-seeded (Proposition 4.11), and a step that brings it below one must be held strictly beyond the dwell boundary of Proposition S11.4 to enter the basin of absence; release at the boundary approaches the saddle, and earlier release returns to capture. A graduated sequence therefore crosses the separatrix only where some step crosses its own threshold and is held.* The trust the earlier steps build changes $\varpi$ in the game of Proposition 8.6; it changes the field only where it is spent on such a step. That is the part of graduated reciprocation (Osgood 1962) that survives capture: it answers a coordination failure, and it moves capture only where a step lands past a threshold and stays.

### Reachability as a capture basin

**Remark S11.6 (What a theorem of reachability would say) [def].** In the language of viability theory (Aubin 1991; Cardaliaguet, Quincampoix and Saint-Pierre 1999), a target is *reachable* from the present state if that state lies in the viable capture basin: the set from which some admissible control brings the system to the target while the state stays viable throughout; the states from which it is reached within a time $T$ form the capture basin of horizon $T$. For this paper the state is the field of Section 4 together with the host of Section 3, the admissible controls are the corrections the transition can apply (insulating rule-making, provision, sharing, custody), and the target is the configuration without capture, with the host above its floor. *Proved:* in the one-domain field model, from the initial states of Proposition S11.4 that lie in the capture basin with $x_0<1$, insulation enters the basin of absence iff it is held strictly longer than $T_{\min}$. That boundary is an infimum, not an attained minimum: release exactly on the separatrix approaches the saddle, and the absence equilibrium itself is reached only asymptotically. With a floor under a recruitable alternative as a second control, the boundary is bounded whatever the age of capture, and a holding time beyond it by a margin suffices (Proposition S11.4(4)). *Not proved:* the basin for several domains with all controls, with disturbances, and jointly with the host's floor. That is why Table 11.3 is a table of requirements with the support each has, and not a capture-basin proposition.

### The tracks and their time constants

Each track of Section 11.4 rests on a slow variable, and the order in which the tracks start follows their time constants.
- *Track 1, the shared account of hazards*, rests on shared understanding, which cannot run ahead of the evidence: for a class whose evidence arrives only after a manifestation window, no measurement shortens it (Proposition 5.12). Its instruments, evaluation suites and incident channels, are the fastest of the five to build, which is why agreement among independent measurements is the first indicator that can move.
- *Track 2, narrow commitments*, rests on human capacity $\mathcal M$, which decays at $\delta_h$ under delegation and recovers from it in $\varrho_h^{-1}\ln\big[(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M)/(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M_{\min})\big]$ (Lemma 6.3); kept in parallel it removes the lock at a cost in strength, and where correction rests on it, it also sets how fast correction must grow (Proposition 4.6).
- *Track 3, correction and shared services*, rests on rule support in each corrector domain, with the dwell of Proposition S11.4. If $1/(\varepsilon d)$ is an institutional turnover of five to ten years, an assumption and not an estimate, the dwells computed there run from about a decade to more than a century [num: DW, with the assumed turnover]. That is why this track starts first and cannot wait for the trust of Track 4.
- *Track 4, delegation, access, custody and decision rights*, rests on the capability held in check, whose growth raises the net gain from exit where the exit value outgrows staying (Proposition S8.5), against the clock $T_{\rm dec}$ to a decisive position (Remark S2.1); custody's installation and verification must take less than that time, so its preparation runs furthest ahead of need.
- *Track 5, broadening*, rests on the record of the arrangements under stress, which accrues only through cycles of stress and recovery, and on the self-models formed inside them, which change over generations, a time the field does not model [conj]. After a recovery, mobilization falls back and certificates can fail again (Remark 4.7), the regulatory cycle; openness of membership has to be built in from Track 1, since a bloc that closes early is hard to open later [conj].

### Table 11.3, row by row

*Existence* rests on Proposition 10.1, Proposition S10.3 and Theorem 10.4; *reachability* on Proposition 8.6 with Corollary S11.1, Proposition S8.1, Proposition 6.4 with $q_L=1$, Proposition 11.1 and Remark S11.5; *survival of the transient* on Theorem 10.4(1), Lemma 6.3, Theorem 5.13, Corollary 5.7 and the two clocks of Proposition 4.13; and *persistence* on Proposition S8.5, Proposition 4.10, Proposition S10.2, Proposition 4.11, Proposition S11.4 and Remark S9.1. The table claims no more than its rows. No result shows that the four can be met together, and graduated steps are shown to reach only as a ratchet (Remark S11.5); Remark S11.6 states what a proof of reachability would have to cover.

## S13. Interfaces between the models

The theory is built from models that are each solved on their own: a stock model of compounding (Section 2.2), a load model on one host (Section 3), a field model of correctors (Section 4), a repeated game with breakouts (Section 5.2), survival under coverage (Section 5.3), and the delegation and training games of Section 6. Where a sign or a threshold passes from one model to another, it passes through an *interface*: an assumption about how a quantity of one model moves a quantity of another. Each is listed here with its status. **Proved**: a result of this paper derives the join, under the conditions stated. **Numerical**: only computed models support it, with the block of S15 that computes them. **Open**: it is assumed, and the arrows that cross it are hypotheses. A cell of Table 7.1 marked † and an entry of the S7 matrix that names an interface cross one of the open joins. A cross-model bound is used only where its transfer assumptions are stated; Proposition S16.1 in S16 supplies one reduced composition of field, load and host dynamics. It does not close the institutional or hazard interfaces below.

### The loop and what it reaches

In the deterministic-share model, the private elasticity gives the local price calculation under the additional premises of Proposition 2.2(4). **Numerical, held for outside review:** finite-jump lottery premiums, their amplification at parity, the laggard's falling price, and a winner-take-all fixed point beside the smooth one (Remark S2.2) [num: MP]. Catch-up can reverse the win-share elasticity correction without reversing the total price correction. **Open:** which contests discourage or encourage catching up; whether the concentrated fixed point survives in the continuous-state game; a derivative-based objective reduction for the full strategic lottery | Section 2's claim box; the standing row of Table 7.1; the strategic objective join remains open |

| Funding and corrective production | Money, human effort, AI assistance, access and executive authority; the institutional rate $d$ and separately hazard coverage $U_t$ and response latency | Distinct production relations from resources to institutional dissipation and to effective coverage and response | **Open.** $d^\ast$ is a rate threshold, not a budget or a hazard-coverage requirement. Conversion audits, intervention evidence, catch rates and miss correlation constrain different outputs; none supplies the missing dollar-to-rate mapping by itself. Provisional financing must be tested against both obligations | X10 correction fund; C3a; the correction-rate/coverage interface |
| Correction policy and executable correction | A feasible policy in the correctable kernel, and the institutions that observe, authorize and execute it | Funding, appointments, access, infrastructure, human competence, authority, measurement and response time continue to permit timely action through learning, selection and succession | **Open.** S16 proves a reduced invariant region under a maintained feasible correction rate and liability form, not that institutions preserve those inputs. A negative local return to obstruction excludes neither a profitable distant strategy nor coordinated amendment. Test N investigates this implementation and preservation problem; barrier feasibility or a funding formula alone does not resolve it | Definition 8.1; Remark 10.5; Proposition S16.1; Sections 10 and 12; C4, Test N |
| Masking and alarms | Reports, by (13), and the decisions taken on them | Reports averaged and non-strategic (A5) | **Maintained**, with other rules treated in Remark 4.9: a bound on masking from a channel the field does not control; a disjunctive alarm; the dynamics of reports, which static masking does not hide and a filter with memory does [num: AC]. Masking by the evaluated model is a premise with an evidence row (EV6), which C12 measures | The talk and alignment-tax rows of Table 7.1; S7, P9 and P12 |

### Enforcement and survival

| Interface | Joins | Assumption | Status | Used in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Enforcement and verified agreement | The enforcement condition (14) and the verification threshold of Proposition 8.6 | The one-shot payoffs of Proposition 8.6 read as continuation values | **Proved:** where the stage games coincide and trust is one, the two thresholds are one number (Remark 5.5) [identity: JX]; they differ where the sanction has to be enforced by the actors it protects (S5). Closed | Section 8.3; Table 9.1, R4 |
| The scissors' blades together | The four arms of Proposition 5.17, each proved in its own model | Along a path on which the stakes outgrow provision, the arms move at once, through the won share $\omega$ | **Proved** arm by arm. **Numerical:** in two reduced models, with rivals not responding and the stakes arm off in their base runs, both blades move through $\omega$, the objective blade doing most of the closing, and neither moves where provision keeps pace (Remark S5.2) [num: JX]. **Open:** that the arms move together in the world (C0; Test E) | Section 5.5; Table 7.1 |
| Shared loss and irreversible hazard | The contest's shared loss (Section 3.4) and survival (Section 5.3) | (hazard–damage link): the hazard of the irreversible event proportional to shared damage | Premise; C1 | The race channel (Proposition 5.10) |
| Coverage and residual risk | The uncovered share $u_t$ and survival | (residual floor); for the bound to hold for every actor, (shared failure) | Premise; C1, the central crux. Under it a summable uncovered share is necessary, and sufficient under a residual bound below one (Theorem 5.13; Table 9.1, R5). Broad E1 changes the social arrangement but does not by definition establish these growth, coverage or residual-risk bounds | S7, P21; E3 on every branch including broad E1; E2 for the separate decisive-class trigger |
| Failed assurance and actual hazard | Masked-divergence admission and the residual floor | A bound on undetected divergence must imply a lower bound on irreversible harm, separately from what an evaluator fails to establish | **Open:** failed detection proves neither that harmful divergence persists nor that its hazards are nonsummable. The residual floor needs independent evidence. Capability limits under missing assurance are recommendations under the protection standard, not consequences of a proved survival limit | Section 6.4; C1, C9, C12; E2 and E3 |
| Lifecycle and fallback | A governance arrangement before or after construction, and the fallback hazards of Lemma 8.2 | Continuing custody or independently executed disposition, with actual residual capability, knowledge, copies and rebuilding capacity included on each history | **Open.** A preconstruction limit does not imply a low-overhang fallback after construction. Separately held functions, registered powers or a destruction plan do not establish effective execution or the lemma's hazard inequalities | X8, X10; D1, D4, D5; C4 |
| Shared loss and coupling | The shared loss $\ell$ and the coupling $\kappa$ | Distinct quantities, never counted twice | Maintained (Section 3.4) | Proposition 3.6 |

### AI

| Interface | Joins | Assumption | Status | Used in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| The outer training loop | Developers' relative payoff and the trained systems' contribution to it | Level 2 selects on relative payoff, with $(A_\tau)$; masking rises with the share of evaluation done by the evaluated lineage, which Proposition 4.8 takes as given | Premise. Where developers are selected otherwise, Proposition 6.7 does not apply; where an attributable liability exceeds the tax, $(A_\tau)$ fails and the safeguard spreads (Table 9.1, R8a); the tax is measured class by class (C9a–d) | S7, P9 and P23; the alignment-tax row of Table 7.1 |
| Delegation speed and detection | The speed gain $s$ of Proposition 6.2 and the detection clock $\upsilon$ of Remark 5.5 | Escalation at machine speed shortens the time in which detection and response can act | **Open:** cited mechanisms, no joint model (S6) [conj]. Keeping AI detection available under narrow E1 does not by itself preserve the end-to-end clock: authorization and effective response must also occur before decisiveness. Equality of that full clock is a separate condition to measure (C7) | The delegation row of Table 7.1, against (14); S7, P22 |
| Talk and the self-model | $\theta$ and the bias of talk (Proposition 5.16) | Uniform-quadratic payoffs and a common $\theta$ | **Proved**, and closed: one effective bias $b_0(n-1-\theta)/(n-1+\theta)$, whatever linear effects reach third parties (Proposition 5.16(1)); with third-party payoffs that are not linear the gap in units of the state is unchanged to the grid's resolution [num: TK]. That pairs who still talk act as blocs is a reading [conj] | The talk row of Table 7.1 |

### Levels, hosts and minds

| Interface | Joins | Assumption | Status | Used in |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Selection and strategy | Carrier selection and strategic actors | A result about selection transfers to strategic actors only where their objective is shown to be the selected one | Rule of Section 3.1. Two results carry the transfer: Proposition 3.6(3), by which finite-population selection favours $\theta=-1$, and Proposition 3.9, by which compounding sets the working objective | Part I throughout |
| The host | One host and the many shared conditions it stands for | The forgiving logistic host of (3) | **Proved:** crossing results hold a fortiori on any host that regenerates no faster at any level (S3). **Numerical:** viability results need a margin, computed on both hosts, and on a host that degrades by itself below a threshold a shortfall is permanent (Theorem 10.4(1)) [num: HO]. **Open:** vector hosts, in which one component can begin to collapse while the average reads as healthy (S3) | Proposition 3.4; Theorem 10.4; Table 9.1, R1 |
| Slow variables of mind | The selected self-model and the field model | A slow variable for the share of decision positions held by the winning model, with possible bistability and hysteresis | **Open:** not modelled. A toy in the research programme of TESTS, in which actors who act on their working objective also make the rules that set the next period's gain, is bistable (block ML, derived); it is not a model of minds | S7, the four loops; Section 2.3 |

*Reading.* The proved and closed joins are what let the parameter matrix of S7 be read across models at all; the open ones are where the paper's integration is a hypothesis and not a theorem, and they are why Table 7.1 is read row by row, each row in its own model, and why the claim that the thresholds move together is C0 [conj]. Three tasks would close major gaps. One model that derives the stage games of (14) from the stock dynamics would join compounding to enforcement without the stakes arm being assumed (*stocks and stage payoffs*). One that selects capacity in several coupled domains while the contest moves the shares would replace the last quasi-static step of Section 4 (*capacity and the field*). A third task is to derive how institutions preserve the access, resources, authority and time needed for correction as their participants change them. Proposition S16.1 partially composes capture, liability, load and host viability while maintaining those inputs; it makes their remaining status explicit rather than supplying them. Test N is a proposed test of that durability, and C1 still needs independent hazard evidence. The slowest join, the formation of minds, no model here contains.

## S14. Counterexamples that bound the claims

Each entry refutes a stronger version of a claim or marks where a claim stops; the result named states the weaker claim that survives. The last column names the block of S15 that computes the entry, part by part; *closed form* marks an entry whose arithmetic is in the proof cited, and *evidence* one that rests on an evidence row. Entries marked (O) are the boundary cases of the outside review of V6.1: every default run of the checks ends with their index and asserts that the eight it requires hold (S15).

### Compounding (Section 2)

| Counterexample | What it refutes or bounds | Where | Check |
|---|---|---|---|
| (O) Outside the domain, $\Gamma_\delta=9/7$ ($n=2$, $\kappa\Omega=1$, $r=2$, $d=0.2$, $\delta=0.9$): the printed multiplier is $-4.5$, while a relative perturbation is worth a positive amount at every size | That $\Gamma_\delta/(1-\Gamma_\delta)$ prices standing beyond $\Gamma_\delta<1$; there the price is infinite, a cusp, not negative | Proposition 2.2(3) | PD(a) |
| $\Gamma>1>\Gamma_\delta$ ($\delta=0.8$, $\Gamma_\delta=0.889$): equal standing is unstable, yet the price per unit converges to the formula's 8 | That instability of equal standing makes the price of standing infinite | Proposition 2.2(3) | LG(B); PD |
| (O) Faster decay of a ratio channel with diffusion ($r=1.8$, $\delta_f=0.1$): raising $d$ from 0.1 to 0.2 takes $(\Gamma,\Gamma_\delta)$ from (0.90, 0.58) to (1.20, 0.88) | That correction lowers the loop gain in every channel, and V6.1's single row for $d$ | Proposition S2.4(2) | CH(c); CH(a), (b) |
| Provision in a channel whose decisiveness rises faster than the stock ($h=X^2$): raising $k_0$ from 0.1 to 0.2 raises the gain from 1.75 to 2.25; in a linear level channel it stays at 1.125 | That provision lowers the gain in every channel; it does where decisiveness rises less than in proportion to the stock | Proposition S2.4(3) | CH(a) |
| A countervailing channel with delay: net gain 0.25, roots of modulus 1.0075, deviations oscillating with a period of 51.6 rounds and reaching 292 times a unit start by round 600 | That a net gain below one ensures stability, so that correction can be subtracted from $\Gamma$ | Remark S2.3 | CH(e) |
| One gain, two speeds: at $\Gamma=1.2$ a gap goes from 1% to 50% in 41 periods at a turnover of 0.5 and in 980 at 0.02; V6.1's factor $1+d_K(\Gamma-1)$ gives 1.067 where the exact factor is 1.100 | That the loop gain sets how fast leads open; it sets only whether they do | Remark S2.1 | TD(a)–(c) |
| Diffusion: at $\Gamma=1.5$, $d_K=\delta_f=0.05$, $r=3$, a 1% gap has not reached 50% after 10,000 periods, where the linearization predicts 80 | That $\Gamma>1$ makes a lead decisive in finite time | Remark S2.1 | TD(b) |
| Discouragement: at a structural gain of 0.45 the equilibrium gain is 1.11 | That $\Gamma<1$ keeps equal standing stable where rivals respond | Proposition 2.2(4); Remark S2.2(3) | MP(c) |
| Catch-up: with a flow cost of standing below the top ($L_{\rm sub}=100$), $r^{\rm eq}/r=0.993$ at $\Gamma=0.3$ | That strategic response always amplifies the gain | Remark S2.2(5) | MP(d), held for outside review |
| The Markov laggard: at a quarter share its price of standing is 57% and 25% of the structural value at $\Gamma=0.3$ and 0.6; at larger jumps 0.065 against 1.62 at parity, and $-0.039$ with a shared loss | That every actor prices standing as at parity; the laggard stops contesting standing, the exclusion of Proposition 2.3(2) seen from its side | Remark S2.2(6); Proposition 3.9 | MP(c), (g) |
| Multiplicity: at $\Gamma=0.2$, $d=0.05$, a winner-take-all fixed point (price 1.392, about 12.5 times the structural value) beside the smooth one (0.1169) | That the structural gain fixes the equilibrium; where both exist, the belief that a lead is decisive selects the equilibrium in which it is | Remark S2.2(4) | MP(e), held for outside review |
| (O) A cap at exactly the equal-standing level ($n=2$, $r=3$, $K^\ast=2.5$, no sharing, provision or diffusion): a laggard at 2.499 falls to $2.4\times10^{-32}$ | That a cap at $K^\ast$ holds standing; a strict cap below it does, in 400 of 400 settings | Proposition 2.3(2) | CE(e); CE(b) |
| (O) Diffusion alone ($n=2$, $r=2$, $d_K=0.2$, $\delta_f=0.6$, cap at $K^\ast$): the laggard's inflow stays positive, of order $10^{-4}$ at its minimum, and $(0.01,2.5)$ goes to $(2.5,2.5)$ | That recovery from every start needs sharing or provision; diffusion is an inflow not tied to rank | Proposition 2.3(2) | CE(f) |
| Diffusion as a source, not a guarantee: with $\delta_f=0.05$ alone, a laggard starting at 0.3 of a cap at $0.9K^\ast$ stalls at 0.16 of the cap, its inflow positive at zero (0.056) but dipping to $-0.013$ | That a positive inflow at zero capacity brings a laggard back from every start | Proposition 2.3(2) | CE(a) |

### Boundaries made explicit in V7

These are executable counterexamples to the stronger claims, not empirical estimates. Block RV, introduced in V7 and preserved in `checks_v72.py`, runs the three original independent scripts in `verification/` (S15).

| Counterexample or control | What it refutes or preserves | Where |
|---|---|---|
| At $n=2$, $\kappa=d=\delta=1/2$, $r=1$, no sharing, provision or diffusion, and $\Omega(S)=S^2$, the stationary reference $S=1$ has relative gain $1/3$ and common gain $2/3$, but every positive common perturbation has infinite discounted winnings | Constant reference coefficients and gains below one do not establish a derivative of the actual nonlinear value | Proposition 3.9; S3 |
| A fixed-opponent finite-jump lottery at $r=1.5$, $\Gamma=0.6$, $d=0.2$, $\delta=0.97$ gives premium 1.03111035 on 2,000 cells, versus local expression 1.08178439; the 1,000-cell result differs by less than $4\times10^{-6}$, and a linear-strength control agrees | Finite lottery jumps need not have the deterministic local price | Proposition 2.2; S3 |
| A zero inbound row with $x(0)=0.99$ and accumulated support $z(0)=2$ leaves an uncaptured share about $3.49\times10^{-15}$ at time 50 before eventual recovery | Eventual recovery does not establish present freedom from capture | Proposition S10.2 |
| Detection zero, uncovered share one, and hazards $h_t=2^{-t-2}$ leave limiting survival about 0.577576 | Failed assurance and uncovered exposure alone do not establish a residual risk floor | Remark 6.8; S6 |
| $F=1$, $p_{\rm det}(K)=e^{-K}$ and $c_k(K)=e^{-2K}$ retain a positive safeguarding advantage; at $K(t)=\log(t+2)$ its integral diverges | Falling detection and bounded sanctions do not defeat a tax that falls faster | Remark 6.8 |
| $v(\Omega)=1-e^{-\Omega}$ bounds effort at $3/16$ in the four-player unit-cost, unit-decisiveness contest, without making it zero | Bounded utility does not imply finite satiation or stopping | Proposition 5.15 |
| Detection in 0.5 time units with a response in 0.5 beats a decisive time of 2; replacing the response by one taking 2 misses it | Identical detection does not preserve the end-to-end response clock | Section 11.3; Remark 5.5 |
| Complete pooling sends stocks $(2,1)$ to $(1.5,1.5)$ | Pooling preserves strict order only below complete pooling | Proposition 10.2 |
| At detection $1/2$, changing $(R,T)$ from $(2,3)$ to $(1,1.2)$ shrinks the raw temptation gap but raises the trust threshold from $1/2$ to $5/9$ | A smaller unweighted gap alone does not relax trust | Corollary S11.1 |
| Releases just before and just after the critical dwell go to different basins | The critical dwell is a boundary infimum; robust recovery needs strict entry into the basin | Proposition S11.4; Remark S11.6 |

The mathematical checks also preserve the proportional-prize derivative on both sides of its threshold, and test the otherwise-alike premise against a correlated background. The transition checks exercise the unsafe-start qualification of the cited pacing model; they do not independently certify that paper's full equilibrium theorem.

Block RP of `checks_v72.py` runs `verification/readiness_checks.py`. The selection example has an analytical bound for all time, with independent host integrations at two step sizes; the other entries pair exact witnesses with direct dynamics, optimization or valuation checks as appropriate. These checks do not establish empirical prevalence or certify a complete institutional design.

| A protective trait with local benefit 0.2 and cost 0.1 rises from share 0.1 to 0.99959 by time 100, with no cognitive-understanding state. | Fixed $\delta=0.9$, $g=0.1$, $C=1$, $D=100$, $v=0$, and escape chance 0.001: normalized cooperation value 1 exceeds deviation value 0.2; the escape threshold is 0.009889. At escape chance 0.02 deviation pays | High decisive value alone does not rule out indefinitely repeated verified containment at fixed primitives, conditional on credible punishment and a maintained escape bound | Proposition 5.4; Corollary 5.7 |

## S15. Numerics

`checks_v72.py` preserves the inherited numerical suite and V7 block RV, V7.1 block RP, and adds V7.2 block RC. It uses the Python standard library. A default run exercises 45 blocks; its counted pass/fail results and timings are in `checks_v72.log`. The inherited V6.2 default run passed 213 assertions; V7 RV added four mathematical regression groups, six systems tests and eight transition tests. RP adds eight named readiness groups from `verification/readiness_checks.py`. RC adds four groups from `verification/correction_checks.py`. The final count treats each RV, RP and RC group as one gate, not each internal assertion. `python3 checks_v72.py XX YY` selects blocks; `--full` enlarges the heavy grids, and `--legacy` runs earlier suites separately.

The suite reproduces specified numerical claims and boundary cases. Fourteen inherited blocks also contain diagnostic comparisons that are printed without assertion gates; a successful exit does not certify every printed comparison or every proof. Earlier tagged numbers retain their identified legacy scripts. No run supplies empirical evidence about the world.

*Kinds.* An **independent** block decides the outcome by direct computation (value iteration, simulation, numerical best response, numerical Jacobian, search over strategy profiles), never by the inequality under test. An **identity** block evaluates an algebraic identity or a threshold formula against its own statement, and guards only against slips; a **valuation check** is an identity that values a fixed path of play. A **derived** block computes numbers from formulas that other blocks check. Where a block's parts differ in kind, its header marks the parts, and the text tags an identity part [identity: …], not [num: …].

### Earlier suites

Results carried unchanged from earlier versions keep the blocks that first checked them, run by `--legacy`; their successful runs are inherited from the V6.2 verification record dated 4 October 2026. V7.2's default run does not re-run them. A tag names a legacy block as "legacy v5, FS", never by its code alone, because two legacy codes repeat current ones with other content.

- `../v5/checks_v5.py`: FS, the finite sprint and prevention (Proposition 8.5); CMP, the two cruxes at common horizons (Section 8.4, S8); N13, the assurance condition among $n$ (Proposition 8.6); SR, survival ratios over a common horizon (Proposition S8.1(5)); K7, interim incentive compatibility (Proposition S10.3); HO, the two roots of Proposition S6.2(2), now also SM(15); and, unnamed, the ceilings of Proposition 5.2, Lemma 5.11 and the numbers of Theorem 5.13.
- `../v4/checks_v45.py`: N22, the common frontier with Nash-verified racer subgames (Proposition 10.1); K7, the mechanism-design form (Proposition S10.3); LD, a lead under diffusion (Remark S8.2); and, unnamed, the sprint and freeze of Proposition 5.14.
- `../v4/checks_v44.py`: SV, survival branches (Proposition S8.1, its numbers recomputed from the closed forms for V6.2); unnamed, the gradual shift of Proposition 5.9, used again in Proposition 7.2(3).
- `../v4/checks_v42.py`: unnamed, the bargaining protocols of Proposition 5.9(3) and the derivative action of Proposition 5.12.
